# Partial Escape Analysis (PEA) for Jeandle — Implementation Plan

## Context

Jeandle currently lacks any form of escape analysis, meaning all object allocations survive to code generation even when their uses could be scalar-replaced. Partial Escape Analysis (PEA) is a flow-sensitive optimization that tracks whether objects "escape" the current method on a per-path basis. Objects that never escape can be fully eliminated (scalar replacement); objects that escape only on some paths get their allocations deferred to the escape point (materialization), avoiding allocation on paths where the object stays local.

This plan is based on a detailed study of:
- Lukas Stadler's PhD thesis "Partial Escape Analysis and Scalar Replacement for Java" (2014)
- The Graal compiler's PEA implementation (`jdk.graal.compiler.virtual.phases.ea.*`)
- The Jeandle LLVM IR patterns generated by `jeandleAbstractInterpreter.cpp`

**Where Graal source and the paper conflict, the Graal source is the ground truth.**

This plan designs PEA as two separate LLVM passes — an **analysis** pass and a **transform** pass — consistent with LLVM's principle of separating analysis from transformation. PEA runs as the first optimization pass in the pipeline, before `JavaOperationLower(0)`, so all JavaOp calls are still identifiable as function calls.

**Out of scope**: Deoptimization handling, read elimination (can be added as a future extension), frame state management.

---

## Architecture Overview

```
┌──────────────────────────────────┐
│  PartialEscapeAnalysis            │  FunctionAnalysis
│  (read-only dataflow)             │
│                                   │
│  Forward dataflow over BBs in RPO │
│  Tracks VirtualObjects,           │
│  ObjectStates, global alias map   │
│  Records per-block Effects        │
│  Produces PEAResult               │
└──────────────┬───────────────────┘
               │ PEAResult
               ▼
┌──────────────────────────────────┐
│  PartialEscapeTransform           │  FunctionPass
│  (IR modification)                │
│                                   │
│  Consumes PEAResult:              │
│  Two-pass application:            │
│  Pass 1: Non-CFG-kill effects     │
│    - Insert materializations      │
│    - Create PHI nodes             │
│    - Replace loads/calls          │
│    - Replace virtual references   │
│  Pass 2: CFG-kill effects         │
│    - Remove eliminated stores     │
│    - Remove eliminated locks      │
│    - Remove eliminated allocs     │
│    - Simplify dead branches       │
│    - Dead code cleanup            │
└───────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Pipeline after integration:**
```
PEA Analysis + PEA Transform → JavaOperationLower(0) → InstSimplify → TypeCheckElimination → O3 → InsertGCBarriers → JavaOperationLower(1) → TLSPointerRewrite → RS4GC
```

---

## 1. Data Structures

This section specifies every shared data structure used by both the analysis and transform passes. The structures are designed so that:

- The analysis pass produces a `PEAResult` that is a *complete, declarative description* of every IR change to be made. No analysis state survives into the transform pass except via `PEAResult`.
- All transformations are recorded as *per-block, deferred Effects* (Section 1.6) — never applied immediately. This mirrors Graal's `GraphEffectList` / `EffectList` design (see `GraphEffectList.java:54`, `EffectList.java:43`).
- All address-space discipline is enforced uniformly: only LLVM pointers in `addrspace(1)` (Jeandle's `JavaHeapAddrSpace`, see `Metadata.h:32`) are candidates for virtualization.
- Memory ownership is explicit: `PEAResult` owns every `VirtualObject` (via `std::unique_ptr`); the `VirtualObjects` vector grows monotonically so raw `VirtualObject*` taken at any point remain valid for the lifetime of the analysis (see invariant V1 in Section 1.7).

**File layout** — all structures live in `llvm/include/llvm/IR/Jeandle/PartialEscape.h` (header-only data definitions), with non-trivial method bodies in `llvm/lib/IR/Jeandle/PartialEscape.cpp`.

### 1.1 ObjectID and VirtualObject

```cpp
// A dense, opaque identifier into PEAResult::VirtualObjects.
// Grows monotonically during analysis; never reused. See invariant V1.
using ObjectID = unsigned;
static constexpr ObjectID InvalidObjectID = ~0u;

class VirtualObject {
public:
  enum ClassKind : uint8_t { Instance, Array };

private:
  // Stable identifier. Assigned at construction by PEAResult::createVirtualObject.
  // The field is intentionally private and immutable to enforce invariant V2
  // (ObjectID never changes after creation).
  const ObjectID ID;

  // Kind of allocation: instance (jeandle.new_instance) or array
  // (jeandle.newarray).
  ClassKind Kind;

public:
  // ===== Allocation provenance =====
  // The call/invoke instruction that allocated this object. This is the
  // anchor for both materialization (insert allocation back at this point
  // if never escaped) and elimination (erase this call when fully virtual).
  //
  // The instruction is an InvokeInst for jeandle.new_instance /
  // jeandle.newarray (they carry deopt bundles and may throw OOM), but the
  // data structure stores CallBase* to be uniform.
  CallBase *AllocationCall = nullptr;

  // ===== Type / size =====
  // Klass pointer extracted via jeandle::extractKlassConstant from
  // AllocationCall->getArgOperand(0). Must be non-zero for virtualization.
  uintptr_t Klass = 0;

  // For Instance: the byte size argument (operand 1) of jeandle.new_instance,
  // required to be a ConstantInt. For Array: 0 (size depends on length).
  uint32_t SizeInBytes = 0;

  // For Array: the array length expression (operand 1) of jeandle.newarray.
  // MUST be a ConstantInt for virtualization. If non-constant, the
  // allocation is not a candidate (see Design note - constant array length).
  Value *ArrayLengthVal = nullptr;
  uint32_t ArrayLength = 0; // cached constant value of ArrayLengthVal

  // For Array: the LLVM element type derived from the element JavaKind.
  // E.g., int[] -> i32, Object[] -> ptr addrspace(1), byte[] -> i8.
  Type *ArrayElementType = nullptr;

  // For Array: indexScale = sizeof(element) in bytes (1, 2, 4, 8).
  uint32_t ArrayIndexScale = 0;

  // For Array: arrayOopDesc::base_offset_in_bytes(elementKind).
  // For Instance: 0 (instance fields use field->offset_in_bytes() directly).
  uint32_t ArrayBaseOffset = 0;

  // ===== Field/element layout =====
  // Discovered lazily from GEP+load/store patterns observed at
  // virtualization time. Entries are kept sorted by Offset to make
  // getFieldIndex() a binary search. Two different offsets within the same
  // VirtualObject MUST not overlap; if a store hits an overlapping range,
  // the object is forced to materialize (see Design note - type coercion).
  struct FieldDesc {
    int64_t Offset;     // byte offset from object base (header-relative)
    Type *LLVMType;     // i1/i8/i16/i32/i64/half/float/double/ptr addrspace(1)
    uint8_t ByteSize;   // sizeof(LLVMType) in bytes; cached for overlap checks
    bool IsReference;   // true iff LLVMType is ptr addrspace(1)

    bool overlaps(int64_t Off, uint8_t Size) const {
      return Off < Offset + ByteSize && Offset < Off + Size;
    }
  };
  SmallVector<FieldDesc, 8> Fields;

  // For Array: a fixed FieldDesc at -1 representing the array length slot.
  // We use a sentinel offset rather than a real one because the actual
  // arrayOopDesc::length_offset_in_bytes is target-dependent (not knowable
  // at LLVM-build time) - see Design note - array length slot.
  static constexpr int64_t ArrayLengthSlotOffset = -1;

  // ===== Identity tracking =====
  // True if this allocation's result is provably never compared with == to
  // anything other than null. In Jeandle ALL allocations have identity
  // (the frontend never emits VirtualBoxingNode equivalents), so the
  // question is whether identity is *observable*.
  //
  // Identity is unobservable iff the allocation result has a single use AND
  // that use is a PHI/Select instruction. Used as a precondition for PHI
  // virtualization across distinct allocation sites. Graal's analog is
  // EffectsClosure.isSingleUsageAllocation().
  bool IsSingleUsageAllocation = false;

  // True if this object is read by Object.hashCode() / System.identityHashCode
  // (or any instruction that consumes its identity hash). If true, the
  // object MUST materialize because the identity hash depends on the
  // object's address.
  bool IdentityHashObserved = false;

  // True if a synchronized-block on this object MUST be preserved (e.g.,
  // because the object escaped before the lock acquire). When false, locks
  // on this object are eliminated. Tracked here (rather than on
  // ObjectState) because it is a *global* property of the allocation;
  // ObjectState only tracks the *current* lock depth on this path.
  bool MustPreserveLocks = false;

public:
  VirtualObject(ObjectID id, ClassKind k, CallBase *alloc)
      : ID(id), Kind(k), AllocationCall(alloc) {}

  ObjectID getID() const { return ID; }
  ClassKind getKind() const { return Kind; }
  bool isInstance() const { return Kind == Instance; }
  bool isArray() const { return Kind == Array; }

  // ----- Field/element layout -----
  // Returns the index into Fields for the given byte offset, or -1 if no
  // entry covers exactly [Offset, Offset+ByteSize). Strict equality on
  // (Offset, ByteSize) - accesses that overlap a stored field with a
  // different size are NOT served by this and must cause materialization.
  int getFieldIndex(int64_t Offset, uint8_t ByteSize) const;

  // Lazily creates a FieldDesc for (Offset, Ty). Preconditions checked by
  // the caller: (a) Ty is a primitive or ptr addrspace(1); (b) Offset
  // does not overlap any existing FieldDesc with a different
  // (Offset, ByteSize). If a conflicting overlap is detected the method
  // returns -1 and the caller must force materialization.
  int getOrCreateFieldIndex(int64_t Offset, Type *Ty);

  // Returns the index of the special array-length slot for an Array
  // VirtualObject (asserts Kind == Array). The slot is created on first
  // use by computeFieldsForArray() during construction.
  int getArrayLengthFieldIndex() const;

  // For an Array: convert a constant element index into the FieldDesc
  // offset key. Returns -1 if Index is out of bounds. The result is
  // ArrayBaseOffset + Index * ArrayIndexScale and matches what the
  // frontend's compute_array_element_address() would produce.
  int64_t arrayElementOffset(uint32_t Index) const;

  // Inverse: parse a (base + index*scale) GEP chain into a constant
  // element index. Returns std::nullopt if the GEP is not in the canonical
  // "array_element_base + array_element_address" pattern emitted by
  // jeandleAbstractInterpreter::compute_array_element_address.
  std::optional<uint32_t> matchArrayElementGEP(GetElementPtrInst *GEP) const;

  // ----- Type metadata -----
  // The LLVM type a materialized instance of this object would have:
  // always `ptr addrspace(1)`, regardless of Instance vs Array.
  static Type *getMaterializedType(LLVMContext &Ctx) {
    return PointerType::get(Ctx, jeandle::AddrSpace::JavaHeapAddrSpace);
  }

  // Number of materialized entries. For Instance: Fields.size(). For
  // Array: ArrayLength (which equals the number of element entries; the
  // length slot is separate and is NOT counted here, mirroring Graal's
  // VirtualArrayNode.entryCount() == length).
  unsigned entryCount() const;

  // ----- Cloning -----
  // Produce a detached copy with NO ID (caller must register via
  // PEAResult::createVirtualObject to obtain a fresh ObjectID). Used at
  // PHI virtualization sites where two distinct objects merge into a new
  // virtual object (Graal: VirtualObjectNode.duplicate()).
  std::unique_ptr<VirtualObject> duplicate() const;
};
```

**Design note — no IsExact field**: Both `jeandle.new_instance` and `jeandle.newarray` attach `java-klass` AND `java-klass-exact` as return attributes (see `Attributes.h:31` and the call sites in `jeandleAbstractInterpreter.cpp:2755-2763, 2981-2992`). Every virtual object's type is therefore exact by construction. We do not store a separate `IsExact` bit, in contrast to Graal where `VirtualInstanceNode(type, hasIdentity)` carries the `hasIdentity` flag (`VirtualInstanceNode.java:46`).

**Design note — IsSingleUsageAllocation**: Graal's identity policy for PHI virtualization checks whether the allocation can be aliased through identity comparisons. We approximate this with the conservative *single-PHI-use* test: an allocation result that has exactly one use, and that use is a PHI or Select, cannot be `==`-compared to any non-PHI value, so two such allocations can be merged into a new virtual object without violating Java's `==` semantics. Otherwise, PHI virtualization across distinct allocation sites must materialize all inputs.

**Design note — constant array length**: Following Graal's `VirtualArrayNode(componentType, int length)` constructor (`VirtualArrayNode.java:54`), `length` is required to be a compile-time constant. We enforce this by refusing to virtualize a `jeandle.newarray` whose length argument is not a `ConstantInt`. Non-constant lengths are recorded but immediately classified `AlwaysEscapes`. Rationale: without a constant length, `entryCount()` is undefined and `arrayElementOffset()` cannot bound-check.

**Design note — type coercion policy**: Graal's `VirtualInstanceNode` derives each entry's `JavaKind` from the field declaration (`VirtualInstanceNode.java:111-115`); two stores at the same offset must agree on the kind by the verifier. In LLVM we do *not* have field declarations, so we discover types from store/load instructions. The policy is: *two stores at the same offset must agree on (Offset, ByteSize, IsReference)*. The element bit-width and reference-ness must match; we deliberately allow two stores of the same size but different scalar LLVM types (e.g., `i32` and `float`) to share an entry — the entry's `LLVMType` is locked to the type of the first store, and subsequent stores of compatible size are bitcast on demand at materialization. Any conflict in size or reference-ness forces materialization of the object before the conflicting access.

**Design note — array length slot**: Graal exposes array length via the `ArrayLengthProvider` interface and `VirtualArrayNode.findLength()` (returns a constant; `VirtualArrayNode.java:146`). We store the array length both as `ArrayLength` (the constant) and as a `FieldDesc` at the sentinel offset `ArrayLengthSlotOffset = -1`. The sentinel is used to satisfy any `jeandle.arraylength` call uniformly via the same field-lookup machinery, without requiring the analysis to know the target-dependent value of `arrayOopDesc::length_offset_in_bytes()`. The actual layout offset is irrelevant because PEA never emits a load of the length slot on a virtual object — it folds `jeandle.arraylength(virtual)` to the constant directly.

**Design note — header slots are not virtualizable**: The mark word and klass pointer at the object header are never represented as `FieldDesc` entries. Any access via GEP+load with offset 0 (mark) or offset 8 (klass, on 64-bit) to a virtual object forces materialization. Likewise, calls like `jeandle.load_klass` on a virtual object are *folded* (we know the klass constant) rather than served via the field machinery. This matches Graal's behavior, where `VirtualObjectNode.entryIndexForOffset` returns -1 for header offsets.

**Design note — duplicate()**: `VirtualObject::duplicate()` copies `Kind`, `AllocationCall`, `Klass`, `SizeInBytes`, `ArrayLengthVal`, `ArrayLength`, `ArrayElementType`, `ArrayIndexScale`, `ArrayBaseOffset`, the entire `Fields` vector, `MustPreserveLocks`, and `IdentityHashObserved`. It does NOT copy `ID` (the copy's ID is `InvalidObjectID` until registered) and resets `IsSingleUsageAllocation = false` (a merged object is by construction not a single-use allocation; it is the *result* of merging, which implies multiple uses through the PHI). This matches Graal's `afterClone()` resetting `objectId = -1` (`VirtualObjectNode.java:73-76`).

### 1.2 FieldValue (Entry Value Representation)

Following Graal's design, field entries store information about whether the slot holds a concrete LLVM value, a virtual-object reference, a materialized pointer, or is uninitialized (default zero). LLVM Values cannot represent "a reference to a virtual (not-yet-allocated) object" directly because such an object has no `Value*`. We therefore use a tagged union:

```cpp
class FieldValue {
public:
  enum Tag : uint8_t { Unknown, Scalar, VirtualRef, MaterializedRef };

private:
  Tag T = Unknown;
  union {
    Value *V;       // Scalar / MaterializedRef
    ObjectID Ref;   // VirtualRef
  } U = {nullptr};
  // The slot's declared LLVM type, taken from the discovering store/load.
  // For Unknown: nullptr. For VirtualRef: ptr addrspace(1).
  Type *DeclaredType = nullptr;

public:
  // ---- Construction ----
  static FieldValue unknown() { return {}; }
  static FieldValue scalar(Value *V) {
    assert(V && "scalar value must be non-null");
    FieldValue F; F.T = Scalar; F.U.V = V; F.DeclaredType = V->getType();
    return F;
  }
  static FieldValue virtualRef(ObjectID ID, Type *RefTy) {
    assert(ID != InvalidObjectID);
    assert(RefTy->isPointerTy() &&
           RefTy->getPointerAddressSpace() == jeandle::AddrSpace::JavaHeapAddrSpace);
    FieldValue F; F.T = VirtualRef; F.U.Ref = ID; F.DeclaredType = RefTy;
    return F;
  }
  static FieldValue materializedRef(Value *Ptr) {
    assert(Ptr && Ptr->getType()->isPointerTy());
    FieldValue F; F.T = MaterializedRef; F.U.V = Ptr;
    F.DeclaredType = Ptr->getType();
    return F;
  }

  // ---- Queries ----
  Tag getTag() const { return T; }
  bool isUnknown() const { return T == Unknown; }
  bool isScalar() const { return T == Scalar; }
  bool isVirtualRef() const { return T == VirtualRef; }
  bool isMaterializedRef() const { return T == MaterializedRef; }

  Value *getScalar() const { assert(isScalar()); return U.V; }
  ObjectID getVirtualRef() const { assert(isVirtualRef()); return U.Ref; }
  Value *getMaterialized() const { assert(isMaterializedRef()); return U.V; }
  Type *getDeclaredType() const { return DeclaredType; }

  // ---- Default values ----
  // Returns the Java default for FieldType: zero for all primitives,
  // ConstantPointerNull(addrspace(1)) for references. The semantics match
  // JLS section 4.12.5 and Graal's
  // ObjectState.createEscapeObjectState() default-value handling
  // (ObjectState.java:151-156). Used at materialization for Unknown entries.
  static Constant *defaultFor(Type *FieldType);

  // ---- Equality (for state convergence and merge dedup) ----
  // Two FieldValues are equivalent iff their Tag matches AND:
  //   - Scalar: their Value* are identical (no IR-equality; we rely on
  //     LLVM's value-numbering elsewhere)
  //   - VirtualRef: their ObjectID match AND the referenced ObjectStates
  //     in the containing PEABlockState are equivalent (transitively).
  //     Transitivity check is delegated to PEABlockState (see Section 1.4)
  //     because it requires access to the surrounding state.
  //   - MaterializedRef: their Value* are identical.
  //   - Unknown == Unknown.
  // The shallow equality method here ignores transitive VirtualRef
  // equivalence - that is the caller's responsibility.
  bool shallowEquals(const FieldValue &O) const;

  // Stable hash for use in DenseMap / set keys. Reference equality on
  // Value*; identity on ObjectID. Calls llvm::hash_value.
  llvm::hash_code hash() const;
};
```

**Invariant F1 (VirtualRef containment)**: If a `FieldValue` with tag `VirtualRef` holds `ID = X`, then in *every* `PEABlockState` that contains this `FieldValue` (transitively through any chain of `Entries`), the `ObjectStates[X]` must be present and in `Virtual` state. The analysis enforces this by *eagerly* updating all referring `FieldValue`s when an object materializes (see `updateStatesForMaterialized()`, mirroring Graal's `PartialEscapeClosure.updateStatesForMaterialized`). A stale `VirtualRef` pointing at a materialized object is a correctness bug; assertions in `PEABlockState::getObjectStateForModification` check this.

**Invariant F2 (no primitive/reference punning)**: A `FieldValue` whose `DeclaredType` is `ptr addrspace(1)` cannot be replaced by one whose `DeclaredType` is a primitive, or vice versa, without first materializing the containing object. Any such attempted store causes the analysis to invalidate virtualization for that object. Rationale: LLVM IR permits bitcasting between i64 and ptr, but the GC barrier and stack-map machinery (RewriteStatepointsForGC) requires precise type tracking. A primitive-to-pointer pun would silently smuggle a non-oop value into a slot that the GC treats as an oop.

**Invariant F3 (default for Unknown)**: An `Unknown` entry materializes to `FieldValue::defaultFor(FieldType)`, which is zero for every primitive and `ConstantPointerNull(addrspace(1))` for references. This matches both JLS section 4.12.5 default-value rules and Graal's behavior of clearing entries that equal the default (`ObjectState.java:151-156`).

### 1.3 ObjectState

```cpp
// The per-path state of one virtual object. Lives inside PEABlockState.
//
// State machine:
//
//        +-----------+ materialize(ptr)  +--------------+
//        |  Virtual  | ----------------> | Materialized |
//        +-----------+                   +--------------+
//             ^                                 |
//             |  (no transition back; once       updateMaterialized(ptr)
//             |   materialized, an object         (allowed: re-pointing the
//             |   stays materialized on this      materialized value to a
//             |   path)                           PHI that selects between
//             |                                   multiple materializations)
//             |
//        construction: always starts Virtual with empty entries
//
class ObjectState {
public:
  enum StateKind : uint8_t { Virtual, Materialized };

private:
  StateKind Kind = Virtual;

  // ---- Virtual mode payload ----
  // One FieldValue per FieldDesc in the VirtualObject. Indexed identically
  // to VirtualObject::Fields. SIZE is fixed at construction (= owning
  // VirtualObject's number of entries) and never changes.
  // Following Graal's invariant that `entries.length` is fixed once
  // `VirtualObjectNode.entryCount()` is known (ObjectState.java:174).
  SmallVector<FieldValue, 8> Entries;

  // Lock stack depth on the *current path*. Mirrors Graal's LockState
  // linked list (LockState.java:37) but collapsed to a depth count because
  // we do not track per-MonitorIdNode identity - Jeandle's monitorenter/
  // monitorexit are themselves the identity (each call site is one lock
  // level). Recursive locks increment normally.
  //
  // Lock-elision is sound iff every monitorenter on a virtual object has
  // a matching monitorexit reachable on every path. The analysis verifies
  // this by checking LockCount == 0 at every exit and at every escape
  // point; a non-zero LockCount at materialization forces all the lock
  // calls to be preserved (re-emitted on the materialized pointer).
  unsigned LockCount = 0;

  // ---- Materialized mode payload ----
  // The ptr addrspace(1) Value* that represents this object after escape.
  // Either:
  //  - the original AllocationCall (when the object never virtualized; we
  //    simply leave the allocation in place), OR
  //  - a fresh allocation call inserted by a Materialize Effect, OR
  //  - a PHI selecting between several materializations from predecessor
  //    blocks at a merge point.
  Value *MaterializedValue = nullptr;

  // ---- Common metadata ----
  // EnsureVirtualized: hard requirement that this object MUST NOT
  // materialize. Currently UNUSED in Jeandle (no equivalent of Graal's
  // EnsureVirtualizedNode at the IR level). Reserved for future use.
  // Kept for shape-compatibility with Graal (ObjectState.java:57).
  bool EnsureVirtualized = false;

  // Copy-on-write flag: when true, this ObjectState may be shared with
  // other PEABlockState objects, so the first modification must clone.
  // Mirrors Graal's `copyOnWrite` (ObjectState.java:62-65, set via share()).
  bool CopyOnWrite = false;

public:
  // ---- Construction ----
  // Create a Virtual state with `numEntries` Unknown entries.
  explicit ObjectState(unsigned numEntries)
      : Entries(numEntries, FieldValue::unknown()) {}

  // ---- Accessors ----
  StateKind getKind() const { return Kind; }
  bool isVirtual() const { return Kind == Virtual; }
  bool isMaterialized() const { return Kind == Materialized; }
  ArrayRef<FieldValue> entries() const { assert(isVirtual()); return Entries; }
  const FieldValue &getEntry(unsigned Idx) const {
    assert(isVirtual()); return Entries[Idx];
  }
  Value *getMaterializedValue() const {
    assert(isMaterialized() && MaterializedValue); return MaterializedValue;
  }
  unsigned getLockCount() const { return LockCount; }
  bool hasLocks() const { return LockCount != 0; }
  bool getEnsureVirtualized() const { return EnsureVirtualized; }

  // ---- Mutators (call only via PEABlockState::getObjectStateForModification) ----
  void setEntry(unsigned Idx, FieldValue V) {
    assert(isVirtual());
    Entries[Idx] = V;
  }

  // materialize: transition Virtual -> Materialized. CALLERS must
  // (a) have generated the allocation as a separate Effect, (b) updated
  // all FieldValues in other ObjectStates that reference this ID to use
  // MaterializedRef (via PartialEscapeClosure::updateStatesForMaterialized
  // in Graal; we mirror this), (c) re-emitted any held locks as monitor
  // calls on Ptr if MustPreserveLocks.
  void materialize(Value *Ptr) {
    assert(isVirtual());
    Kind = Materialized;
    MaterializedValue = Ptr;
    Entries.clear();   // entries are no longer authoritative
    // LockCount is intentionally retained for asserts during transform.
    // EnsureVirtualized is intentionally NOT cleared: it is a property of
    // the original allocation site and surviving it through materialization
    // would be a bug - callers MUST check getEnsureVirtualized() before
    // calling materialize().
  }

  // updateMaterializedValue: re-point the materialized pointer (only after
  // already materialized). Used at merges where multiple predecessor
  // materializations need to be unified through a PHI.
  void updateMaterializedValue(Value *NewPtr) {
    assert(isMaterialized() && NewPtr);
    MaterializedValue = NewPtr;
  }

  void addLock() { assert(isVirtual()); ++LockCount; }
  void removeLock() { assert(isVirtual() && LockCount > 0); --LockCount; }
  void setEnsureVirtualized(bool B) { EnsureVirtualized = B; }

  // ---- Copy-on-write ----
  void markShared() { CopyOnWrite = true; }
  bool isShared() const { return CopyOnWrite; }
  // Deep copy: clones Entries vector; clears CopyOnWrite on the result.
  ObjectState clone() const;

  // ---- Equivalence for fixpoint ----
  // Returns true iff (Kind, MaterializedValue, LockCount, Entries) all
  // match, ignoring CopyOnWrite and EnsureVirtualized. EnsureVirtualized
  // is omitted to match Graal's ObjectState.equals()
  // (ObjectState.java:307-320). VirtualRef entries are compared by
  // ObjectID equality only; deeper equivalence is the caller's
  // responsibility.
  bool equivalentTo(const ObjectState &O) const;

  // Stable hash for use in caching merge results. Combines Kind,
  // MaterializedValue (pointer identity), LockCount, and a hash over
  // Entries.
  llvm::hash_code hash() const;
};
```

**Invariant O1 (Kind/Value exclusivity)**: `Kind == Virtual` iff `MaterializedValue == nullptr`. Enforced by `materialize()` and asserted by the accessors. Mirrors Graal's `ObjectState.isVirtual()` assertion (`ObjectState.java:167`).

**Invariant O2 (entry count immutability)**: `Entries.size()` is set at construction to the owning `VirtualObject`'s entry count and never changes thereafter. Cloning preserves the size. Materialization clears entries (size becomes 0) — that state is terminal.

**Invariant O3 (lock balance for elision)**: For PEA to eliminate locks on object `O`, every monitorenter on `O` reachable from the allocation must have a matching monitorexit reachable before the object escapes or before function return. The analysis verifies this by:
1. Decrementing `LockCount` only via `removeLock()`, which asserts `> 0`.
2. At materialization, if `LockCount > 0`, the held locks are re-emitted on the materialized pointer (in correct nesting order) via Effects.
3. At function exit, all virtual objects must have `LockCount == 0` or the surviving locks are re-emitted on the materialized pointer (which forces materialization at the exit; we conservatively materialize before function return).

### 1.4 PEABlockState

```cpp
// The forward dataflow state at one program point (typically: at the
// entry or exit of a BasicBlock). Stores the per-object state and a
// small amount of block-level metadata. Acts as the lattice element in
// the fixpoint.
class PEABlockState {
public:
  // RefCount: shared sentinel for copy-on-write of the ObjectStates
  // vector itself (whole-array sharing). Mirrors Graal's
  // PartialEscapeBlockState.RefCount (PartialEscapeBlockState.java:77-79).
  // When Count > 1, any modification must first allocate a new RefCount
  // and clone the vector.
  struct RefCount {
    mutable unsigned Count = 1;
  };

private:
  // Indexed by ObjectID. Some entries may be std::nullopt if that object
  // is not reachable on the current path (this happens for objects
  // allocated only on a branch we didn't take). Following Graal's
  // PartialEscapeBlockState.objectStates
  // (PartialEscapeBlockState.java:62), which allows null entries.
  //
  // Stored as a shared_ptr to a vector so the whole-array COW works:
  // copying a PEABlockState bumps the RefCount; the vector is only cloned
  // on first modification when Count > 1.
  std::shared_ptr<SmallVector<std::optional<ObjectState>, 8>> ObjectStates;
  std::shared_ptr<RefCount> ArrayRefCount;

  // Dead: this block was proven unreachable during analysis (e.g., a
  // constant-folded conditional cut it off). Mirrors
  // EffectsBlockState.dead (EffectsBlockState.java:39). Dead blocks
  // contribute nothing to merges and have their effects suppressed.
  bool Dead = false;

  // Exception edges that the analysis has determined to be unreachable
  // (e.g., when a checkcast on a virtual object is folded). Used to
  // suppress unwind-edge state propagation. Mirrors
  // EffectsBlockState.exceptionEdgesToKill (EffectsBlockState.java:45).
  // The KEY identifies the dominating InvokeInst whose unwind edge is
  // dead; the value is the unwind successor BB. Cleared when:
  //   - the InvokeInst itself is replaced by a non-throwing equivalent
  //     (KillBranch CFG-kill effect);
  //   - control reaches a block where the InvokeInst no longer dominates.
  SmallDenseMap<InvokeInst*, BasicBlock*, 2> ExceptionEdgesToKill;

public:
  PEABlockState();
  PEABlockState(const PEABlockState &); // shallow-copies + bumps RefCount
  PEABlockState &operator=(const PEABlockState &);

  // ---- ObjectState management ----
  // Register a new virtual object, returning its ObjectID. Grows the
  // ObjectStates vector as needed. ID may be discontiguous if a previous
  // block already created higher IDs; ensureSize() pads with nullopt.
  void addObject(ObjectID ID, ObjectState State);

  bool hasObjectState(ObjectID ID) const;
  const ObjectState &getObjectState(ObjectID ID) const;
  ObjectState &getObjectStateForModification(ObjectID ID);
  // Returns nullptr if not present.
  const ObjectState *getObjectStateOptional(ObjectID ID) const;

  unsigned getStateCount() const {
    return ObjectStates ? ObjectStates->size() : 0;
  }

  // Reset all object states to a fresh empty vector of the given size.
  // Used during merge stabilization when materializations invalidate
  // prior merge decisions (matches Graal's
  // PartialEscapeBlockState.resetObjectStates(int), line 404).
  void resetObjectStates(unsigned NumObjects);

  // Fast-path adoption: if `Other` and `this` will agree on every
  // ObjectState (e.g., we are the only successor of Other), share the
  // underlying vector instead of cloning. Mirrors Graal's
  // adoptAddObjectStates (PartialEscapeBlockState.java:426).
  //
  // Precondition: `this` has not yet been modified after construction;
  // in practice this is called only by the merge processor when it has
  // decided that the merged state would equal one predecessor's state
  // exactly. Postcondition: every shared ObjectState in `Other` has its
  // CopyOnWrite flag set to true.
  void adoptObjectStates(const PEABlockState &Other);

  // ---- Alias resolution local to a path ----
  // Resolve a Value* to an ObjectID by chasing through:
  //   - BitCastInst, AddrSpaceCastInst, FreezeInst (passthroughs)
  //   - GetElementPtrInst with all-zero indices
  //   - PHINode whose incoming values all resolve to the same ObjectID
  //     (using the global AliasMap as the authoritative source)
  // Returns std::nullopt for any other shape. Does NOT recurse
  // infinitely (cycle-safe: uses a small visited set; returns nullopt
  // on cycle).
  std::optional<ObjectID> resolveVirtualRef(Value *V,
                                            const AliasMap &Aliases) const;

  // ---- Lifecycle ----
  bool isDead() const { return Dead; }
  void markDead() { Dead = true; }

  void killExceptionEdge(InvokeInst *I, BasicBlock *UnwindBB);
  bool isExceptionEdgeKilled(InvokeInst *I) const;
  ArrayRef<std::pair<InvokeInst*, BasicBlock*>> killedExceptionEdges() const;

  // ---- Fixpoint convergence ----
  // Two block states are equivalent iff:
  //   - both Dead, or both alive
  //   - for every ObjectID i in
  //     [0, max(getStateCount(), O.getStateCount())):
  //       both states have either nullopt at i, or equivalent ObjectStates
  //   - exceptionEdgesToKill match (set equality)
  // Mirrors PartialEscapeBlockState.equivalentTo (line 387).
  bool equivalentTo(const PEABlockState &O) const;

  // ---- Deep copy (for branch points) ----
  // Forces a full deep copy: clones the ObjectStates vector and every
  // contained ObjectState. The default copy constructor performs only a
  // shallow (refcount-bumping) copy.
  PEABlockState deepClone() const;

private:
  // Internal: returns a mutable pointer to the ObjectStates vector,
  // cloning if ArrayRefCount->Count > 1. Mirrors Graal's
  // getObjectStateArrayForModification (line 147).
  SmallVector<std::optional<ObjectState>, 8> *getArrayForModification();
};
```

**Design note — two-level COW**: There are TWO copy-on-write boundaries:

1. *Vector-level* (`ArrayRefCount`): bumping `RefCount` when copying a `PEABlockState` avoids cloning the entire `ObjectStates` vector. The vector is cloned only on the first modification when `Count > 1`.
2. *Element-level* (`ObjectState::CopyOnWrite`): each `ObjectState` carries its own COW flag, set when the vector is shared via `adoptObjectStates`. Modification of a shared element clones it in-place inside the vector.

This is exactly Graal's design (`PartialEscapeBlockState.java:147-163`). Initial implementation may use deeper cloning for simplicity (see Section 5.9); the data-structure shape supports the optimization later.

**Design note — ObjectStates indexed by global ObjectID**: ObjectIDs are issued globally (by `PEAResult::createVirtualObject`) and grow monotonically as the analysis discovers new allocations. A `PEABlockState` may legitimately have a sparse `ObjectStates` vector: positions for objects allocated only on paths not reaching this block are `std::nullopt`. The vector is grown on demand via `addObject(ID, ...)` (which calls `ensureSize(ID+1)`), mirroring `PartialEscapeBlockState.ensureSize` (line 366).

**Design note — Dead semantics**: A `Dead` block is excluded from merge: its `ObjectStates` are not consulted, and effects in `BlockEffects[BB]` for a `Dead` BB are dropped at commit time. A block can become `Dead` if a ControlFlow split's condition is folded to a constant (e.g., a checkcast on a virtual object) and one successor is provably unreachable. `Dead` is sticky: once set, it does not clear during fixpoint iteration.

**Design note — ExceptionEdgesToKill**: For an `InvokeInst` whose throw behavior the analysis has proven impossible (e.g., the receiver was a known-non-null virtual object, eliminating the implicit null check; or type narrowing proved the only thrown class is impossible), the unwind successor is recorded here. The transform pass converts the `InvokeInst` to a `CallInst` and inserts an unconditional branch to the normal successor. The unwind successor is *not* immediately removed because PHIs in the unwind block may reference virtual objects; cleanup is deferred to the CFG-kill effect pass.

### 1.5 Global Alias Map

```cpp
// Tracks two kinds of replacement:
//
// 1. VirtualAliases: LLVM Value* -> ObjectID.
//    Indicates that this Value is the "name" of a virtual object on every
//    path that reaches the Value's defining block. Examples:
//      - the CallBase returning the allocation result
//      - a BitCast/AddrSpaceCast of the above
//      - a PHI whose incoming values are all the same ObjectID
//      - a GEP with all-zero indices
//
// 2. ScalarAliases: LLVM Value* -> Value*.
//    A pure scalar replacement: e.g., a `load i32, ptr %fieldaddr`
//    replaced by the previously stored `i32 %v`. The original Value is
//    still alive in IR during analysis; the alias is applied by the
//    transform pass.
//
// Splitting into two maps (rather than one Value*->Value*) makes the
// distinction unambiguous in code, avoids confusion in propagation
// (HasVirtualInputs vs HasScalarReplacedInputs), and matches Graal's
// internal distinction between "alias points at a VirtualObjectNode" and
// "alias points at a ValueNode".
class AliasMap {
  // Primary maps.
  DenseMap<Value*, ObjectID> VirtualAliases;
  DenseMap<Value*, Value*>   ScalarAliases;

  // Reverse index: every Value that USES a virtually-aliased value, so
  // re-processing at loop iteration can quickly find affected users.
  // Mirrors the role of Graal's `hasVirtualInputs` NodeBitMap (set in
  // EffectsClosure when an alias is added).
  DenseSet<Instruction*> HasVirtualInputs;

  // Symmetric reverse index for scalar replacements: users of a scalar-
  // replaced value need re-canonicalization at Tier 3.
  DenseSet<Instruction*> HasScalarReplacedInputs;

public:
  // ---- Mutators ----
  // Register V as an alias for the virtual object with the given ID.
  // Also marks every current USER of V with HasVirtualInputs.
  // PRECONDITION: V is in addrspace(1) and is not already in VirtualAliases.
  void addVirtualAlias(Value *V, ObjectID ID);

  // Register V as scalar-replaced by Replacement.
  // PRECONDITION: V and Replacement have compatible types.
  void addScalarAlias(Value *V, Value *Replacement);

  // Reset alias for V (used at the start of a block during loop
  // re-iteration: Graal's `aliases.set(node, null)` in
  // EffectsClosure.processBlock).
  void resetAlias(Value *V);

  // ---- Queries ----
  std::optional<ObjectID> getVirtualAlias(Value *V) const;
  Value *getScalarAlias(Value *V) const;

  bool hasVirtualInputs(Instruction *I) const {
    return HasVirtualInputs.count(I);
  }
  bool hasScalarReplacedInputs(Instruction *I) const {
    return HasScalarReplacedInputs.count(I);
  }

  // Drill through any number of nested aliases:
  //   - if V is a scalar alias, follow the chain
  //   - if V is a virtual alias whose ObjectID is currently Materialized
  //     in the given state, return the MaterializedValue (so callers
  //     don't have to re-check the state every time)
  // Returns V itself if no alias applies.
  Value *resolve(Value *V, const PEABlockState &State) const;

  // ---- Lifecycle ----
  // Reset both maps and reverse indices entirely. Used at the start of
  // the analysis and on rollback (Section 2.5 overflow).
  void clear();

  // Snapshot for rollback at loop depth 1 (Section 2.5).
  AliasMap snapshot() const;
  void restore(const AliasMap &S);

  // Invalidate by Value identity: the analysis pass should call this
  // when it discards a previously-tracked instruction (e.g., when
  // reverting an optimistic virtualization). The transform pass does
  // NOT need this because it does not mutate the AliasMap.
  void invalidate(Value *V);
};
```

**Design note — global, not per-block**: The alias map is a function-wide table, not per-block, mirroring Graal's `EffectsClosure.aliases` which is a single `NodeMap` over the entire graph. Reasons:

- Once an alias is established at a Value's defining block, it is valid at every dominated use — there is no need to copy the alias into every block.
- Loop re-iteration may *change* an alias (e.g., a PHI input becomes virtualized in iteration 2 that wasn't in iteration 1). With a global map we can `resetAlias()` on entry to the loop's blocks during re-iteration; with per-block maps we would need to clone the entire map per block per iteration.

**Design note — ConstantExpr handling**: LLVM `ConstantExpr` (e.g., a GEP expressed as a constant) is not an `Instruction` and has no defining block. It cannot be a virtual alias because a virtual object's address is never constant (it is the SSA value of an allocation call). However, a `ConstantExpr` GEP whose base is a virtual alias *Instruction* would have that base appear in the operand list — but since `ConstantExpr` is unique'd across the whole module, we cannot safely add it to a per-function map. Policy: when the analysis encounters a `ConstantExpr` operand that references a virtual base, the use is treated as an escape (the use cannot be locally rewritten). This is consistent with `extractKlassConstant`'s philosophy of carefully traversing ConstantExpr chains rather than caching them (`JavaType.h:99`).

**Design note — RAUW awareness**: The analysis pass *never* mutates IR, so no aliases become stale during analysis. The transform pass mutates IR under the guidance of `PEAResult`; it does not consult the `AliasMap` after IR mutation. Therefore, `AliasMap` does not need `ValueHandle`-based RAUW callbacks; raw `Value*` pointers are sufficient.

**Design note — HasVirtualInputs propagation**: When `addVirtualAlias(V, ID)` is called, the implementation walks `V->users()` and adds every user `Instruction*` to `HasVirtualInputs`. This is one-shot: if a *new* user of V is created after the alias is added (which only happens via the analysis inserting an Effect that creates a future Instruction), the analysis records that user's effect explicitly. We do NOT use a use-listener.

### 1.6 PEAResult (Analysis Output)

```cpp
class PEAResult {
public:
  // ---- Virtual object inventory ----
  // unique_ptr ownership: PEAResult owns every VirtualObject. The vector
  // grows monotonically; existing pointers remain valid (no reallocation
  // hazard because we wrap each entry in unique_ptr).
  SmallVector<std::unique_ptr<VirtualObject>, 8> VirtualObjects;

  enum class EscapeKind : uint8_t {
    NeverEscapes, PartiallyEscapes, AlwaysEscapes
  };
  DenseMap<ObjectID, EscapeKind> EscapeClassification;

  // ---- Block exit states ----
  // The committed PEABlockState at each block's exit point. Required by
  // the transform pass for any effect whose payload references a virtual
  // object (it must know what state that object is in at the relevant
  // block).
  DenseMap<BasicBlock*, PEABlockState> BlockExitStates;

  // ---- Effects ----
  // Effect kinds enumerate every possible IR mutation. Granularity is
  // kept high enough that one Effect describes one IR-level change,
  // mirroring Graal's per-operation Effect subclasses in GraphEffectList.
  enum class EffectKind : uint16_t {
    // ===== Non-CFG-kill effects (applied in Pass 1, RPO+SeqNo order) =====

    // Replace the result of a Load with a Value already-existing in IR.
    ReplaceLoad,

    // Replace the result of a JavaOp CallInst/InvokeInst with a Value.
    // For folded checkcast/instanceof/arraylength/load_klass.
    ReplaceCall,

    // Replace one operand of an instruction with a different Value.
    // Used for in-place rewrites at e.g. icmp eq virtual,null -> false.
    ReplaceInput,

    // Drop a StoreInst that writes a field of a virtual object.
    EliminateStore,

    // Drop a monitorenter/monitorexit call.
    EliminateLock,

    // Drop the allocation call (InvokeInst for
    // jeandle.new_instance/newarray). Pass 2 also retargets the normal
    // successor (this is the CFG-kill).
    EliminateAllocation,

    // Materialize a virtual object before InsertBefore.
    Materialize,

    // Create a PHI node in Block for the named "field index" of an
    // ObjectID. The PHI's incoming pairs are computed by SetPHIInput
    // effects in predecessor blocks.
    CreatePHI,

    // Initialize a PHI's input. Must run AFTER the CreatePHI for that PHI.
    SetPHIInput,

    // Add a virtual alias to the global alias map. Stored so PEAResult
    // remains self-contained for diagnostics and verification.
    AddVirtualAlias,

    // Same, for scalar aliases. Used by Pass 1 to drive ReplaceLoad
    // rewrites at use sites discovered after the alias was recorded.
    AddScalarAlias,

    // ===== CFG-kill effects (Pass 2, after all Pass 1 effects) =====

    // Replace a conditional branch with an unconditional branch.
    KillBranch,

    // Delete an instruction from the IR.
    DeleteNode,
  };

  struct Effect {
    EffectKind Kind;

    // The block to which this effect belongs. Effects are bucketed by
    // BlockEffects[Block] for ordered iteration in transform.
    BasicBlock *Block = nullptr;

    // Stable ordering key within a block. Assigned at Effect creation
    // time (monotonically increasing counter per analysis run). Two
    // Effects in the same Block are applied in ascending SeqNo order.
    uint32_t SeqNo = 0;

    // Common targets.
    Instruction *Target = nullptr;         // affected/erased/replaced
    Instruction *InsertBefore = nullptr;   // for Materialize, CreatePHI

    // Payload fields (only those relevant to Kind are valid).
    Value      *Replacement = nullptr;       // ReplaceLoad/Call/Input, AddScalarAlias
    unsigned    InputIndex = 0;              // ReplaceInput
    ObjectID    ObjID = InvalidObjectID;     // Materialize, AddVirtualAlias, CreatePHI
    SmallVector<FieldValue, 8> FieldValues;  // Materialize
    SmallVector<ObjectID, 2>   Prerequisites;// Materialize
    unsigned    LockCount = 0;               // Materialize
    int         PHIFieldIndex = -1;          // CreatePHI (-1 == "alias PHI")
    Type       *PHIType = nullptr;           // CreatePHI
    SmallVector<Value*, 4>       PHIIncomingValues;  // SetPHIInput
    SmallVector<BasicBlock*, 4>  PHIIncomingBlocks;  // SetPHIInput
    bool        BranchCondition = false;     // KillBranch
  };

  // ---- Effect lists, partitioned by purpose ----

  // BlockEffects[B] : effects produced by processing instructions inside
  // B. The transform pass applies these in two passes (non-CFG-kill
  // first, then CFG-kill) per block, walking blocks in RPO.
  DenseMap<BasicBlock*, SmallVector<Effect, 16>> BlockEffects;

  // MergeEffects[MergeBB] : effects produced when computing the merge
  // of predecessor states INTO MergeBB. These include CreatePHI effects
  // for field PHIs and AddVirtualAlias effects for the merged virtual
  // objects. Separated from BlockEffects because they may be cleared and
  // recomputed during merge stabilization.
  DenseMap<BasicBlock*, SmallVector<Effect, 8>> MergeEffects;

  // AfterMergeEffects[MergeBB] : effects that must run after every
  // CreatePHI in MergeBB has been applied. Specifically: SetPHIInput
  // effects, because they reference the PHI node that CreatePHI
  // produces.
  DenseMap<BasicBlock*, SmallVector<Effect, 8>> AfterMergeEffects;

  // LoopMergeEffects[Loop] : effects synthesized for a loop header's
  // *fixpoint* merge. Stored separately so that on non-convergence, the
  // per-iteration loop body effects can be discarded without losing the
  // entry-side initialization.
  DenseMap<const Loop*, SmallVector<Effect, 8>> LoopMergeEffects;

  // ---- Counters (for gating, diagnostics, stable test output) ----
  int VirtualizationDelta = 0;   // +virtualized - materialized
  int AllocationDelta = 0;       // +inserted by Materialize - deleted by EliminateAllocation
  uint32_t NextSeqNo = 0;        // for Effect::SeqNo assignment

  // ---- Convenience ----
  ObjectID createVirtualObject(std::unique_ptr<VirtualObject> VO);

  // Bucket helpers.
  uint32_t nextSeqNo() { return NextSeqNo++; }
  void addBlockEffect(Effect E);
  void addMergeEffect(BasicBlock *BB, Effect E);
  void addAfterMergeEffect(BasicBlock *BB, Effect E);

  // Clear effects for a set of blocks. Used during loop re-iteration.
  void clearBlockEffects(ArrayRef<BasicBlock*> Blocks);
  void clearMergeEffects(BasicBlock *MergeBB);
  void clearAfterMergeEffects(BasicBlock *MergeBB);
  void clearLoopMergeEffects(const Loop *L);

  // True iff at least one Effect would alter IR. Used to gate the
  // transform pass; a result with no effects is a no-op pass and we can
  // skip the transform entirely.
  bool hasOptimizationOpportunity() const;
};
```

**Design note — effect granularity**: One `Effect` = one IR-level operation. This keeps Effects easy to apply in isolation, easy to clear during loop re-iteration, and easy to test. We deliberately do NOT have aggregated effects like "MaterializeAndStoreAndInitialize" — instead, materialization emits a `Materialize` effect, and field initialization is baked into the `Materialize`'s `FieldValues` snapshot consumed inside the transform's materialization routine.

**Design note — CFG-kill classification**: Following Graal's `Effect.isCfgKill()` (`EffectList.java:56`, `GraphEffectList.java:291,312`), effects that change the CFG (remove edges, replace branches, delete invoke nodes) are applied AFTER all non-CFG-kill effects. Reason: CFG changes invalidate PHI input indices in successor blocks. By the time we apply CFG-kill effects, all PHIs that needed updating have been replaced or patched, and PHIs that survive can be safely re-indexed by erasing the correct incoming entry.

**Design note — deterministic ordering**: The transform pass MUST produce identical IR on every run for the same input. We guarantee this by:
1. Iterating BasicBlocks in reverse postorder (RPO), computed once via `ReversePostOrderTraversal<Function*>` for stable iteration.
2. Within a block, sorting `BlockEffects[BB]` by `(SeqNo, ptrOrder(Target))` before applying.
3. Within `MergeEffects` / `AfterMergeEffects` / `LoopMergeEffects`, the same `SeqNo` ordering applies.
4. `SeqNo` is assigned in the order `addBlockEffect()` etc. are called. The analysis pass is structured so that the order of `add*Effect` calls is itself a deterministic function of the input function.

**Design note — Effects do not mutate analysis state**: An `Effect` is a *pure declarative record*. Adding an Effect to `PEAResult` does NOT change the analysis's view of any `Value*`'s alias, any `ObjectState`'s entries, or any `PEABlockState`. The analysis updates its own state via `PEABlockState::*` / `AliasMap::*` methods first, then records the equivalent Effect for the transform. This strict separation is the same as Graal's: `EffectsClosure` updates its `BlockT` lattice element via private methods, and only adds to its `effects` field as a side-channel.

**Design note — VirtualizationDelta and AllocationDelta**: Following Graal (`GraphEffectList.java:64-79`). After analysis:
- `VirtualizationDelta > 0` means the pass discovered net virtualization opportunities. The transform should run.
- `AllocationDelta < 0` means more allocations were ELIMINATED than INSERTED (the common case for successful PEA).
- `AllocationDelta == 0 && VirtualizationDelta == 0` means PEA found no opportunities and the transform pass is a no-op; skip it.

These counters drive the `hasOptimizationOpportunity()` gating method.

### 1.7 Cross-Structure Invariants

The following invariants tie the data structures together. The analysis pass MUST maintain all of them; assertion checks in the transform pass verify them as a defense-in-depth measure.

**Invariant V1 (VirtualObject lifetime)**: `PEAResult::VirtualObjects` is the sole owner of every `VirtualObject`. The vector only grows during analysis (never shrinks, never reorders). Therefore raw `VirtualObject*` pointers obtained at any time remain valid throughout the analysis and transform passes. Justification: `unique_ptr` storage + push-only access pattern.

**Invariant V2 (ObjectID stability)**: A `VirtualObject`'s `ID` is set once at creation by `PEAResult::createVirtualObject` and is `const`. No operation changes an ID. PHI virtualization that creates a *new* merged virtual object obtains a *new* ObjectID via a fresh `createVirtualObject(duplicate())` call.

**Invariant V3 (ObjectStates <-> VirtualObjects correspondence)**: For every `ObjectID i` for which any `PEABlockState` contains a non-nullopt `ObjectStates[i]`, the corresponding `PEAResult::VirtualObjects[i]` MUST be present (i.e., `i < VirtualObjects.size()`). The converse does not hold: a VirtualObject may exist in `VirtualObjects` but have nullopt in some block states (object not yet allocated on those paths).

**Invariant V4 (Materialization side effects)**: When an `ObjectState` transitions Virtual->Materialized in some `PEABlockState`:
1. Every other `ObjectState` in the same `PEABlockState` whose entries contain a `VirtualRef` to the materialized object MUST be updated to replace those `VirtualRef`s with `MaterializedRef`s pointing to the new materialized `Value*` (Graal: `updateStatesForMaterialized`).
2. The global `AliasMap` MUST be updated: any `Value*` previously mapped to the materialized object's `ObjectID` is now resolved (via `AliasMap::resolve`) to the materialized `Value*`. This is automatic because `resolve` consults the per-block state.
3. A `Materialize` effect MUST be added to `BlockEffects[BB]` with `InsertBefore` set to the IR position triggering the materialization.

**Invariant V5 (Address-space discipline)**: Every `Value*` that appears as the target of `addVirtualAlias` is a pointer in `addrspace(1)`. Every `MaterializedRef` is also a pointer in `addrspace(1)` (or a PHI of such). The analysis filters out any candidate whose type does not satisfy this. Justification: `jeandle::AddrSpace::JavaHeapAddrSpace = 1` is the canonical marker for "this pointer references a Java heap object" (`Metadata.h:32`); only such pointers participate in the GC barrier and RewriteStatepointsForGC machinery downstream.

**Invariant V6 (Metadata preservation on Load replacement)**: When a Load is replaced via `ReplaceLoad`, the Load's `!java-klass`, `!java-klass-exact`, `!nonnull`, `!range`, and `!align` metadata MUST be *forwarded* to the Replacement value by way of an `llvm::AAMDNodes`-style merge:
- If the Replacement is a `Constant`, no merge is needed (constants do not carry instruction metadata).
- If the Replacement is an `Instruction` and the metadata strengthens it, the transform attaches the metadata as long as the Replacement *dominates* every use that previously had access to the Load's metadata.
- Conflicting metadata (e.g., the Load knew the klass, the Replacement is a known-different-klass constant — a bug) raises an assertion.

Concretely, this is implemented in the transform pass's `replaceLoadPreservingMetadata(Load*, Value*)` helper, which mirrors LLVM's standard practice for ScalarEvolution/InstSimplify replacements but additionally copies the Jeandle-specific Java metadata.

---


---

## 2. Analysis Pass: `PartialEscapeAnalysis`

```cpp
class PartialEscapeAnalysis : public AnalysisInfoMixin<PartialEscapeAnalysis> {
  friend AnalysisInfoMixin<PartialEscapeAnalysis>;
  static AnalysisKey Key;
public:
  using Result = PEAResult;
  Result run(Function &F, FunctionAnalysisManager &FAM);
};
```

### 2.1 High-Level Algorithm

Following Graal's `PartialEscapeClosure` design, the analysis records ALL transformations as deferred per-block effects. Effects are not applied during analysis; they are consumed by the transform pass after convergence. The algorithm is a forward, flow-sensitive dataflow with a per-loop fixpoint iteration.

```
PartialEscapeAnalysis::run(Function &F, FunctionAnalysisManager &FAM):
  1. Guard: skip the function unless the module's named metadata
     "jeandle.java_method_compilation" lists F. This metadata is attached
     by JeandleAbstractInterpreter on every function that originates from a
     Java method (vs. runtime stub functions, vector helpers, or imported
     intrinsics). Without it we cannot trust the IR conventions PEA assumes
     (the addrspace(1) heap-ref convention, the jeandle.* JavaOp callee
     names, the "deopt" operand bundle on invokes, etc.).
     Implementation: scan F.getParent()->getNamedMetadata("jeandle.java_method_compilation").
     If absent, return an empty PEAResult with hasOptimizationOpportunity()==false.

  2. Acquire analyses from FAM:
       DominatorTree     &DT  = FAM.getResult<DominatorTreeAnalysis>(F);
       LoopInfo          &LI  = FAM.getResult<LoopAnalysis>(F);
       PostDominatorTree &PDT = FAM.getResult<PostDominatorTreeAnalysis>(F);
       TargetLibraryInfo &TLI = FAM.getResult<TargetLibraryAnalysis>(F);
     PDT is used to identify post-dominator-defined safe materialization
     points. TLI is used to recognise the `jeandle.*` callee set even when
     declarations carry attributes that might confuse name matching.

  3. Compute the iteration order:
     a. Compute reverse-post-order (RPO) over the CFG starting at
        F.getEntryBlock(): ReversePostOrderTraversal<Function*> RPOT(&F).
     b. Identify natural loops via LoopInfo and tag the loop headers.
     c. Detect irreducible CFGs by running Tarjan's SCC over the CFG once.
        For each non-trivial SCC, test whether any block in the SCC is a
        LoopInfo header for a loop whose body equals that SCC. If not, mark
        the SCC as irreducible and set `IrreducibleFallback = true` for
        every block in the region.
     d. Identify unreachable blocks: blocks B with no predecessor other
        than themselves AND that are not the entry block. For each such B,
        set `state.Dead = true` and skip Tier-1/2/3 processing for B's
        instructions. (LLVM does not always prune these; SimplifyCFG runs
        AFTER PEA in the canonical schedule, but PEA may be moved earlier,
        so we cannot assume cleanliness.)

  4. Collect allocation sites in one pass: scan F for `CallBase`s whose
     called function is `jeandle.new_instance` or `jeandle.newarray`. Store
     them in a `DenseMap<BasicBlock*, SmallVector<CallBase*, 4>>` for fast
     lookup during block processing.

  5. Construct the entry block state and pre-populate side-tables that hold
     for ALL execution paths reaching the function:
     a. Create an empty PEABlockState for F.getEntryBlock(). No virtual
        objects exist at entry — function arguments are by definition
        already-escaped references owned by the caller.
     b. For each Argument A of F:
        - If A has the `nonnull` attribute (or is a primitive value),
          record this in `NonNullArgs` (a DenseSet<Value*>) used by Tier 3
          to fold `icmp eq A, null` to false.
        - If A has the `java-klass`/`java-klass-exact` attributes, record
          the klass constant in `ArgKlass[A]` so Tier 3 can fold
          `jeandle.checkcast(K, A)` etc. when K is a supertype of
          ArgKlass[A].
        - A is NOT added to the alias map: it stays as itself. Aliases
          only map V to a REPLACEMENT; the identity case relies on
          `AliasMap.getAlias(V)` returning V when no entry exists.
     c. Record `align`, `dereferenceable`, `dereferenceable_or_null`
        argument attributes into a `DerefSizeMap` used by Tier 3 when
        folding `ptrtoint` of an argument inside an icmp.
     d. Register all `Constant` global pointers reachable via the
        function's instructions as scalar values (they are immutable, so
        they cannot be virtual). This includes `ConstantPointerNull`,
        `GlobalVariable`, and `ConstantExpr` roots.

  6. Initialize the global alias map (empty) and the per-block effect
     lists (empty). Allocate `VirtualizationDelta = 0` and
     `AllocationDelta = 0` counters used to decide whether the transform
     pass needs to run.

  7. Forward dataflow in RPO with loop fixpoint:
     for each BB in RPO:
       if BB is Dead: continue
       if BB is a loop header:
         processLoop(LI.getLoopFor(BB))  // see Section 2.5; runs the body
                                          // to fixpoint, possibly
                                          // downgrading to MATERIALIZE_ALL
                                          // on overflow.
         continue
       processBlock(BB)

     processBlock(BB):
       1. Merge predecessor exit states into a fresh PEABlockState. The
          merge uses the iterative do-while loop described in Section 2.4
          (the merge body may inject materializations into predecessor
          blocks and must repeat until quiescent).
          - If ALL predecessors had Dead==true, mark BB as Dead and return.
          - If BB has zero predecessors (entry block), use the entry state
            from step 5.
       2. Reset aliases for all PHI nodes of BB: AliasMap.resetAlias(phi)
          for each PHI. This is Graal's `aliases.set(node, null)` pattern
          — on re-iteration of a loop, the PHI's previous alias must be
          discarded so it can be re-resolved against new predecessor
          states.
       3. Process PHIs first (Section 2.3.19 — handled by the PHI
          subagent).
       4. For each non-PHI instruction I in BB in textual order:
            applyThreeTier(I)             // see Section 2.2.
       5. Snapshot the resulting state as BlockExitStates[BB].

  8. After the RPO pass converges (loops fixpointed, all blocks
     processed):
     a. Walk every `unique_ptr<VirtualObject>` and consult the final
        block exit states to classify each:
          - NeverEscapes:   no Materialize effect anywhere references its
                            ID.
          - AlwaysEscapes:  every block that contains a use of the
                            allocation either materializes it OR is on
                            the only path reaching it.
          - PartiallyEscapes: at least one Materialize effect exists AND
                            at least one path through F in which the
                            object's state remains Virtual at every sink
                            (return/escape) on that path.
     b. Call PEAResult::commitEffects() to lift the per-block effect
        lists into the final mappings used by the transform pass.
     c. If VirtualizationDelta + AllocationDelta == 0, set
        hasOptimizationOpportunity()==false so the transform pass is
        skipped.

  9. Return PEAResult.
```

#### 2.1.1 The "java-method-compilation" guard

Jeandle compiles three distinct kinds of LLVM functions in a single Module:
(a) the Java method being JIT-compiled (only one per compile request),
(b) the imported template module that contains runtime stub bodies such as
`jeandle.new_instance`, `jeandle.monitorenter_with_*`, and GC barriers, and
(c) intrinsic helpers added by various LLVM passes (e.g. `llvm.assume`).

PEA is only sound for case (a) because only there are the IR conventions —
`ptr addrspace(1)` for Java heap pointers, `"deopt"` operand bundles on every
side-effecting invoke, the `jeandle.*` callee names being honest about side
effects — guaranteed to hold. The stub bodies in (b) use raw memory
operations on addrspace(0) and addrspace(1) without respecting Java
semantics.

We use the module-level named metadata `!jeandle.java_method_compilation`
whose operands are MDTuple references to the Java functions. The frontend
attaches one entry per compile. Run the pass over a Function only if it
appears in that list.

#### 2.1.2 Iteration order, irreducible loops, unreachable blocks

LLVM RPO (`ReversePostOrderTraversal<Function*>`) suffices for reducible
CFGs. For irreducible CFGs:

- `LoopInfo` only recognises natural loops. If an SCC of the CFG with more
  than one block has more than one entry, none of its blocks are reported
  as loop headers, yet the dataflow does not converge in a single RPO
  pass.
- Detection: Tarjan's SCC over the CFG; for each non-trivial SCC, test
  whether any block is a LoopInfo header for a loop whose body equals
  the SCC. If not, mark the SCC irreducible.
- Behaviour for irreducible SCCs: every allocation `A` whose `BasicBlock`
  parent is in the SCC, OR whose result has a use inside the SCC, is
  immediately escaped — record no VirtualObject for it. Allocations
  entirely outside the SCC are processed normally. Blocks inside the SCC
  still get a `PEABlockState`, but their state is reduced to "all virtual
  references reaching this block are materialized at the block entry."

Unreachable blocks: B such that DT does not dominate B from entry. Set
`state.Dead = true`. When merging into a successor, predecessor states
with `Dead==true` are skipped (their values do not contribute to PHIs; the
PHI input slot is filled with `undef` or the existing IR operand, but the
alias map for that slot is treated as the empty set). If ALL predecessors
are Dead, the successor is Dead.

### 2.2 Three-Tier Instruction Processing Model

Following Graal's `processNode` / `processNodeInternal` design. The same
instruction may be processed by Tier 1, then Tier 2, then Tier 3 within a
single pass over the block — these are not mutually exclusive. The
`applyThreeTier(I)` driver:

```
applyThreeTier(Instruction *I):
  // Tier 1: Allocation nodes — always processed regardless of input state.
  if (isJeandleAllocationCall(I)) {
    tier1Allocate(cast<CallBase>(I));
    // Tier 1 success returns; the instruction is now an alias for a
    // VirtualObject and its later uses will be handled by Tier 2.
    return;
  }

  // Tier 2: Virtualizable patterns with at least one virtual input.
  bool processedAsVirtualizable = false;
  if (AliasMap.hasVirtualInputs(I)) {
    if (isRecognisedVirtualizablePattern(I)) {
      processedAsVirtualizable = applyVirtualizationRule(I);
      // applyVirtualizationRule sets I's alias if it succeeded
      // (e.g., a load on a known field becomes an alias for the stored
      // value).
    }
    if (!processedAsVirtualizable) {
      // Generic case: materialize every virtual operand at I, then
      // proceed.
      materialiseVirtualOperands(I);
    }
  }

  // Tier 3: Canonicalisation/simplification driven by replaced inputs.
  if (AliasMap.hasScalarReplacedInputs(I)) {
    tryCanonicalize(I);
  }
```

**Tier 1 — Allocation nodes** (always processed): exactly the call sites of
`@jeandle.new_instance` and `@jeandle.newarray`. They are always
`InvokeInst`s in current Jeandle frontend output (they carry a `"deopt"`
operand bundle and may throw OOM on the unwind path), but PEA must accept
plain `CallInst` as well — a later pipeline stage (or future inlining)
might rewrite invokes to calls. Detect by callee name:
`getCalledFunction() && getCalledFunction()->getName() == "jeandle.new_instance"`.

**Tier 2 — Nodes with virtual inputs**: triggered solely by the
`HasVirtualInputs` set. The set is populated by `AliasMap.addVirtualAlias`
which, when binding `V -> VirtualObject`, walks `V->users()` and inserts
each direct user `U` into `HasVirtualInputs`. Transitivity is implicit:
once U is processed by Tier 2 and itself becomes an alias for a virtual
object (e.g. a load that resolves to a `FieldValue::virtualRef`), its
addVirtualAlias call propagates to U's users.

Recognised virtualizable patterns are exactly the rules in Sections 2.3.3
through 2.3.18. Any instruction not matching one of those patterns falls
into the generic escape case (Section 2.3.18).

**Tier 3 — Nodes with scalar-replaced inputs**: triggered by the
`HasScalarReplacedInputs` set, populated by `AliasMap.addScalarAlias`.
This tier mirrors Graal's `processNodeWithScalarReplacedInputs`. Valid
canonicalisable instructions in our LLVM setting:

- `icmp` (one or two replaced operands) — try
  `InstructionSimplifier::simplifyICmpInst`. If it yields a `Constant`,
  record `Effect::ReplaceCall` (we re-use that effect kind for any value
  replacement). If it yields a non-constant simplification, record
  `Effect::AddScalarAlias` to canonicalize uses.
- `bitcast`, `addrspacecast`, `inttoptr`, `ptrtoint`, `trunc`, `zext`,
  `sext`, `fpext`, `fptrunc`, `fneg`, `select` (constant condition),
  binary `add/sub/mul/and/or/xor/shl/lshr/ashr` — pass to
  `llvm::simplifyInstruction()` with the operand-replacement map.
- `extractvalue`/`extractelement` from a constant aggregate (rare in
  Jeandle IR but possible) — fold to the embedded constant.
- `freeze` — if the input becomes a non-undef constant, replace with the
  constant.

For each successful canonicalisation, record:
- `Effect::ReplaceCall` with a Constant replacement (handles general
  value replacement; the effect kind name is historical), OR
- `Effect::ReplaceInput` if only one operand of I is now improved but I
  itself does not simplify (allows downstream Tier 3 to see better
  inputs without forcing a replacement).

#### 2.2.1 Workset / fixpoint inside a single block

Within a block, instructions are processed in textual order. This is
sufficient because:

1. `addVirtualAlias(V, ...)` is only called when V was just produced by
   the current instruction I (Tier 1 allocation, or a load whose entry
   is a `VirtualRef`). All `V->users()` within the current block come
   AFTER I in textual order, so they will be visited later in the same
   iteration. Cross-block users are handled when their block is
   processed.
2. `addScalarAlias(V, Repl)` has the same property.
3. No worklist within a block — but a cross-block worklist is needed for
   loop iteration (Section 2.5).

**Intra-block subtlety**: Tier 3 canonicalisation may create NEW alias
entries that affect instructions textually BEFORE the current one ONLY
when the new alias is for a value defined earlier in the block. By
construction (Tier 3 only replaces the CURRENT instruction with a
value), the new alias's domain is always the current instruction, whose
later users have not been visited yet. So one forward sweep suffices.

#### 2.2.2 "Process to completion" semantics

When `addVirtualAlias(V, ID, VObj)` runs:
- It calls `for (User *U : V->users()) HasVirtualInputs.insert(U);`.
- It does NOT recursively call `applyThreeTier(U)`. The driver's outer
  loop reaches U in textual order (same block) or in RPO (later block).

When `addScalarAlias(V, Repl)` runs:
- `HasScalarReplacedInputs.insert(U)` for each user U.
- Also `HasVirtualInputs.insert(U)` if Repl is itself a virtual reference
  (rare; happens when canonicalisation produced a value that is itself an
  alias of a virtual object).
- Does NOT cascade.

For Tier 1: (i) record `Effect::EliminateAllocation`, (ii)
`addVirtualAlias(allocResult, ID, VObj)`. After (ii), every user of
allocResult in the same block is in `HasVirtualInputs` and will be
visited correctly when the driver reaches them.

#### 2.2.3 Tier interaction on the same instruction

An instruction I can be in both `HasVirtualInputs` and
`HasScalarReplacedInputs` simultaneously. Order:

- Tier 1 runs first (only for allocations).
- Tier 2 runs SECOND. If Tier 2 succeeds and replaces I with a value, do
  NOT run Tier 3 on I (clear `HasScalarReplacedInputs[I]` when Tier 2
  records a replacement effect).
- Tier 2 may fail (non-constant load offset, etc.) and materialise the
  virtual operand. After materialisation the operand is no longer a
  virtual ref — Tier 3 can run on the resulting instruction.
- Tier 3 runs THIRD.

#### 2.2.4 Effects recorded per tier (cross-reference)

Each per-instruction rule below states the exact `EffectKind` values it
records into `BlockEffects[currentBlock]`, following the enum in
Section 1.6. Multiple effects per instruction are common (e.g. an
eliminated store records `EliminateStore` and nothing else, while an
allocation records `EliminateAllocation` plus `AddVirtualAlias`).

### 2.3 Instruction Processing Rules

For every kind of instruction PEA may encounter, the analysis specifies
the exact LLVM detection predicate, the state mutations, the effects
recorded, and the alias-map updates. The rules apply unchanged regardless
of where PEA sits in the LLVM pipeline; we explicitly do NOT assume any
particular frontend pattern beyond the conventions documented in
Section 1.

#### 2.3.1 Allocation: `invoke @jeandle.new_instance(klass, size) [ "deopt"(...) ]`

**Detection**.
```cpp
auto *CB = dyn_cast<CallBase>(I);
if (!CB || !CB->getCalledFunction() ||
    CB->getCalledFunction()->getName() != "jeandle.new_instance")
  return false;
```
Normally an `InvokeInst`; we also accept `CallInst`.

**Pre-conditions to virtualize**. If any check below fails, leave the
instruction untouched (no virtualization, no escape — the allocation
remains in IR and executes as written):

1. Current block must not be Dead and must not be part of an irreducible
   SCC (Section 2.1.2).
2. `klass = CB->getArgOperand(0)` must reduce, via
   `jeandle::extractKlassConstant(klass)`, to a non-null `Klass*`
   constant. If non-constant, skip virtualization. The Jeandle frontend
   always emits this as a constant, but a future pass could substitute
   a load. Implementation reuses `jeandle::extractKlassConstant` from
   `TypeCheckElimination.cpp`.
3. `size = CB->getArgOperand(1)` must be a `ConstantInt` (only fixed-size
   instances are virtualisable). The frontend always emits this as
   `klass->size_helper() * HeapWordSize`.
4. The klass must not be a value-based class (see 2.3.13). Test via
   `VMCallbacks::IsValueBased(Klass)`.
5. The klass must be fully initialised and instantiable. Test via
   `VMCallbacks::IsInitialized(Klass)`.
6. If CB is an `InvokeInst`, its unwind successor's first non-PHI must
   NOT be a `CleanupPadInst` or `CatchPadInst` (Windows EH not
   supported). Assert in debug, warn-and-skip in release.

**Actions on success**.
- Allocate `VirtualObject` with `Kind=Instance`, `Klass=K`,
  `SizeInBytes=S`, `AllocationCall=CB`.
- Create `ObjectState` with `Kind=Virtual`, `Entries` empty (lazy field
  discovery), `LockCount=0`, `EnsureVirtualized=false`,
  `CopyOnWrite=false`.
- Allocate `ObjectID` and register in current `PEABlockState`.
- `AliasMap::addVirtualAlias(CB, ID, VObj)`; this also marks every user
  of CB in `HasVirtualInputs`.
- Record `Effect::EliminateAllocation(Target=CB, Block=currentBlock)`.
- Record `Effect::AddVirtualAlias(Target=CB, Block=currentBlock,
  ObjID=ID, AliasVObj=VObj)`.
- Increment `VirtualizationDelta` and `AllocationDelta` (per Graal's
  `effects.addVirtualizationDelta(1)` / `addAllocationDelta(1)`).

**Exception edge handling for `InvokeInst`**.
- For the unwind successor, the virtual object does NOT exist (the
  allocation failed). Snapshot the `PEABlockState` BEFORE the alias was
  added and use that snapshot as input to the unwind successor.
- For the normal successor, the virtual object is in scope.
- `EliminateAllocation` and `AddVirtualAlias` are recorded against the
  invoke's own block. The transform pass, when removing the invoke,
  rewrites the CFG so control flows directly from the invoke's
  predecessor through to the normal successor; the unwind successor's
  edge from this allocation site is removed (handled by
  `Effect::DeleteNode` and its `KillBranch` companion in Pass 2).

**`"deopt"` operand bundle**. The bundle carries debug info that PEA
ignores for analysis. The transform pass must NOT preserve this bundle on
the allocation being REMOVED. For MATERIALIZED allocations (the
Materialize effect creates a fresh invoke), the bundle MUST be preserved
verbatim because downstream RewriteStatepointsForGC and deopt-info
machinery require it.

**Attribute propagation**. Replacement values must not carry stale
attributes from CB. When materialising, the new invoke we synthesise
carries the same return attributes (`java-klass=K`, `java-klass-exact`,
`nonnull`, `dereferenceable=S`) as CB. When scalar-replacing field
loads, the loaded value's metadata is taken from the stored value (see
2.3.4 for `!java-klass` propagation).

#### 2.3.2 Allocation: `invoke @jeandle.newarray(array_klass, length) [ "deopt"(...) ]`

**Detection**: same shape as 2.3.1 with callee name `jeandle.newarray`.

**Pre-conditions**:
1. `array_klass = CB->getArgOperand(0)` reduces via
   `extractKlassConstant` to a non-null array `Klass*`. Else skip.
2. `length = CB->getArgOperand(1)` must be `ConstantInt`. Else skip — the
   entry count must be known at compile time to allocate the `Entries`
   vector and reason about element offsets.
3. Constant length L must satisfy
   `0 <= L <= MaximumEscapeAnalysisArrayLength` (default 32, matching
   Graal). If L < 0 (impossible from valid IR but
   `getSExtValue` could return so for very large unsigned values), skip.
4. Block not Dead, not in irreducible SCC.
5. Unwind successor not a Windows EH funclet pad.

**Actions on success**.
- Allocate `VirtualObject` with `Kind=Array`, `ArrayKlass`,
  `ArrayLengthVal = ConstantInt of L`, `AllocationCall=CB`.
- Determine the element LLVM type via
  `jeandle::JavaType::elementTypeFor(ArrayKlass)`. For reference arrays,
  `ptr addrspace(1)`; for primitive arrays, the appropriate primitive
  type.
- Pre-allocate `Entries` with L+1 slots: L data slots
  (`FieldValue::unknown()`) plus one reserved slot at
  `arrayOopDesc::length_offset_in_bytes` whose initial value is the
  constant `ArrayLengthVal`. `getOrCreateFieldIndex` special-cases the
  length offset so that any load from it returns the constant length
  without lazy creation.
- Same `addVirtualAlias`, `Effect::EliminateAllocation`,
  `Effect::AddVirtualAlias` as 2.3.1.
- Same `InvokeInst` exception-edge handling as 2.3.1.

**Type identity for arrays**. Java reference array element type identity
is preserved by `ArrayKlass` (the array class). Element class is
`ArrayKlass->element_klass()` accessible via VMCallbacks.

**`jeandle.array_store_check` interaction**: stores of a reference into a
virtual reference array are preceded by an `@jeandle.array_store_check`
call (which only checks compatibility, does not publish). Handled in
2.3.14 — non-escaping; its `i1` result must be folded if both array
klass and stored value klass are statically known.

#### 2.3.3 Store: `store [atomic|volatile] %val, ptr addrspace(N) %ptr`

This rule covers every variant of `StoreInst`. `StoreInst` API:
- `isVolatile()` — true for `volatile`.
- `isAtomic()` — true if any AtomicOrdering other than NotAtomic.
- `getOrdering()` — Unordered / Monotonic / Acquire / Release / AcqRel
  / SequentiallyConsistent.
- `getSyncScopeID()` — system, single-thread, or target-defined scope.

Jeandle's frontend currently emits ONLY `Unordered` (plain Java fields)
or `SequentiallyConsistent` (volatile fields), but PEA must handle all
orderings because later passes may strengthen/weaken orderings, and
inlined intrinsics may introduce them.

**Resolution of the store target**.
```cpp
Value *Ptr = SI->getPointerOperand();
```
