```
        materializedPtrPHI = createPHI(ptr addrspace(1), MergeBB)
        record Effect::CreatePHI(materializedPtrPHI, MergeBB, ptr addrspace(1))
              in MergeEffects[MergeBB]

        for i in 0 .. alive.size()-1:
          predBB = PredBBs[stateIndexes[i]]
          obj = alive[i].getObjectState(objID)
          if obj.isVirtual():
            if !ensureVirtual:
              obj.EnsureVirtualized = false     // downgrade
            materialized |= ensureMaterialized(alive[i], objID,
                                               predBB, predBB->getTerminator(),
                                               BlockEffects[predBB])
            obj = alive[i].getObjectState(objID)  // re-fetch — now materialized
          record Effect::SetPHIInput(materializedPtrPHI,
                                     stateIndexes[i],
                                     obj.MaterializedPtr)
                in AfterMergeEffects[MergeBB]

        NewState.addObject(objID, ObjectState::materialized(
                                     materializedPtrPHI,
                                     LockCount = 0,
                                     EnsureVirtualized = false))

      // --- Phase 1d: re-virtualize across materialized fields ---
      // See Section 2.4.1.
      updateNewStateForMaterialized(NewState)

    // --- Phase 2: Process explicit PHI nodes in MergeBB ---
    for phi in MergeBB.phis():
      AliasMap.resetAlias(phi)    // clear stale alias (essential for loop re-iter)
      if AliasMap.hasVirtualInputs(phi):
        materialized |= processPhi(phi, alive, stateIndexes)
        // processPhi follows Section 2.3.19 case logic.

    // --- Phase 3: Stabilization ---
    if materialized:
      NewState.resetObjectStates(/*size=*/ globalVirtualObjectCount)
      MergeEffects[MergeBB].clear()
      AfterMergeEffects[MergeBB].clear()
      // BlockEffects[predBB] effects from materialization are PRESERVED:
      // they reflect real side-effects in the predecessor blocks that
      // happened regardless of whether the merge succeeds this iteration.
      // The same materialization will not be re-recorded next iteration
      // because the object state in alive[i] is now Materialized, so
      // ensureMaterialized returns false on its next call.

  } while (materialized)

  // --- Phase 4: Commit ---
  BlockEffects[MergeBB].append(MergeEffects[MergeBB])
  BlockEffects[MergeBB].append(AfterMergeEffects[MergeBB])
  return NewState
```

**Termination argument.** Each iteration of the do-while loop is bounded by the total number of virtual objects, because every iteration where `materialized == true` strictly reduces the number of virtual ObjectStates across all `alive[i]` (each materialization flips one `Kind` from `Virtual` to `Materialized` in some predecessor's state, and once `Materialized`, it stays `Materialized` for the remainder of the merge). The loop terminates in at most `O(|alive| * |VirtualObjects|)` iterations. In practice it terminates in 1–3 iterations.

**Oscillation impossibility.** Materialization is monotonic in the partial order `Virtual > Materialized`. The merge's intermediate decisions (e.g., creating a field PHI) are *retracted* (via the clear of `MergeEffects` and `AfterMergeEffects`), but the **state** (the `Kind` of each ObjectState in `alive[i]`) only moves downward. So there is no oscillation.

**Defensive bound.** Implementations should still cap the loop at, e.g., `2 * |VirtualObjects| + 8` iterations and assert if exceeded. This catches bugs (e.g., a buggy `ensureMaterialized` returning `true` after the object is already materialized) without silently looping forever.

#### 2.4.1 Phase ordering: tightly handle re-virtualization across merges

When a merge materializes object `B` in predecessor `i` (i.e., `alive[i].getObjectState(B).Kind` flips to `Materialized`), any other virtual object `A` in `alive[i]` whose field references `B` as a `FieldValue::VirtualRef(B)` must have that entry updated to `FieldValue::MaterializedRef(B.materializedPtr)`. This is Graal's `updateStatesForMaterialized` (PartialEscapeClosure.java:654–670) and **must run** immediately after every materialization triggered inside the merge loop.

```
updateStatesForMaterialized(state, materializedObjID, materializedPtr):
  changed = false
  for objID in 0 .. state.stateCount-1:
    objState = state.getObjectStateOptional(objID)
    if !objState || !objState.isVirtual(): continue
    for fieldIdx in 0 .. objState.Entries.size()-1:
      fv = objState.Entries[fieldIdx]
      if fv.isVirtualRef() && fv.RefID == materializedObjID:
        // Convert in place. COW-protect first.
        getObjectStateForModification(state, objID).Entries[fieldIdx]
            = FieldValue::materializedRef(materializedPtr)
        changed = true
  return changed
```

**Where this runs in the merge.** Inside `ensureMaterialized` (called from Phase 1c.v and from `mergeObjectStates`), the materialized state's entry is updated via the analogous `PartialEscapeBlockState::materializeBefore` call, which in Graal invokes `escape()` and then `updateStatesForMaterialized` on the per-predecessor state. In Jeandle, each call to `materialize(objID, ...)` ends with:

```
state.getObjectState(objID).materialize(materializedPtr)   // flip to Materialized
updateStatesForMaterialized(state, objID, materializedPtr) // propagate
```

This runs **for the predecessor state** (`alive[i]`), not the merged `NewState`. The merge loop's next iteration re-reads `alive[i]`'s entries when running `mergeObjectStates`, picking up the converted `MaterializedRef` automatically.

**Cascading depth.** If `A.f = VirtualRef(B)` and `C.g = VirtualRef(A)`, and `B` is materialized first:

1. `updateStatesForMaterialized(state, B, B_ptr)` flips `A.f` to `MaterializedRef(B_ptr)`. `A` is **not** materialized as a result — `A`'s field merely got a materialized value, which is perfectly fine while `A` itself remains virtual.
2. `C.g` is untouched (it points to `A`, which is still virtual).

So propagation is one-level per call. `A` and `C` do not need to be materialized just because `B` was. They only get materialized if *they themselves* are escape-triggered by a separate use.

**Why this must be inside the do-while.** If we updated only after the merge loop terminated, a field PHI created by `mergeObjectStates` in iteration K could reference a `VirtualRef(B)` that becomes a `MaterializedRef(B_ptr)` in iteration K+1 — but the iteration K's PHI input would already have been recorded with the stale `VirtualRef`. By updating inside each materialization call (which runs inside the do-while), the predecessor states are always consistent before the next iteration starts.

#### 2.4.2 mergeObjectStates — Merging Field Values for Virtual Objects

Called from Phase 1c.iii (all-virtual case) and from Case C of Section 2.3.19 (PHI virtualization). Produces field PHIs for entries that differ across predecessors, and returns `materialized` (true if any field-level materialization happened).

```
mergeObjectStates(resultObjID, sourceObjs, alive[N]) -> bool:
  // sourceObjs[i] is the per-predecessor ObjectID being merged into resultObjID.
  // For the Phase 1c.iii caller, sourceObjs == null which means "use resultObjID
  // for all predecessors" (Graal's IntUnaryOperator at line 1105).
  getSrcObj = (i) -> sourceObjs == null ? resultObjID : sourceObjs[i]

  resultVO     = VirtualObjects[resultObjID]
  entryCount   = resultVO.Fields.size()
  ensureVirtual = true
  compatible   = true

  // -- Compatibility pass --
  for i in 0 .. N-1:
    srcState = alive[i].getObjectState(getSrcObj(i))
    ensureVirtual &= srcState.EnsureVirtualized
    for fieldIdx in 0 .. entryCount-1:
      fv = srcState.Entries[fieldIdx]
      expectedTy = resultVO.Fields[fieldIdx].LLVMType
      actualTy   = (fv.isScalar() ? fv.ScalarVal->getType()
                   : fv.isMaterializedRef() ? fv.MaterializedPtr->getType()
                   : fv.isVirtualRef() ? ptr addrspace(1)
                   : nullptr /* Unknown */)
      if actualTy != nullptr && !areCompatibleTypes(expectedTy, actualTy):
        // Type mismatch: e.g., one pred stored i32 to offset O,
        // another stored i64 to the same offset. PEA cannot coerce.
        // Mark all incoming virtual objects for materialization.
        compatible = false
        break

  // -- Compatible: build merged ObjectState with PHIs for differing entries --
  if compatible:
    mergedEntries[entryCount]
    values[entryCount] = alive[0].getObjectState(getSrcObj(0)).Entries
    phiNeeded[entryCount] = {false ...}

    for fieldIdx in 0 .. entryCount-1:
      for i in 1 .. N-1:
        field_i = alive[i].getObjectState(getSrcObj(i)).Entries[fieldIdx]
        if !field_i.isEquivalentTo(values[fieldIdx]):
          phiNeeded[fieldIdx] = true
          break

    materialized = false
    for fieldIdx in 0 .. entryCount-1:
      if !phiNeeded[fieldIdx]:
        mergedEntries[fieldIdx] = values[fieldIdx]
        continue

      // Decide PHI type from the field's declared LLVM type.
      fieldTy = resultVO.Fields[fieldIdx].LLVMType
      isReference = resultVO.Fields[fieldIdx].IsReference

      if isReference:
        // mergeObjectEntry (Graal lines 1340–1365)
        fieldPHI = createPHI(ptr addrspace(1), MergeBB)
        record Effect::CreatePHI(fieldPHI, MergeBB, ptr addrspace(1))
              in MergeEffects[MergeBB]

        for i in 0 .. N-1:
          srcObjID = getSrcObj(i)
          if srcObjID == InvalidObjectID:
            // Self-reference slot (Phi virtualization case); use the PHI
            // itself as input on that edge (Graal line 1345).
            record Effect::SetPHIInput(fieldPHI, stateIndexes[i], fieldPHI)
                  in AfterMergeEffects[MergeBB]
            continue
          srcState = alive[i].getObjectState(srcObjID)
          if !srcState.isVirtual():
            // Intermediate materialization invalidated the merge —
            // bail out, return materialized=true so the outer loop retries.
            return true
          entry = srcState.Entries[fieldIdx]
          if entry.isVirtualRef():
            nestedID = entry.RefID
            predBB = PredBBs[stateIndexes[i]]
            // Materialize the nested virtual object in the predecessor.
            materialized |= ensureMaterialized(alive[i], nestedID,
                                               predBB, predBB->getTerminator(),
                                               BlockEffects[predBB])
            srcState = alive[i].getObjectState(srcObjID)  // re-fetch
            if srcState.isVirtual():
              // Update the predecessor's entry to point to the materialized ptr.
              newEntry = FieldValue::materializedRef(
                            alive[i].getObjectState(nestedID).MaterializedPtr)
              alive[i].setEntry(srcObjID, fieldIdx, newEntry)
              entry = newEntry
          // Resolve to a concrete LLVM Value*
          inputVal = (entry.isScalar() ? entry.ScalarVal
                     : entry.isMaterializedRef() ? entry.MaterializedPtr
                     : /* Unknown */ ConstantPointerNull::get(ptr addrspace(1)))
          record Effect::SetPHIInput(fieldPHI, stateIndexes[i], inputVal)
                in AfterMergeEffects[MergeBB]

        mergedEntries[fieldIdx] = FieldValue::materializedRef(fieldPHI)
      else:
        // Primitive field: simpler — no recursion into nested virtuals.
        fieldPHI = createPHI(fieldTy, MergeBB)
        record Effect::CreatePHI(fieldPHI, MergeBB, fieldTy)
              in MergeEffects[MergeBB]

        for i in 0 .. N-1:
          srcObjID = getSrcObj(i)
          srcState = alive[i].getObjectState(srcObjID)
          if !srcState.isVirtual():
            return true   // bail; outer loop will retry
          entry = srcState.Entries[fieldIdx]
          inputVal = (entry.isScalar() ? entry.ScalarVal
                     : /* Unknown */ Constant::getNullValue(fieldTy))
          record Effect::SetPHIInput(fieldPHI, stateIndexes[i], inputVal)
                in AfterMergeEffects[MergeBB]

        mergedEntries[fieldIdx] = FieldValue::scalar(fieldPHI)

    NewState.addObject(resultObjID, ObjectState::virtual(
                                       mergedEntries,
                                       LockCount = alive[0].getObjectState(getSrcObj(0)).LockCount,
                                       EnsureVirtualized = ensureVirtual))
    return materialized

  // -- Incompatible: materialize every source object in its predecessor --
  materializedPtrPHI = createPHI(ptr addrspace(1), MergeBB)
  record Effect::CreatePHI(materializedPtrPHI, MergeBB, ptr addrspace(1))
        in MergeEffects[MergeBB]
  for i in 0 .. N-1:
    srcObjID = getSrcObj(i)
    if srcObjID == InvalidObjectID:
      record Effect::SetPHIInput(materializedPtrPHI, stateIndexes[i], materializedPtrPHI)
            in AfterMergeEffects[MergeBB]
      continue
    if !ensureVirtual && alive[i].getObjectState(srcObjID).isVirtual():
      alive[i].getObjectState(srcObjID).EnsureVirtualized = false
    predBB = PredBBs[stateIndexes[i]]
    ensureMaterialized(alive[i], srcObjID, predBB, predBB->getTerminator(),
                       BlockEffects[predBB])
    record Effect::SetPHIInput(materializedPtrPHI, stateIndexes[i],
                               alive[i].getObjectState(srcObjID).MaterializedPtr)
          in AfterMergeEffects[MergeBB]

  NewState.addObject(resultObjID, ObjectState::materialized(
                                     materializedPtrPHI,
                                     LockCount = 0,
                                     EnsureVirtualized = ensureVirtual))
  return true
```

**`areCompatibleTypes` semantics.** Two LLVM types are compatible iff they are:
- Identical; OR
- Both pointer types in the same address space; OR
- Both primitive of the same bit width and same signedness category (we treat all integer types of the same width as compatible because LLVM IR is type-erased w.r.t. signedness); OR
- Both `float` (f32) or both `double` (f64).

Mismatched widths (e.g., i32 vs i64 written to the same field offset) are **incompatible** in Jeandle's model. The merge falls back to materialization.

**Unknown vs Scalar.** When one predecessor's field is `Unknown` (never written) and another's is `Scalar(V)`, the merged PHI sees the *default zero/null* on the Unknown side and `V` on the Scalar side. The implementation materializes the zero/null as `Constant::getNullValue(fieldTy)` and uses it as the PHI input from that predecessor.

**Lock-count mismatch.** Phase 1c.ii already filtered this case (`locksMatch == false` ⇒ does not enter Phase 1c.iii). If `mergeObjectStates` is called from PHI virtualization (Section 2.3.19 Case C), lock equality is already a precondition.

**EnsureVirtualized propagation.** `ensureVirtual &= obj.EnsureVirtualized` over all predecessors. The merged ObjectState's `EnsureVirtualized` is true iff all predecessors agree it must stay virtual.

**Two-slot kinds and byte-array slicing.** Graal handles 64-bit values stored across two 32-bit slots and byte arrays with multi-byte stores. Jeandle does not stripe values across slots — every field is one LLVM type at one offset. The two-slot logic from PartialEscapeClosure.java:1117–1254 is **omitted** in Jeandle's port. If a future Jeandle change introduces unaligned multi-byte writes, this section must be extended.

#### 2.4.3 PHI input ordering

LLVM PHI nodes are ordered by `(incoming value, incoming block)` pairs in the order they were added. PEA must add inputs in the same order as `MergeBB->predecessors()` to keep the resulting PHI's input slots aligned with subsequent passes that index by predecessor.

The implementation uses `MergeBB->getPredecessor(idx)` for indexing and iterates `0 .. MergeBB->getPredecessorCount()-1`. The `alive` array's `stateIndexes[]` maps from the alive-index back to the predecessor-index so that `phi->setIncomingBlock(stateIndexes[i], ...)` is used in `Effect::SetPHIInput`.

For dead predecessors (filtered out in Phase 0), the PHI input at that slot is left at its original value (`UndefValue` or whatever the IR carries). Pass 2 of the transform may simplify those slots away during dead-code cleanup.

If a predecessor appears multiple times in `MergeBB->predecessors()` (e.g., a `switch` with two arms branching to the same block), each appearance is a separate slot. PEA's `alive` deduplication is **per slot**, not per block: identical incoming states from the same physical predecessor still occupy distinct slots, and `Effect::SetPHIInput` is recorded for each slot independently. The merge is over `N` slots, not over `N` unique predecessor blocks.

#### 2.4.4 Critical edges

A critical edge is a CFG edge `(U, V)` where `U` has multiple successors and `V` has multiple predecessors. PEA records `Effect::Materialize` with `Block = U` and `InsertBefore = U->getTerminator()`, which inserts the allocation code into `U` immediately before its terminator.

**Why this is safe without edge splitting.** The materialization in `U` happens on *every* execution of `U`, regardless of which successor is taken. Hoisting the allocation up to all of `U`'s executions is conservative — it costs one extra allocation along the other-successor path. But:
- If the other successor also uses the same virtual object's materialized pointer (joins downstream), we win twice.
- If the other successor does not use it, we pay one extra allocation — measurable but bounded.

This conservatism is identical to Graal's behavior, where `materializeBefore` is called with `predecessor.getEndNode()` and the materialization is inserted unconditionally in the predecessor block.

**Why edge splitting is NOT required.** Splitting a critical edge `(U, V)` would create a new block `M`, redirecting `U`'s branch to `M` and `M`'s branch to `V`. The materialization could then be placed in `M`, executing only when the U→V edge is taken. PEA does **not** do this, because:
1. It mutates the CFG before the transform pass runs, violating the separation of analysis and transform.
2. PHI input indexes in `V` would need adjustment (M replaces U in the PHI input list), which is bookkeeping noise.
3. LLVM's `BreakCriticalEdges` is a separate concern; if it's enabled upstream of PEA, PEA benefits automatically. If not, the conservative cost is acceptable.

**Insertion-point pitfall.** If `U`'s terminator is an `InvokeInst` (call-with-exception), inserting *before* the invoke is correct: the materialization runs unconditionally, then the invoke fires. The merge block `V` will be either the invoke's normal or unwind successor; either way, the materialized pointer dominates `V`. If `V` happens to be the unwind successor, the materialization still happened before the call, so its result is valid in `V`.

**PHI input preservation under predecessor materialization.** Inserting allocation code at the end of `U` does **not** require re-numbering PHI inputs at `V`. The PHI's incoming-block list still names `U`; the materialized pointer is a new SSA value that becomes the PHI's incoming-value at the `U` slot via `Effect::SetPHIInput(phi, stateIndexes_for_U, materializedPtr)`. The original IR value (the allocation invoke's result, or a non-virtual value) at that slot is *replaced*, not deleted; no slot shift occurs.

#### 2.4.5 Special-shape merge blocks

- **Self-loop merge** (merge block where one predecessor is itself): handled like any back-edge merge inside `processLoop`. The merge block's own exit state is fed back as a predecessor state in the next iteration.
- **Empty merge block** (only the terminator): no instructions to process beyond merging predecessor states. The PHI processing pass is a no-op (no PHIs to walk).
- **Merge block with only PHIs and a terminator**: the standard path applies. PHI processing handles every PHI.
- **Merge block reachable only from the entry**: not a merge in the multi-pred sense; treated as straight-line (single-pred fast path in Phase 0).
- **Merge block with a `landingpad`**: a landingpad is the first non-PHI instruction of an unwind successor. The unwind block may have multiple predecessor invokes (rare in Jeandle but legal). The merge processor treats it like any merge: filter dead preds, merge object states, process PHIs. The landingpad itself is not virtualizable; PEA leaves it untouched.


### 2.5 Loop Handling

Loops are the most delicate part of partial escape analysis. Unlike straight-line code, where a single forward pass over the dominator-ordered blocks suffices, a loop forces the analyzer to reason about a fixed point: the state that flows out of the back edges must agree with the state assumed at the loop header. Any disagreement triggers another iteration with a more conservative starting state, and any effect recorded during a non-final iteration must be erased before the next pass — otherwise the transform phase will commit decisions that were made under stale assumptions.

The Jeandle implementation follows Graal's `EffectsClosure.processLoop` (lines 366–566 of `EffectsClosure.java`) and `PartialEscapeClosure.processLoopExit` (lines 738–799 of `PartialEscapeClosure.java`) closely. The sub-sections below describe the algorithm in execution order, then cover the escalation modes, the rollback machinery, the loop-exit proxy creation, and a small widening heuristic that bounds iteration count on common patterns.

### 2.5.1 Loop Discovery and Prerequisites

PEA runs on the LLVM IR after the LLVM CFG has been built but before the standard mid-level pipeline. Loops are discovered with LLVM's `LoopInfo` analysis, which is requested as a prerequisite on the PEA pass. Dominator information is required throughout (see Section 2.4) so `DominatorTree` is requested as well. Both are preserved when PEA finishes, because subsequent passes in the Jeandle pipeline depend on them.

```cpp
// In JeandlePartialEscapeAnalysis::getAnalysisUsage:
AU.addRequired<LoopInfoWrapperPass>();
AU.addRequired<DominatorTreeWrapperPass>();
AU.addPreserved<LoopInfoWrapperPass>();
AU.addPreserved<DominatorTreeWrapperPass>();
```

Each natural loop the closure sees is processed in inner-to-outer order during a single block-iterator traversal of the function. The outer iterator (an analogue of Graal's `ReentrantBlockIterator`) calls into `processLoop(L, initialState)` whenever it encounters a loop header on its main RPO walk; the recursive call returns one exit state per `LoopExitBlock` (the unique successor of a back-edge-target predecessor that leaves the loop) and the outer iterator resumes with those states.

**Design note — choice of loop representation**: LLVM `Loop` objects identify natural loops only. Irreducible regions are not given a header by `LoopInfo`. PEA refuses to virtualize allocations whose live range crosses an irreducible region (it materializes any virtual entries the moment the iterator reaches an unrecognized merge), so the algorithm below need only handle natural loops. The check is `LI.getLoopFor(BB) != nullptr` for any BB the iterator visits; if a merge has predecessors from outside the loop it currently believes itself in, the merge is treated as an irreducible merge and all virtual objects entering it are materialized.

### 2.5.2 Forward-Edge State Computation and Header Setup

Before iteration begins, the analyzer needs an initial state for the loop header that contains **only** information coming from forward edges (the loop pre-header). Back-edge information is what the iteration computes; if it were folded in at the start, the iteration would be circular.

```cpp
mergeForwardEdgeStates(Loop *L, const DominatorTree &DT):
  // The forward predecessor is unique for a natural loop: the loop pre-header.
  // (LLVM's loop simplification pass ensures every loop has exactly one
  //  pre-header. Jeandle runs LoopSimplify before PEA to make this invariant
  //  hold.)
  BasicBlock *PreHeader = L->getLoopPreheader();
  assert(PreHeader && "PEA requires simplified loops");
  return cloneState(BlockExitStates[PreHeader]);
```

The state returned here is the **initial loop state** that the rest of `processLoop` operates on. Graal calls the same value `initialState` (parameter to `EffectsClosure.processLoop` at line 366) and, after stripping locations killed by writes inside the loop, `initialStateRemovedKilledLocations` (line 397). Jeandle does not strip killed locations from the entry state — the analysis is already field-sensitive at the LLVM SSA level, so there is no separate read-cache that needs invalidating.

Once the initial state is in hand, two pre-iteration steps run:

1. **`processInitialLoopState`** (Graal `PartialEscapeClosure.java:723`). For each LLVM PHI at the loop header that has a forward-edge incoming value already known to alias a virtual object, install the PHI as an alias of the same virtual object. This keeps subsequent uses of the PHI from being treated as escape points; without it, the very first use inside the loop body would materialize the object.

   ```cpp
   processInitialLoopState(Loop *L, BlockState &initialState):
     BasicBlock *Header = L->getHeader();
     for (PHINode &Phi : Header->phis()):
       Value *ForwardIn = Phi.getIncomingValueForBlock(L->getLoopPreheader());
       Value *Alias = resolveAlias(initialState, ForwardIn);
       if (auto *VRef = dyn_cast<VirtualRef>(Alias)):
         AliasMap[&Phi] = VRef;
       else:
         AliasMap[&Phi] = nullptr;  // clear any stale alias
   ```

2. **`propagateEnsureVirtualized`** (Graal `PartialEscapeClosure.java:686–714`). The `EnsureVirtualized` flag is transitive: if object A is marked ensure-virtualized and one of its field entries references object B, then B must also stay virtual, otherwise materializing A would force B's materialization too. A fixed-point loop walks the initial state, propagating the flag until no further changes happen. This step is run only when the depth threshold has been crossed (see Section 2.5.7).

### 2.5.3 The Iteration Loop

The core of `processLoop` is a bounded `while` loop (Graal sets the bound to 10 via the literal `iteration < 10` at line 458 of `EffectsClosure.java`). At a high level the iteration:

1. Snapshots all per-function analysis state for rollback (only at outermost loop with deep-nest potential).
2. Escalates to `STOP_NEW_VIRTUALIZATIONS` if depth exceeds the cutoff.
3. Computes the initial loop entry state from the pre-header.
4. Runs up to `MAX_ITERATIONS` body walks, each followed by a header merge of the forward-edge state with the back-edge end states. After each merge:
   - If the merged state is equivalent to the previous iteration's result, commit effects and process loop exits.
   - Otherwise, discard the iteration's effects, widen `lastMergedState`, and repeat.
5. On `OverflowException` at the outermost loop, rolls back state and re-processes the loop in `MATERIALIZE_ALL` mode.
6. On `MAX_ITERATIONS` reached the first time, also escalates to `MATERIALIZE_ALL`. A second `MAX_ITERATIONS` failure is a fatal error.

The detailed pseudocode mirrors Graal's `EffectsClosure.processLoop`:

```cpp
processLoop(Loop *L, BlockState &initialState):
  // --- 1. Snapshot all per-function analysis state for rollback. ---
  bool MayOverflow = anyLoopDeeperThan(LoopCutoffDepth);
  Snapshot SS;
  if (MayOverflow && L->getLoopDepth() == 1):
    SS = takeSnapshot(initialState);

  // --- 2. Depth-based mode escalation. ---
  if (L->getLoopDepth() > LoopCutoffDepth /* default 20 */):
    currentMode = STOP_NEW_VIRTUALIZATIONS;
    propagateEnsureVirtualized(initialState);

  // --- 3. Pre-loop hooks. ---
  processInitialLoopState(L, initialState);

  BlockState lastMergedState = cloneState(initialState);
  bool tooManyIterationsSeen = false;
  bool[] knownAliveLoopEnds(L->getNumBackEdges(), false);

  // --- 4. The iteration / convergence loop. ---
  while (true):                          // outer retry on overflow
    try:
      for (int iter = 0; iter < MAX_ITERATIONS; ++iter):
        // 4a. Process the body once.
        LoopInfo bodyInfo = walkLoopBody(L, cloneState(lastMergedState));

        // 4b. Merge the forward-edge initial state with every back-edge
        //     end state at the loop header.
        mergeProcessor.merge(concat(initialState, bodyInfo.endStates));

        // 4c. Monotonicity check (Graal lines 500–510).
        for (size_t e = 0; e < bodyInfo.endStates.size(); ++e):
          assert(!(knownAliveLoopEnds[e] && bodyInfo.endStates[e].isDead()));
          knownAliveLoopEnds[e] |= !bodyInfo.endStates[e].isDead();

        // 4d. Convergence test.
        if (mergeProcessor.newState.equivalentTo(lastMergedState)):
          // CONVERGED — commit and return.
          commitConvergedIteration(L, mergeProcessor, bodyInfo, initialState);
          if (currentMode != REGULAR_VIRTUALIZATION && L->getLoopDepth() == 1):
            currentMode = REGULAR_VIRTUALIZATION;
          return bodyInfo.exitStates;

        // 4e. Not converged — discard, widen, retry.
        discardLoopEffects(L);
        lastMergedState = mergeProcessor.newState;
    catch (OverflowException &):
      if (L->getLoopDepth() != 1) throw;     // bubble out to outermost loop
      rollback(SS);
      materializeAllVirtual(SS.InitialState, L->getLoopPreheader(),
                            BlockEffects[L->getLoopPreheader()]);
      initialState = SS.InitialState;
      lastMergedState = cloneState(initialState);
      currentMode = MATERIALIZE_ALL;
      continue;

    // Fell through MAX_ITERATIONS without converging. Try MATERIALIZE_ALL once.
    if (!tooManyIterationsSeen):
      tooManyIterationsSeen = true;
      discardLoopEffects(L);
      currentMode = MATERIALIZE_ALL;
      continue;
    report_fatal_error("PEA: too many iterations in loop");
```

**Design note — why merge across iterations uses the same `MergeProcessor`**: The merge processor caches PHIs it has already created for a given merge block (Graal's `materializedPhis`, `valuePhis`, `valueObjectVirtuals` fields, `PartialEscapeClosure.java:808–810`). Iteration N+1 reuses cached PHIs rather than producing fresh ones, which both avoids leaks and lets the `equivalentTo` check terminate (a PHI introduced in iteration K appears unchanged in iteration K+1 if no new operand is needed). The cache is gated on `needsCaching = mergeBlock.isLoopHeader()` (Graal line 816).

**Design note — `equivalentTo`, not `==`**: Two `BlockState` instances are equivalent when, for every ObjectID, both states agree on `Virtual` vs `Materialized`, on lock count, and on each entry slot. Reference-typed entries are compared by VirtualRef identity (same RefID) or by underlying `Value*`; primitive-typed entries are compared by `Value*` only. PHIs synthesized at the loop header by the previous merge are compared by identity, so a PHI introduced in iteration K appears unchanged in iteration K+1 if no new operand was needed. Convergence is reached as soon as the merge stops producing new PHIs.

### 2.5.4 Walking the Loop Body

`walkLoopBody` is a guarded RPO traversal restricted to `L->getBlocks()`. Subtleties:

* **Header is never re-processed inside the body walk.** The header block's instructions are processed once per iteration *as part of the body* (with the entry state == `lastMergedState`). The convergence merge that produces `mergeProcessor.newState` is a separate operation that happens after the body walk completes.
* **Inner merges still use the regular merge algorithm from Section 2.4.** That algorithm itself iterates to a fixed point when materializations cause new materializations (the `do { materialized = ... } while (materialized);` outer loop), so an inner merge can in principle materialize objects mid-iteration. Any such materialization is recorded in `BlockEffects[predecessor]`, which is what the discard step at 4e clears.
* **Exit states are computed without materializing virtual objects** — `processLoopExit` is what decides per-object whether to materialize, install a PHI proxy, or do nothing.

### 2.5.5 Effect Discarding

Discarding effects across non-converged iterations is the single most important correctness property of the algorithm:

```cpp
for (auto *B : L->getBlocks()):
  BlockEffects[B].clear();           // all per-block effects (loads, stores,
                                     //   replacements, materializations,
                                     //   PHIs created mid-iteration)
  if (B == L->getHeader()):
    MergeEffects[B].clear();
    LoopMergeEffects[L].clear();
```

Note the asymmetry: `BlockEffects` is cleared for *every* block in the loop, but `MergeEffects` and `LoopMergeEffects` are cleared only at the header. Non-header merges live entirely inside the body walk; their effects already sit in `BlockEffects[predecessor]` and are erased by the per-block clear. The loop header is the only block whose merge effects are split across two stores (`MergeEffects[header]` for pre-merge insertions, `LoopMergeEffects[L]` for after-merge insertions) because those effects are committed only once the loop converges.

**Design note — why effects, not the state, are discarded**: After step 4e, the merge processor's `newState` (which has at least as many PHIs as `lastMergedState`) becomes the new `lastMergedState`. The state widens monotonically; the *effects*, however, are recomputed from scratch in iteration K+1 because they may now reference different values, different PHIs, or different materialization sites. Discarding effects but keeping the widened state is what makes the fixed point reachable in a bounded number of iterations: each iteration either widens the state strictly (adding a PHI or transitioning Virtual → Materialized), or terminates. Strict widening is bounded because there are finitely many ObjectIDs and finitely many fields per object.

**Design note — PHIs that were created in the IR**: Effects describe IR changes but do not perform them — the transform pass at the end of analysis is what touches the IR. Clearing `BlockEffects` means those changes were never committed, so there is nothing to undo. The PHIs created by `MergeProcessor` live inside its caches and the merge processor instance is **kept across iterations** (Graal `EffectsClosure.java:447`), so a PHI produced in iteration K is still available in iteration K+1; if iteration K+1 produces the same set of incoming values, the cache returns the same PHI and the merged state continues to reference it. This is what allows `equivalentTo` to return true once the iteration stabilizes.

### 2.5.6 Loop Exit Processing

After convergence, each loop exit needs its own pass to insert SSA proxies (LLVM PHI nodes in single-incoming form) for values whose definitions are inside the loop body but whose uses are outside it. Graal's `PartialEscapeClosure.processLoopExit` (lines 738–766) splits this into two sub-cases:

#### Virtual at loop exit (`processVirtualAtLoopExit`, Graal line 787)

An object that is still virtual at the exit need not be materialized — virtuality is preserved across the loop boundary. However, its *entries* may reference values that are only defined inside the loop, in which case those values must be wrapped in a proxy PHI at the exit so that subsequent uses dominate properly.

```cpp
processVirtualAtLoopExit(ExitBlock, ObjectID, exitObj, entryObj,
                         exitState, Effects):
  BasicBlock *LoopBodyPred = ExitBlock->getSinglePredecessor();
  for (int field = 0; field < exitObj.numFields(); ++field):
    Value *exitV = exitState.getEntry(ObjectID, field);

    // Skip loop-invariant or virtual-identity entries.
    if (isa<Constant>(exitV)) continue;
    if (isa<VirtualRef>(exitV)) continue;

    // Need a proxy PHI if any of these conditions hold:
    //   (1) exitV is a PHI at the loop header (value varies per iteration)
    //   (2) the object did not exist at loop entry (created inside the loop)
    //   (3) the object was materialized at loop entry (different shape)
    //   (4) the entry value of this field differs from the exit value
    if (isPhiAtHeader(exitV, L->getHeader())
        || entryObj == nullptr
        || !entryObj->isVirtual()
        || entryObj->getEntry(field) != exitV):
      Effects.append(CreateLCSSAProxy{
        .Block       = ExitBlock,
        .Type        = exitV->getType(),
        .IncomingVal = exitV,
        .IncomingBB  = LoopBodyPred,
      });
      exitState.setEntry(ObjectID, field, /* placeholder for proxy */);
```

#### Materialized at loop exit (`processMaterializedAtLoopExit`, Graal line 768)

An object that was virtual at loop entry but became materialized inside the loop body has a `MaterializedPtr` that is only defined inside the loop:

```cpp
processMaterializedAtLoopExit(ExitBlock, ObjectID, exitObj, entryObj,
                              exitState, Effects):
  Value *exitPtr = exitObj.MaterializedPtr;
  // Create a proxy PHI if any of:
  //   (a) the object did not exist at loop entry
  //   (b) the object was virtual at loop entry
  //   (c) the materialized pointer changed across the loop
  if (entryObj == nullptr
      || entryObj->isVirtual()
      || entryObj->MaterializedPtr != exitPtr):
    Effects.append(CreateLCSSAProxy{
      .Block       = ExitBlock,
      .Type        = exitPtr->getType(),
      .IncomingVal = exitPtr,
      .IncomingBB  = ExitBlock->getSinglePredecessor(),
    });
    exitState.updateMaterializedValue(ObjectID, /* placeholder */);
```

**Design note — relationship to LCSSA**: LLVM has an LCSSA form maintained by `LCSSAPass`, guaranteeing that every value defined inside a loop and used outside has a one-incoming PHI in each exit block. PEA effectively re-implements LCSSA construction for the values it introduces (materialized pointers, rewritten field entries) because those values do not exist in the IR until the transform pass runs and so cannot be processed by `LCSSAPass` ahead of time. After PEA's transform pass completes, the function is re-LCSSAed if any downstream pass requires it; PEA's effect-driven PHI insertions are LCSSA-compatible by construction.

**Design note — exception-handling BCIs**: Graal includes a `forceMaterialization` check (`PartialEscapeClosure.java:742`) for exits whose `stateAfter` is an exception-handling BCI, because deoptimization through such an exit would reconstruct the object in interpreter frames with invalid state. Jeandle has no deoptimization (out of scope), so this special case does not apply.

### 2.5.7 Escalation Modes

The analyzer carries a single `currentMode` enum that gates the behavior of `virtualize()` and `ensureMaterialized()`. The modes mirror Graal's `EffectsClosureMode`:

```cpp
enum class EffectsClosureMode {
  REGULAR_VIRTUALIZATION,    // default
  STOP_NEW_VIRTUALIZATIONS,  // a.k.a. STOP_NEW_VIRTUALIZATIONS_LOOP_NEST in Graal
  MATERIALIZE_ALL,
};
```

**REGULAR_VIRTUALIZATION** — the default. Allocations are virtualized, escapes materialize, loops iterate to convergence. This is the only mode in which new VirtualObject IDs are minted.

**STOP_NEW_VIRTUALIZATIONS** — entered whenever `L->getLoopDepth() > LoopCutoffDepth` (default 20). Once in this mode:
* New `VirtualizableAllocation`s are **not** virtualized inside the deeply-nested loop body; their backing allocation is left intact (Graal `PartialEscapeClosure.java:287–303`).
* Existing virtual objects entering from an outer scope are preserved.
* `ensureMaterialized()` on a virtual object:
  * If the object has `EnsureVirtualized == true`, throw `RetryableBailoutException` (Graal line 550). Jeandle's analogue calls into the compiler's bailout path, recording a non-permanent compile-time failure and signaling the caller to retry with PEA disabled.
  * Otherwise throw `OverflowException` (Graal line 562), caught at the outermost loop to trigger `MATERIALIZE_ALL`.

**MATERIALIZE_ALL** — entered as catch-side of `OverflowException` or as second-chance retry after `MAX_ITERATIONS` exhausted:
* `processStateBeforeLoopOnOverflow` (Graal `PartialEscapeClosure.java:230`) materializes every virtual object in the initial loop state at the end of the pre-header.
* Inside the body, every object that is created or passed through is immediately materialized via `ensureMaterialized()`. The loop trivially converges in one iteration.
* On exit from the outermost loop, the mode reverts to `REGULAR_VIRTUALIZATION`.

**Escalation flow**:

```text
REGULAR_VIRTUALIZATION
    │
    ├── inside loop with depth > LoopCutoffDepth ──► STOP_NEW_VIRTUALIZATIONS
    │       │
    │       ├── ensureMaterialized on ensureVirtualized object
    │       │       └─► RetryableBailoutException
    │       │           (compile retried with PEA disabled)
    │       │
    │       └── ensureMaterialized on regular virtual object
    │               └─► OverflowException
    │                   │
    │                   └── caught at outermost loop ──► MATERIALIZE_ALL
    │                                                      │
    │                                                      └── re-process loop
    │                                                          (converges in 1 iter)
    │                                                          ──► REGULAR_VIRTUALIZATION
    │
    ├── MAX_ITERATIONS reached, first time ──► MATERIALIZE_ALL
    │       └── re-process loop ──► REGULAR_VIRTUALIZATION
    │
    └── MAX_ITERATIONS reached, second time ──► report_fatal_error
                                                 (PEA gives up on this function)
```

**Design note — why `RetryableBailoutException` is distinct from `OverflowException`**: An object with `EnsureVirtualized = true` was marked by the front-end as required-virtual; materializing it would silently violate a contract the source program relies on. Switching to `MATERIALIZE_ALL` would materialize it (wrong outcome). The correct response is to discard PEA's work for this function and recompile without PEA — exactly what `RetryableBailoutException` requests. Jeandle implements this by setting a per-compile flag observed by `JeandleCompiler::compile_method`, restarting the compilation with PEA effectively disabled for this method only.

### 2.5.8 Snapshot, Rollback, and the Sense of "All State"

The snapshot/rollback machinery exists because `MATERIALIZE_ALL` re-processes the loop from scratch, but only after analysis state has been written. Re-processing without rolling back would compound stale aliases, stale effects, and stale entry states with the new pass.

Graal captures the following state on entry to the outermost loop (`EffectsClosure.java:419–435`):

| Graal name | Jeandle name | What it holds |
|---|---|---|
| `initialStateRemovedKilledLocationsBackup` | `SS.InitialState` | The loop's initial entry state. |
| `aliasesCopy` | `SS.AliasMap` | `Value*` → `VirtualRef` map. |
| `hasScalarReplacedInputsCopy` | `SS.HasScalarReplacedInputs` | Bit vector marking nodes whose inputs include scalar replacements. |
| `blockEffectsCopy` | `SS.BlockEffects` | Per-block deferred-effect list — deep-copied because the inner list is mutated. |
| `loopMergeEffectsCopy` | `SS.LoopMergeEffects` | Per-loop after-merge effect lists. |
| `loopEntryStatesCopy` | `SS.LoopEntryStates` | Per-loop entry states used by `processLoopExit`. |
| `loopLocationKillCacheCopy` | (not needed) | Memory-kill cache for loops; Jeandle is SSA on LLVM IR and does not maintain a separate read-cache. |

Jeandle additionally snapshots `MergeEffects` and `AfterMergeEffects` for symmetry. `HasVirtualInputs` is **not** snapshotted (matches Graal `EffectsClosure.java:420`); it is reset and recomputed during re-traversal.

**Snapshot scope**: snapshots are taken *only* for the outermost loop (`L->getLoopDepth() == 1`) and *only* if the function actually contains a loop deeper than `LoopCutoffDepth`. The depth check is exactly Graal lines 412–417: walk all loops in the function once and check whether any depth exceeds the cutoff. If no loop is deep enough, no snapshot is taken — the algorithm cannot overflow.

**Rollback** simply assigns the saved values back over the live state:

```cpp
rollback(const Snapshot &SS):
  AliasMap                = SS.AliasMap;
  HasScalarReplacedInputs = SS.HasScalarReplacedInputs;
  HasVirtualInputs.reset();                       // recompute on re-walk
  BlockEffects            = SS.BlockEffects;
  MergeEffects            = SS.MergeEffects;
  AfterMergeEffects       = SS.AfterMergeEffects;
  LoopMergeEffects        = SS.LoopMergeEffects;
  LoopEntryStates         = SS.LoopEntryStates;
```

After rollback, `processStateBeforeLoopOnOverflow` (Section 2.5.9) is invoked to materialize all virtual objects in the rolled-back initial state, then iteration is restarted from the top of the `while (true)` loop with `currentMode = MATERIALIZE_ALL`.

### 2.5.9 `processStateBeforeLoopOnOverflow` and Pre-Loop Materialization

In `MATERIALIZE_ALL` mode, the loop's initial state must contain only materialized objects. The Graal implementation is small (`PartialEscapeClosure.java:230–237`):

```cpp
processStateBeforeLoopOnOverflow(BlockState &initialState,
                                  BasicBlock *PreHeader,
                                  EffectList &Effects):
  for (ObjectID i in initialState.objectIDs()):
    if (initialState.hasObjectState(i)
        && initialState.getObjectState(i).isVirtual()):
      initialState.materializeBefore(
        /*InsertBefore=*/PreHeader->getTerminator(),
        /*Virtual=*/getVirtualForID(i),
        /*StrictLockOrder=*/requiresStrictLockOrder,
        /*VirtualObjects=*/virtualObjects,
        /*Effects=*/Effects);
```

`materializeBefore` is the same routine called from `ensureMaterialized` (Section 2.6); it recursively materializes prerequisite virtual entries first, records an `Effect::Materialize` for each, and transitions every affected `ObjectState` to `Materialized` with the appropriate `MaterializedPtr` sentinel.

**Why the pre-header, not the loop header**: materializations placed at the loop header would be re-executed every iteration; placing them at the end of the pre-header puts them on the unique forward edge into the loop and so they execute exactly once per loop entry. The pre-header terminator is guaranteed to be an unconditional branch by LoopSimplify form, so insertion is trivial.

### 2.5.10 Nested Loops

The algorithm is invoked recursively for nested loops via the body walk: when `walkLoopBody` (Section 2.5.4) visits a header of an inner loop, the iterator calls `processLoop` on that inner loop. The dance described in `EffectsClosure.java:375–395`:

* On the **first iteration** of an outermost loop, the outer body walk enters each inner loop. Each inner loop processes itself with whatever initial state the outer walk computed.
* On subsequent iterations of the outer loop, the outer body walk *again* enters each inner loop. Because the outer state has changed (more PHIs, possibly more materializations), the inner loop sees a different initial state and may need to iterate again. This is what "exponential over loop depth" means in Graal's comment — three nested loops can mean `O(MAX_ITERATIONS^3)` body walks in pathological cases.
* The `STOP_NEW_VIRTUALIZATIONS` mode at depth > cutoff caps inner-loop work.
* Snapshots are taken only at the outermost loop. The overflow exception bubbles out of all nested `processLoop` frames (`throw e` at Graal line 531) and is caught only at the outermost frame, which owns the snapshot. A single rollback restores the state to before the entire loop nest was entered.

**Monotonicity across nested calls** (Graal lines 500–510): the assertion that a loop end seen alive in iteration N must remain alive in iteration N+1 is a within-loop invariant; it does not extend across nested loops. Each `processLoop` call has its own `knownAliveLoopEnds` array sized for *that* loop's back edges.

### 2.5.11 Field Instability Widening Heuristic

The strict algorithm above guarantees termination via `MAX_ITERATIONS = 10` plus the two-strike `MATERIALIZE_ALL` escalation. In practice, the common cause of non-convergence is a single field whose value depends on the iteration count (e.g. `obj.counter = obj.counter + 1`). The merge processor's PHI cache usually handles this automatically. To avoid pathological cases, Jeandle adds a per-field instability counter on top of Graal's algorithm:

```cpp
struct FieldInstabilityTracker {
  DenseMap<std::pair<ObjectID, int>, unsigned> ChangeCount;

  void recordFieldChange(ObjectID Obj, int FieldIdx) { ++ChangeCount[{Obj, FieldIdx}]; }
  bool shouldMaterialize(ObjectID Obj, int FieldIdx, unsigned Threshold = 3) const {
    auto It = ChangeCount.find({Obj, FieldIdx});
    return It != ChangeCount.end() && It->second >= Threshold;
  }
  void resetForLoop() { ChangeCount.clear(); }
};
```

The tracker is reset at the start of each `processLoop` invocation and updated during the convergence check. When `shouldMaterialize` returns true for any field of an object, that object is added to a "force materialize" list that is consulted at the top of the next iteration; `processStateBeforeLoopOnOverflow`-style materialization is applied to just that object before re-walking the body.

**Design note — why the heuristic is per-field, not per-object**: a typical "stable" virtual object has many invariant fields and one or two iteration-dependent fields. Materializing the whole object on the first sign of any one field changing is too aggressive. The per-field counter ensures we only materialize when a specific field genuinely fails to stabilize.

**Design note — interaction with `EnsureVirtualized`**: the heuristic explicitly skips objects with the `EnsureVirtualized` flag. Forcing materialization on those would silently violate the front-end contract. If such an object is the source of non-convergence, the algorithm falls through to the `MAX_ITERATIONS` path → `MATERIALIZE_ALL` → `RetryableBailoutException`, and the compile retries without PEA. The heuristic is a pure performance optimization on top of a correct base algorithm.

**Design note — threshold**: 3 is chosen because the merge processor's PHI cache typically stabilizes by iteration 2 if it ever will. A field that still differs at iteration 3 is unlikely to stabilize within `MAX_ITERATIONS = 10`.

**Design note — not in Graal**: This heuristic is a Jeandle-specific addition (verified absent from Graal core via search). It can be omitted in v1 and re-added if profiling shows pathological loop cases.

### 2.5.12 Implementation Summary, Bounds, and Edge Cases

**Algorithmic bounds**:
* Per loop, at most `MAX_ITERATIONS = 10` body walks under `REGULAR_VIRTUALIZATION`, plus at most one walk under `MATERIALIZE_ALL`.
* Across a function with maximum loop depth D, the worst-case body-walk count is `O(MAX_ITERATIONS^D)`. The `LoopCutoffDepth = 20` bound caps this in practice.
* Memory: the snapshot at the outermost loop costs one extra copy of `AliasMap`, `BlockEffects`, etc. — bounded by `O(NumInstructions)`. Freed when the outermost loop completes.

**Edge cases**:
* **Dead initial state**. If the initial state is dead (pre-header provably unreachable), `processLoop` short-circuits and returns one dead state per loop exit (Graal lines 368–374). No body walk happens.
* **Single-iteration loops without back edges**. LLVM's `LoopInfo` only counts loops with at least one back edge, so this case does not arise — a "loop" with no back edge is just a straight-line block. Jeandle asserts `L->getNumBackEdges() >= 1`.
* **Loops with multiple pre-headers**. LoopSimplify guarantees a unique pre-header. Jeandle requires LoopSimplify; the pass manager declares this dependency via `AU.addRequiredID(LoopSimplifyID)`.
* **Loops with multiple exit edges from the same predecessor**. LoopSimplify normalizes loop exits to single-predecessor blocks; the LCSSA-style proxy construction in Section 2.5.6 relies on this.
* **PHI nodes added after PEA's transform pass**. PEA records `CreatePHI` effects but does not query its own freshly-created PHIs. The merge processor's PHI cache uses opaque keys (`virtual` for materialized-pointer PHIs, `(ValueNode, index)` for field PHIs, Graal `PartialEscapeClosure.java:808–810`), not LLVM `PHINode*` identity, so cache lookups across iterations work correctly even though the underlying `PHINode*` is not created until the transform pass.
* **Loops containing only `unreachable` exits**. The exit-block iteration in `processLoop` skips successors that are unreachable; `processLoopExit` is never called for them. Matches Graal's `doMergeWithoutDead` (Graal `EffectsClosure.java:593`).
* **Exceptional exits (LLVM `invoke` unwind edges)**. The unwind destination of an `invoke` inside a loop is a normal loop exit from PEA's perspective; the exit block receives a proxy PHI like any other exit. Any virtual object live across the `invoke` must already have been materialized at the `invoke`'s normal/unwind boundary because `invoke` is a possible escape; this is handled by Section 2.3's `processNode` logic, not by loop processing.
* **Irreducible loops**. LLVM's `LoopInfo` does not identify them. Detection: if a back-edge target is not the dominator of all back-edge sources, the loop is irreducible. PEA materializes any virtual entries on entry to the irreducible region and disables virtualization for the duration. Future work: use `scc_iterator` for an explicit SCC pass.
* **Loop ordering — inner first**. Graal processes loops bottom-up (`ReentrantBlockIterator.java:232–237`) because outer loops depend on inner-loop summaries. LLVM's `PostOrderLoopTraversal` can iterate inner-to-outer; Jeandle uses it.

**LLVM-specific deviations from Graal**:

| Topic | Graal | Jeandle deviation |
|---|---|---|
| Loop representation | `LoopBeginNode` + `LoopExitNode` + `LoopEndNode` (structured) | LLVM `Loop` + `getExitBlocks()` (natural-loops) |
| Pre-header | Implicit (loop predecessor of `LoopBeginNode`) | Required via LoopSimplify; asserted |
| Loop exit | `LoopExitNode` is part of the graph | `ExitBlock` is just a successor; proxy PHIs are synthesized by PEA |
| Read-cache invalidation | `stripKilledLoopLocations` | Not needed (SSA already field-sensitive) |
| Snapshot | Includes `loopLocationKillCache` | Drops that entry |

**Files touched (preview for Section 7)**:
* `JeandlePartialEscape.h/cpp` — declarations for `processLoop`, `processLoopExit`, `processInitialLoopState`, `processStateBeforeLoopOnOverflow`, `propagateEnsureVirtualized`, the `EffectsClosureMode` enum, and the `FieldInstabilityTracker` struct.
* `JeandlePEABlockState.h/cpp` — adds `materializeBefore`, `equivalentTo`, `isDead`.
* `JeandlePEAMergeProcessor.h/cpp` — the merge processor whose `needsCaching` flag is set for loop headers; its PHI caches are what makes iterative convergence work.
* `JeandlePEAEffectList.h/cpp` — adds `insertAtBeginning` and `clear` operations used by the discard step and by the converged-iteration commit.

### 2.6 Materialization During Analysis

Materialization is the conversion of a `VirtualObject` from its "scalar replaced" representation (a logical record of field values held only in `ObjectState`) into a concrete heap object backed by an `invoke @jeandle.new_instance(...)` / `invoke @jeandle.newarray(...)` call and a sequence of initializing field stores. During analysis no IR is mutated: every materialization is recorded as a deferred `Effect::Materialize` in a per-block effect list (see Section 1.6) which the transform pass (Section 4) consumes.

The materialization algorithm is the load-bearing recursion of partial escape analysis, and Jeandle mirrors Graal's `PartialEscapeBlockState.materializeBefore` (Graal source at `PartialEscapeBlockState.java:209`, with `materializeWithCommit` at `:292`) almost line-for-line. Subsections 2.6.1–2.6.10 specify the algorithm with full LLVM API detail.

#### 2.6.1 The materialize() entry point

The public entry point used by every callsite that needs to escape a virtual object is `ensureMaterialized` (mirroring Graal's `PartialEscapeClosure.ensureMaterialized`, source at `PartialEscapeClosure.java:538`). It runs the policy checks (mode, `EnsureVirtualized`, strict-lock-order cascade) and then delegates the actual work to `materialize`.

```cpp
// In PartialEscapeAnalysis (the analysis driver).
//
// Returns true if any materialization (this object or a transitively forced
// one via strict-lock-ordering) was recorded.  Returns false if the object was
// already materialized (no-op).  Throws RetryableBailoutException /
// OverflowException as specified in 2.6.6 below.
//
// All parameters are required; none are defaulted.
bool ensureMaterialized(ObjectID ID,
                        PEABlockState &State,
                        BasicBlock *Block,
                        Instruction *InsertBefore,
                        MaterializationReason Reason);
```

The `MaterializationReason` enum carries diagnostic / placement context — it tells `materialize` why we are escaping the object, which in turn determines the `InsertBefore` semantics (see 2.6.7):

```cpp
enum class MaterializationReason {
  EscapingInstruction,   // a Tier-2 instruction that does not pattern-match
  PhiIncompatible,       // a merge with incompatible incoming virtuals
  MergeMixedMaterial,    // merge where some preds are virtual, some material
  LoopExitForced,        // loop exit value cannot stay virtual
  ReturnEscape,          // value flows into a `ret`
  CallEscape,            // value flows into an opaque call/invoke
  StoreEscape,           // virtual ref is stored into a non-virtual location
  LockOrderCascade,      // forced by strict lock ordering (see 2.6.8)
  ValueBased,            // jeandle.check_if_value_based forced it
  EnsureVirtualizedFail, // sentinel, used only to raise
};
```

The private worker, which performs the actual recursion, is:

```cpp
// Returns the sentinel Value* that other effects can reference as "the
// materialized pointer for ID".  This sentinel is a placeholder pointer
// (an instance of jeandle::MaterializedSentinel — a tiny opaque
// MetadataAsValue used as a marker; see Section 4.X).  The transform pass
// rewrites the sentinel to the real `ptr addrspace(1)` produced by the
// inserted allocation call.
Value *materialize(ObjectID ID,
                   PEABlockState &State,
                   BasicBlock *Block,
                   Instruction *InsertBefore,
                   MaterializationReason Reason);
```

The reason `materialize` returns a `Value*` rather than `void` is so that callers (Tier 2 rewrites, merge logic, PHI processing) can place the sentinel directly into their replacement effect — every other effect that depends on this materialization records the sentinel, and Pass 1 of the transform stage patches all sentinels in a single linear sweep after creating the real allocations.

**Design note — sentinel vs. real Value***: Graal solves this differently because Graal's `VirtualObjectNode` is itself a graph node, so its "materialized form" is also a node and can be referenced before being committed. LLVM IR has no analogous concept, so we use a sentinel. The sentinel is `ptr addrspace(1)` typed so that all effect-recorded uses are well-typed before patching.

#### 2.6.2 Cycle prevention — mark-as-materialized-before-recurse

The canonical risk in materializing nested virtual objects is a cycle: object A's field references object B, and object B's field references object A. If we recurse into entries before flipping A's state, the recursion into B will recurse back into A, and so on.

Graal's solution — adopted verbatim — is to **flip the state to materialized BEFORE recursing into entries** (see `PartialEscapeBlockState.materializeWithCommit`, lines 296–321: `objectState.materializeBefore(...)` is called first, then the loop over entries). Jeandle does the same:

```cpp
Value *materialize(ObjectID ID, PEABlockState &State,
                   BasicBlock *Block, Instruction *InsertBefore,
                   MaterializationReason Reason) {
  ObjectState &OS = State.getObjectStateForModification(ID);

  // (1) Already materialized?  Idempotent.
  if (OS.isMaterialized())
    return OS.MaterializedPtr;

  // (2) Policy check (Section 2.6.6).
  enforceEnsureVirtualizedAndMode(ID, OS, Reason);

  // (3) Snapshot the entries BEFORE we flip state (Section 2.6.9).
  SmallVector<FieldValue, 8> Snapshot = OS.Entries;
  unsigned SnapshotLockCount = OS.LockCount;

  // (4) Flip state NOW — breaks any potential cycle.
  //     OS.materialize() clears Entries and LockCount; the snapshot above
  //     preserves what we need.
  Value *Sentinel = SentinelAllocator.create(/* type = */
      PointerType::get(Block->getContext(), /*AddrSpace=*/1));
  OS.materialize(Sentinel);  // see ObjectState::materialize in Section 1.3

  // (5) Now safely recurse into the snapshot.  Any inner reference back to
  //     ID will see OS.isMaterialized() == true and return Sentinel.
  SmallVector<ObjectID, 4> Prereqs;
  for (size_t i = 0; i < Snapshot.size(); ++i) {
    FieldValue &FV = Snapshot[i];
    if (FV.isVirtualRef()) {
      ObjectID InnerID = FV.getRefID();
      Value *InnerSentinel =
          materialize(InnerID, State, Block, InsertBefore,
                      MaterializationReason::EscapingInstruction);
      // Rewrite the snapshot so the effect-recorded field initializer is a
      // valid `ptr addrspace(1)` SSA value (see 2.6.9 last bullet).
      Snapshot[i] = FieldValue::materializedRef(InnerSentinel);
      Prereqs.push_back(InnerID);
    }
  }

  // (6) Strict-lock-ordering cascade (Section 2.6.8).
  if (requiresStrictLockOrder() && SnapshotLockCount > 0) {
    cascadeLockOrderMaterializations(ID, State, Block, InsertBefore, Prereqs);
  }

  // (7) Record the deferred Materialize effect (Section 1.6 Effect schema).
  recordMaterializeEffect(ID, Block, InsertBefore, Sentinel,
                          Snapshot, SnapshotLockCount, Prereqs);

  // (8) Update other ObjectStates and the alias map (Sections 2.6.4 & 2.6.5).
  updateAliasMapForMaterialized(ID, Sentinel);
  updateStatesForMaterialized(State, ID, Sentinel);

  return Sentinel;
}
```

The key invariant is: **once line (4) executes, `OS.isMaterialized()` is true even though the IR has not yet been emitted**. Any recursive call that reaches `materialize(ID, ...)` again — directly or through `updateStatesForMaterialized` — terminates at the early return in line (1).

#### 2.6.3 Recursive prerequisite materialization

Step (5) of 2.6.2 walks the snapshot of `Entries` and recurses on every `VirtualRef`. The recursive calls themselves record `Effect::Materialize` entries via `recordMaterializeEffect`. Because the recursion is depth-first and `recordMaterializeEffect` appends to the per-block effect list, the inner objects are inserted into `BlockEffects[Block]` **before** the outer object's effect — exactly the order the transform pass needs (the inner allocation must dominate the outer object's field-initialization store that references it).

The `Prereqs` list is stored on the outer object's `Effect::Materialize` (the `Prerequisites` field of the effect, see Section 1.6) so that the transform pass can assert that every prerequisite ID has a sentinel-replacement entry before processing the outer materialization. This is purely a defensive check; correct construction guarantees the order.

**Ordering subtlety — recursion into the same field type twice**: If the snapshot has two `VirtualRef` entries pointing to the same `InnerID`, the second call to `materialize(InnerID, ...)` hits the idempotent fast-path at line (1) and returns the same sentinel. Both field initializers in the outer effect then reference the same sentinel.

**Ordering subtlety — recursion driven by `updateStatesForMaterialized`**: After step (8) propagates the sentinel into other `ObjectState`s' entries, those entries are now `MaterializedRef` rather than `VirtualRef`. They will NOT cause further recursion when (later) one of those other objects is itself materialized — the materialized pointer just flows through verbatim.

#### 2.6.4 Updating the alias map after materialization

Jeandle's alias map (Section 1.5) maps `Value*` → `Value*`. Before materialization, the allocation call's result (`%vobj = invoke @jeandle.new_instance(...)`) is mapped to the `VirtualObjectMarker` (a marker `Value*` carrying the `ObjectID`). After materialization, every consumer of `%vobj` must eventually read the new sentinel `Value*` instead.

We follow Graal's `getAliasAndResolve` pattern (`PartialEscapeClosure.java:1575`): the alias map is **not** rewritten in place. Instead, lookups go through a level of indirection: when the alias map returns a `VirtualObjectMarker`, the caller consults the `PEABlockState` to see whether the corresponding `ObjectState` is still virtual or has flipped to materialized; if materialized, the caller substitutes `ObjectState.MaterializedPtr` (the sentinel).

```cpp
// AliasMap::getAliasAndResolve (Section 1.5).
Value *AliasMap::getAliasAndResolve(const PEABlockState &State, Value *V) const {
  auto It = Map.find(V);
  if (It == Map.end()) return V;  // unmapped
  Value *Replacement = It->second;
  if (auto *Marker = dyn_cast<VirtualObjectMarker>(Replacement)) {
    ObjectID ID = Marker->getID();
    const ObjectState &OS = State.getObjectState(ID);
    if (OS.isMaterialized())
      return OS.MaterializedPtr;  // sentinel; will be patched in transform Pass 1
    return Replacement;  // still virtual; caller must handle the marker
  }
  return Replacement;
}
```

**Why this matters**: avoiding the in-place rewrite keeps `addVirtualAlias` cheap (`O(1)` insert), keeps loop re-iteration cheap (we don't have to track which alias-map entries to roll back), and keeps the alias map's `HasVirtualInputs` / `HasScalarReplacedInputs` sets consistent. The same `Value*` keeps its alias-map entry across the virtual→materialized flip; only the resolution of that entry changes.

`updateAliasMapForMaterialized(ID, Sentinel)` in step (8) of 2.6.2 is therefore not a rewrite of `AliasMap.Map` — it does two things:

1. Insert an alias-map entry from the **sentinel** itself to the `VirtualObjectMarker(ID)`. This is so that downstream lookups whose input is the sentinel (rather than the original allocation result) still resolve back to `ID` for purposes of, e.g., detecting that the same materialized object is being passed to a Tier-2 instruction that expects to see virtual or materialized data.
2. Recompute `HasVirtualInputs` for any instruction that uses the original allocation result. After materialization, the use is no longer "virtual"; it is materialized. The marker is left in place but `HasVirtualInputs.erase(I)` is called and `HasScalarReplacedInputs.insert(I)` if appropriate (the user now sees a real `ptr addrspace(1)` value rather than the abstract marker). This is the only set-membership churn required.

#### 2.6.5 Updating other ObjectStates for transitive materialization

`updateStatesForMaterialized` (Graal: `PartialEscapeClosure.updateStatesForMaterialized` at `PartialEscapeClosure.java:654`) is a linear scan over every `ObjectState` in the current `PEABlockState`. For each still-virtual object's entries array, it rewrites any `FieldValue::virtualRef(ID)` into `FieldValue::materializedRef(Sentinel)`.

```cpp
void updateStatesForMaterialized(PEABlockState &State,
                                 ObjectID MaterializedID,
                                 Value *Sentinel) {
  for (ObjectID Other = 0; Other < State.ObjectStates.size(); ++Other) {
    if (Other == MaterializedID) continue;
    ObjectState &OS = State.ObjectStates[Other];
    if (!OS.isVirtual()) continue;  // already materialized; entries already cleared

    bool Touched = false;
    for (FieldValue &FV : OS.Entries) {
      if (FV.isVirtualRef() && FV.getRefID() == MaterializedID) {
        if (!Touched) {
          // COW: take ownership before mutating.
          State.cowObjectStateIfShared(Other);
          // OS may now be a different memory location after COW — re-fetch.
          // (See the inline helper in PEABlockState.)
          // For brevity assume the helper returns a stable reference here.
          Touched = true;
        }
        FV = FieldValue::materializedRef(Sentinel);
      }
    }
  }
}
```

Two subtleties:

- **Linear over all object states, not just one**: An update to ID `M` may make ID `A.fields[3]` materialized; but if `B.fields[7] == VirtualRef(M)` too, B's entry must also be updated. We do not need any transitive cascade here — `A` and `B` both go from `VirtualRef(M)` to `MaterializedRef(Sentinel)` in the same pass, because we walk every `ObjectState`. Crucially, this update is for fields pointing *at* the newly materialized object, NOT for objects that depend on the newly materialized object's own internal state.
- **No back-propagation of the recursive prerequisites**: When `materialize(ID)` recurses on its own entries (step 5 in 2.6.2), each inner recursive call runs its own `updateStatesForMaterialized`. The order is bottom-up. This means by the time the outer `materialize(ID)` reaches step (8), every other state's entries that point at any of the inner prerequisites have already been rewritten by the prerequisite's own call to `updateStatesForMaterialized`. No special handling needed.

#### 2.6.6 EnsureVirtualized check — RetryableBailoutException semantics

`ObjectState::EnsureVirtualized` (Section 1.3) is the analog of Graal's `EnsureVirtualizedNode` (with `localOnly=false`). It indicates that the source program contains an explicit hint that this object MUST be scalarized (for example, certain JVMCI-injected stress tests or, in Jeandle, future intrinsics for VarHandle scalarization). Materializing an object whose `EnsureVirtualized` is set is therefore a hard failure of the optimization promise.

`enforceEnsureVirtualizedAndMode` (called at step 2 of 2.6.2) implements three policies that match Graal's mode handling (see Section 2.5.3 in the existing plan):

```cpp
void enforceEnsureVirtualizedAndMode(ObjectID ID, ObjectState &OS,
                                     MaterializationReason Reason) {
  if (OS.EnsureVirtualized) {
    // Graal throws RetryableBailoutException; the caller in Jeandle is the
    // PEA pass itself, which catches at function granularity, marks the
    // compilation as "should retry without PEA", and re-runs the rest of
    // the pipeline.  See Section 5.X for the retry plumbing.
    throw RetryableBailoutException(
        "PEA: object marked EnsureVirtualized cannot be materialized "
        "(reason=" + toString(Reason) + ", objectID=" + std::to_string(ID) + ")");
  }
  if (CurrentMode == AnalysisMode::STOP_NEW_VIRTUALIZATIONS &&
      Reason != MaterializationReason::LockOrderCascade) {
    // Section 2.5.3: in deep loop nests, the trigger for switching to
    // MATERIALIZE_ALL is any materialization that is not itself forced by
    // strict lock ordering (which is unavoidable bookkeeping, not a real
    // escape).
    throw OverflowException("PEA: STOP_NEW_VIRTUALIZATIONS mode");
  }
  // MATERIALIZE_ALL: materialization is always allowed and is in fact the
  // expected outcome.  No exception.
}
```

**Important — `EnsureVirtualized` is preserved across `ObjectState::materialize()`**: as noted in Section 1.3 (`materialize` does not clear `EnsureVirtualized`), the flag remains observable even after the state flips. This is mostly defensive — after step (4) the object is materialized and we return, so no further callsite should ever consult `EnsureVirtualized` again, but if any code path does, the flag is still true and any spurious second materialization would still raise.

**Why not just bailout immediately on every materialization in STOP_NEW_VIRTUALIZATIONS?** Because the mode is named "stop new virtualizations". Materializing an *existing* virtual object is allowed (it shrinks the virtualization set, not grows it). What the mode prohibits is the side effect of materialization-on-existing triggering a re-iteration of the loop. The outer driver (Section 2.5) catches `OverflowException` and decides whether to escalate to `MATERIALIZE_ALL`.

#### 2.6.7 Materialization placement rules

The `InsertBefore` parameter of `materialize` is the single source of truth for where the eventual allocation call will be emitted by the transform pass. The placement must satisfy two constraints:

1. **Dominance**: the inserted allocation `invoke` must dominate every use that references the resulting sentinel. Since the sentinel replaces every use of the original `VirtualObject`, this means the insertion point must dominate the original allocation site's use frontier modulo the materialization itself.
2. **Reachability**: the materialization must lie on every dynamic execution path that reaches a use. We cannot insert it on a path that may not be taken (this would skip the heap object on one path and reach a use of the sentinel on another, breaking SSA).

Concrete placement scenarios:

- **In-block escape (`MaterializationReason::EscapingInstruction`, `CallEscape`, `StoreEscape`, `ReturnEscape`, `ValueBased`)**: `Block` is the current block being processed; `InsertBefore` is the offending instruction. The materialization will execute on every path that reaches the escape site.

- **At a merge — mixed virtual/materialized (`MaterializationReason::MergeMixedMaterial`)**: Each predecessor that is virtual must materialize **in its own predecessor block, before the terminator**. So `Block = PredBB` and `InsertBefore = PredBB->getTerminator()`. The merge-block's PHI then collapses the materialized pointers from each predecessor. Predecessors that were already materialized do not call `materialize` again (the idempotent fast path).

- **At a merge — PHI incompatibility (`MaterializationReason::PhiIncompatible`)**: A PHI's incoming values reference incompatible virtual objects (different Klass, lock count mismatch, or non-single-use allocation; see Section 2.3.19). Same placement as MergeMixedMaterial: `Block = PredBB`, `InsertBefore = PredBB->getTerminator()`. After materialization the PHI is a PHI of `ptr addrspace(1)`.

- **At a loop exit (`MaterializationReason::LoopExitForced`)**: This happens when `processVirtualAtLoopExit` (Section 2.5.2) decides the object cannot remain virtual across the loop boundary (e.g., because its allocation is inside the loop and a use is outside, with no compatible PHI in between). The materialization is placed inside the loop body, just before the back-edge or before the originating exit; the precise insertion point is the latest instruction in the loop body that the analysis can still rewrite. In practice this is the `LoopExit` block's first non-PHI instruction if the object is virtual on every path leading to that exit, or the loop's latch terminator if the materialization must happen on every iteration. See Section 2.5.2 for the proxy PHI handling at the exit.

- **Forced by strict lock ordering (`MaterializationReason::LockOrderCascade`)**: The triggering object's materialization point is reused — every cascaded object materializes at the same `Block` / `InsertBefore` as the object that triggered the cascade, ensuring lock-order at runtime matches the bytecode lock acquisition order. See 2.6.8.

The placement is recorded once in the `Effect::Materialize.InsertBefore` field and never modified by subsequent effects. If a later analysis step needs to materialize the same object at an *earlier* point (e.g., we discovered another use that dominates the original `InsertBefore`), we do NOT shift the existing materialization; instead the early use is rewritten to refer to the same sentinel and the transform pass is responsible for inserting the allocation at the recorded point — which by construction dominates both uses because we always pick the earliest of the known escape points.

**Tightening**: the analysis driver maintains, per `(ID)`, the dominator-tree-minimum insertion point among all calls to `materialize(ID, ...)`. If a subsequent call would produce an `InsertBefore` that does NOT dominate the recorded point, the recorded point is updated (and the `Effect::Materialize.InsertBefore` is replaced). This guarantees that the first time we record the effect, we have already done the dominance reasoning. Implementation:

```cpp
void recordMaterializeEffect(ObjectID ID, BasicBlock *Block,
                             Instruction *InsertBefore, Value *Sentinel,
                             ArrayRef<FieldValue> Snapshot,
                             unsigned LockCount,
                             ArrayRef<ObjectID> Prereqs) {
  auto It = MaterializeEffects.find(ID);
  if (It != MaterializeEffects.end()) {
    // Already recorded; reconcile InsertBefore by taking the dominator-tree
    // earliest of the two.
    Effect &Existing = It->second;
    if (!dominates(Existing.InsertBefore, InsertBefore))
      Existing.InsertBefore = earliestCommonDominator(
          Existing.InsertBefore, InsertBefore);
    return;
  }
  Effect E;
  E.Kind = EffectKind::Materialize;
  E.Block = Block;
  E.InsertBefore = InsertBefore;
  E.Replacement = Sentinel;
  E.ObjID = ID;
  E.FieldValues.assign(Snapshot.begin(), Snapshot.end());
  E.Prerequisites.assign(Prereqs.begin(), Prereqs.end());
  E.LockCount = LockCount;
  MaterializeEffects[ID] = E;
  BlockEffects[Block].push_back(E);
}
```

Note: in practice, because forward dataflow processes each instruction once and reaches the first escape point via natural dominator order, the deduplication above is rarely hit outside of loop re-iteration. The discard-on-non-convergence (Section 2.5.1) wipes `MaterializeEffects` along with `BlockEffects`.

#### 2.6.8 Strict lock ordering on materialization

`requiresStrictLockOrder()` is a target-property predicate (`true` on platforms using lightweight locking, false otherwise) modeled after Graal's `PartialEscapeBlockState.materializeVirtualLocksBefore` at `PartialEscapeClosure.java:641`. The Java runtime contract is: locks acquired by a single thread must be released in the reverse order of acquisition (LIFO), and the runtime's monitor stack only validates this if the underlying objects materialize in the same nesting order.

When `requiresStrictLockOrder() == true` and we are materializing object `ID` whose `LockCount > 0`, we must additionally materialize **every** still-virtual object in the current `PEABlockState` that holds a lock with lock-depth *less than* the lock-depth of `ID` (i.e., locks acquired earlier in the source program). Lock depth is computed from the order of `monitorenter` calls in the bytecode and is tracked by an auxiliary `LockDepth[ID]` map updated whenever Section 2.3.5 records an `Effect::EliminateLock`.

```cpp
void cascadeLockOrderMaterializations(ObjectID Trigger,
                                      PEABlockState &State,
                                      BasicBlock *Block,
                                      Instruction *InsertBefore,
                                      SmallVectorImpl<ObjectID> &Prereqs) {
  unsigned TriggerDepth = LockDepth[Trigger];
  // Find all still-virtual objects with lower depth and non-zero lock count.
  SmallVector<std::pair<unsigned, ObjectID>, 4> ToCascade;
  for (ObjectID Other = 0; Other < State.ObjectStates.size(); ++Other) {
    if (Other == Trigger) continue;
    ObjectState &OS = State.ObjectStates[Other];
    if (!OS.isVirtual()) continue;
    if (OS.LockCount == 0) continue;
    if (LockDepth[Other] >= TriggerDepth) continue;
    ToCascade.emplace_back(LockDepth[Other], Other);
  }
  // Cascade in order of increasing lock depth (outermost lock first).
  llvm::sort(ToCascade);
  for (auto &P : ToCascade) {
```
