```cpp
    // Reuse Block / InsertBefore of the trigger.  This places all cascaded
    // allocations + locks in the same straight-line region just before
    // the offending instruction; the runtime then sees outer→inner
    // monitorenter sequence preserved.
    materialize(P.second, State, Block, InsertBefore,
                MaterializationReason::LockOrderCascade);
    Prereqs.push_back(P.second);
  }
}
```

The recorded `Effect::Materialize.LockCount` (set to `SnapshotLockCount` at step 7 of 2.6.2) tells the transform pass to emit, after the allocation but before the next instruction, exactly that many `jeandle.monitorenter_with_*` calls on the materialized object — recreating the lock state that the abstract `ObjectState` had been tracking.

**Note**: cascade is gated on `requiresStrictLockOrder()` AND `SnapshotLockCount > 0`. An unlocked virtual object never triggers a cascade because there is no lock-stack invariant to preserve. Likewise, if `requiresStrictLockOrder()` is false (e.g., heavyweight locking targets), monitors do not need to be acquired in source order with respect to each other across distinct objects, so the cascade is unnecessary.

#### 2.6.9 Recording field values for materialization

The `Effect::Materialize.FieldValues` snapshot is the input to the transform pass's field-initialization sequence. It must be **taken before** `ObjectState::materialize()` clears `Entries` (step 3 of 2.6.2). Three additional invariants must hold for the snapshot to produce valid IR:

1. **Dominance of every scalar entry at `InsertBefore`.** Every `FieldValue::Scalar(V)` in the snapshot must have `V` dominating `InsertBefore`. By construction this is true for in-block escapes (the store that defined `V` lies upstream in the same block, and `InsertBefore` is downstream). For merge materializations at a predecessor terminator, however, the snapshot is taken from the merge block's state, and `V` may be a value defined only in some predecessors. Handling:
   - If `V` is a constant or a function argument: dominance is trivial.
   - If `V` is an `Instruction*` defined in `PredBB`: dominance is satisfied because `InsertBefore` is `PredBB->getTerminator()` and `V` was defined earlier in the block.
   - If `V` is defined in a different predecessor: the snapshot is wrong — see PHI handling below.

2. **Prerequisites' `VirtualRef` entries rewritten to `MaterializedRef`.** Step (5) of 2.6.2 already mutates `Snapshot[i]` from `FieldValue::virtualRef(InnerID)` to `FieldValue::materializedRef(InnerSentinel)`. Because `InnerSentinel` is created at the start of the recursive `materialize(InnerID, ...)` call (line 4 of that call), the sentinel is available for splicing immediately. This guarantees that the recorded `FieldValues` list contains only `Scalar`, `MaterializedRef`, or `Unknown` entries by the time it is appended to the effect list — there are no dangling `VirtualRef`s.

3. **PHI-defined scalar entries use per-predecessor incoming values.** If a field's scalar value `V` is a PHI node in the merge block, materializing at a *predecessor's* terminator must use `Phi->getIncomingValueForBlock(PredBB)` rather than the PHI itself (the PHI is not yet defined at the predecessor's terminator). The snapshot-building code therefore inspects every scalar:

```cpp
FieldValue rewriteScalarForPlacement(FieldValue FV, BasicBlock *Block,
                                     Instruction *InsertBefore) {
  if (!FV.isScalar()) return FV;
  Value *V = FV.getScalarValue();
  if (auto *Phi = dyn_cast<PHINode>(V)) {
    // If the PHI is in a block that does not dominate InsertBefore, we need
    // to use the appropriate incoming value.
    BasicBlock *PhiBlock = Phi->getParent();
    if (PhiBlock != Block && !DT->dominates(PhiBlock, Block)) {
      // Likely case: materializing at PredBB->terminator() for a merge in
      // PhiBlock.  Use the PHI's incoming value for PredBB.
      if (int Idx = Phi->getBasicBlockIndex(Block); Idx >= 0)
        return FieldValue::scalar(Phi->getIncomingValue(Idx));
    }
  }
  return FV;
}
```

This rewrite is applied to every scalar in the snapshot at recording time (inside `recordMaterializeEffect`, before storing into the effect). It mirrors the exact same defensive logic Graal applies when its `materializeBefore` walks the entries.

4. **`Unknown` entries.** Fields that were never written keep their `FieldValue::Unknown` tag in the snapshot. The transform pass interprets `Unknown` as "skip the store" — Java semantics already guarantee zeroed memory at allocation (the inserted `invoke @jeandle.new_instance` calls into the runtime allocator which zero-fills before returning). For arrays, the same applies: unwritten elements remain zero/null per JLS.

#### 2.6.10 What happens after materialization

Once `materialize` returns:

- **The state is monotonic.** `ObjectState::Kind` only ever transitions `Virtual → Materialized`. There is no path back to `Virtual`. This monotonicity is the basis for fixpoint termination: each iteration of a loop can only increase the set of materialized objects, never decrease it, so the lattice height is bounded by the number of allocations.

- **No re-virtualization.** A materialized object is treated by all subsequent analysis as an opaque `ptr addrspace(1)`. Tier-2 patterns that previously matched on the virtual marker now see the sentinel and either: (a) match patterns that operate on already-materialized objects (rare — there are no such patterns in 2.3.X except for monitor ops on materialized objects which are passed through unchanged), or (b) fall through to "non-virtualizable instruction" handling and proceed as if there were no virtual object.

- **The alias map remains stable.** As described in 2.6.4, the alias map still maps `OriginalAllocCall* → VirtualObjectMarker(ID)`. Lookups through `getAliasAndResolve` now return the sentinel. No alias-map churn.

- **Effects are deferred.** No IR has been mutated. The transform pass (Section 4) consumes `BlockEffects` and emits real allocation calls, field stores, and monitor calls in the order they were recorded.

- **Loop re-iteration may discard the effect.** If the loop body containing this materialization does not converge, Section 2.5.1 discards `BlockEffects[B]` for every block in the loop. The next iteration may reach a fixpoint where the same object is materialized again, possibly at the same insertion point, possibly at a different one (e.g., MATERIALIZE_ALL mode forces materialization at the loop preheader). The discard-and-replay design ensures we never carry stale materialization effects across iterations.

---

### 2.7 resolveVirtualRef

`resolveVirtualRef` is the lookup that every Tier-2 and Tier-3 instruction processor calls on each pointer-typed operand to decide whether that operand actually points to a virtual object. It is purely a query: it never mutates the alias map, never materializes, never records effects. Its job is to peel off LLVM IR ceremony (GEPs, address-space casts, freezes, certain inttoptr/ptrtoint launderings) until it either lands on something registered in the alias map as a `VirtualObjectMarker`, or it gives up and returns `std::nullopt`.

#### 2.7.1 Entry points and recursion

```cpp
// Public entry point.  Returns the ObjectID of the virtual object that V
// refers to, or std::nullopt if V does not resolve to any virtual object in
// the current block state.
//
// The current block state is needed for two reasons:
//   1. To consult ObjectState::isMaterialized() — a Value that the alias map
//      reports as a VirtualObjectMarker may have flipped to materialized
//      (Section 2.6.4); in that case resolveVirtualRef returns std::nullopt
//      because the value no longer denotes a virtual object.
//   2. To cache results within a single instruction processing call.  Each
//      invocation of resolveVirtualRef carries a small DenseSet<Value*>
//      visited set to terminate on pathological IR.
std::optional<ObjectID>
PartialEscapeAnalysis::resolveVirtualRef(Value *V,
                                         const PEABlockState &State) const {
  DenseSet<Value *> Visited;
  return resolveVirtualRefImpl(V, State, Visited);
}
```

The implementation is a single recursive function. The recursion is bounded by `Visited` (every visited `Value*` is recorded, and re-visits return `nullopt`); in practice the recursion depth is at most the length of the GEP/cast chain, which LLVM canonicalizes to fewer than ~5 levels for typical Jeandle IR.

#### 2.7.2 GEP traversal — base pointer chasing

The dominant pattern in Jeandle IR (see `jeandleAbstractInterpreter.cpp:2419` and surrounding `compute_instance_field_address`) is:

```text
%fieldptr = getelementptr i8, ptr addrspace(1) %obj, i64 <offset>
```

`resolveVirtualRef` unconditionally recurses through `GetElementPtrInst::getPointerOperand()` regardless of the indices' shape. This is correct because every GEP produces a pointer derived from the base; if the base resolves to a virtual object, so does the GEP. (The field-offset extraction is a separate concern handled by `resolveFieldOffset`, Section 2.8.)

```cpp
if (auto *GEP = dyn_cast<GEPOperator>(V)) {
  return resolveVirtualRefImpl(GEP->getPointerOperand(), State, Visited);
}
```

We accept any `GEPOperator` (instruction or constant expression) and any number of indices.

#### 2.7.3 Alias map lookup precedence

The alias map is consulted **first** (before any structural peeling). This matters because:

- A load result `%v = load ptr addrspace(1), ptr addrspace(1) %p` may have been added to the alias map by Section 2.3.4 as a virtual alias for a nested `VirtualRef` field. In that case `%v` itself denotes a virtual object even though it has no structural relationship to its allocation site.
- A PHI node's result may have been added as a virtual alias by Section 2.3.19. Its operands are not virtual — only the PHI itself is.

```cpp
std::optional<ObjectID>
resolveVirtualRefImpl(Value *V, const PEABlockState &State,
                      DenseSet<Value *> &Visited) const {
  if (!Visited.insert(V).second)
    return std::nullopt;  // cycle

  // (1) Direct alias map lookup.
  if (Value *Replacement = AliasMap.getAlias(V)) {
    if (auto *Marker = dyn_cast<VirtualObjectMarker>(Replacement)) {
      ObjectID ID = Marker->getID();
      if (State.getObjectState(ID).isVirtual())
        return ID;
      // Marker present but ObjectState materialized: V is no longer virtual.
      return std::nullopt;
    }
    // Scalar replacement aliases never resolve to a virtual object.
    return std::nullopt;
  }

  // (2) Structural peeling — see 2.7.2, 2.7.4, 2.7.5.
  // ... fall through to GEP, cast, freeze, etc. ...
}
```

#### 2.7.4 Handling Constants (ConstantExpr GEP, null, undef, poison)

- **`Constant::isNullValue()` (i.e., `null` pointer)**: returns `std::nullopt`. Null is never a virtual object.
- **`UndefValue` / `PoisonValue`**: returns `std::nullopt`. Undef/poison may not be a heap pointer; treating it as a virtual object would let us "virtualize" undefined behavior, which is incorrect.
- **`ConstantPointerNull`**: returns `std::nullopt`. Same as above.
- **`GlobalVariable`**: returns `std::nullopt`. Java statics are tracked through a different path (klass mirror loads) — they are not virtual objects.
- **`ConstantExpr` of kind `getelementptr`**: handled via the `GEPOperator` branch in 2.7.2 (LLVM's `GEPOperator` covers both `GetElementPtrInst` and constant-expression GEPs).
- **`ConstantExpr` of kind `bitcast` / `addrspacecast`**: handled via the cast branches in 2.7.5.
- **`ConstantInt`**: returns `std::nullopt`. Numeric constants cannot be virtual objects. (Reachable when chasing through `inttoptr(ptrtoint)` laundering — see 2.7.5.)

```cpp
if (auto *C = dyn_cast<Constant>(V)) {
  if (C->isNullValue()) return std::nullopt;
  if (isa<UndefValue>(C) || isa<PoisonValue>(C)) return std::nullopt;
  if (isa<GlobalValue>(C) || isa<ConstantInt>(C) ||
      isa<ConstantFP>(C) || isa<ConstantPointerNull>(C))
    return std::nullopt;
  // ConstantExpr GEP/cast: fall through to the structural cases below.
}
```

#### 2.7.5 Materialized aliases — what to return

If the alias map's `Replacement` is a `VirtualObjectMarker` but the corresponding `ObjectState` has already been materialized (i.e., `OS.MaterializedPtr` is the sentinel and `OS.Kind == Materialized`), `resolveVirtualRef` returns `std::nullopt`. This is the correct behaviour: a materialized object is, from the perspective of all subsequent analysis, indistinguishable from any other heap pointer, and Tier-2 processing must NOT try to treat it as virtual.

Callers that need both the "virtual" and "materialized" views — for example, equality-comparison processing (`icmp eq` on two pointers, Section 2.3.12) — must call a sibling query `resolveToMaterializedOrVirtual` that returns a discriminated union `{ Virtual(ID), Materialized(Value*), Unknown }`. `resolveVirtualRef` only handles the virtual case.

**Structural cases for the cast / freeze / launder family**:

```cpp
// AddrSpaceCastInst: only ptr addrspace(1) is the Java heap address space.
// A cast to/from another address space cannot denote a Java oop.
if (auto *ASC = dyn_cast<AddrSpaceCastInst>(V)) {
  if (auto *DstPT = dyn_cast<PointerType>(ASC->getType())) {
    if (DstPT->getAddressSpace() != 1) return std::nullopt;
  }
  if (auto *SrcPT = dyn_cast<PointerType>(ASC->getOperand(0)->getType())) {
    if (SrcPT->getAddressSpace() != 1) return std::nullopt;
  }
  return resolveVirtualRefImpl(ASC->getOperand(0), State, Visited);
}

// BitCastInst between pointers: with opaque pointers (LLVM 17+) this is
// rare; we still chase through.  Cross-address-space bitcasts are illegal
// IR and must be addrspacecast instead — assert here as a defensive check.
if (auto *BC = dyn_cast<BitCastInst>(V)) {
  assert(BC->getType()->isPointerTy() ==
         BC->getOperand(0)->getType()->isPointerTy() &&
         "bitcast must preserve pointer-ness");
  if (BC->getType()->isPointerTy()) {
    auto *DstPT = cast<PointerType>(BC->getType());
    auto *SrcPT = cast<PointerType>(BC->getOperand(0)->getType());
    assert(DstPT->getAddressSpace() == SrcPT->getAddressSpace() &&
           "use addrspacecast to change address space");
  }
  return resolveVirtualRefImpl(BC->getOperand(0), State, Visited);
}

// FreezeInst: passthrough — freeze is a no-op for defined values.
if (auto *FI = dyn_cast<FreezeInst>(V)) {
  return resolveVirtualRefImpl(FI->getOperand(0), State, Visited);
}

// IntToPtr(PtrToInt(x)) laundering: ONLY safe when the int width matches
// the pointer size for the address space.  Tagged pointers (where the int
// has been masked or shifted) must escape.
if (auto *I2P = dyn_cast<IntToPtrInst>(V)) {
  Value *IntOp = I2P->getOperand(0);
  if (auto *P2I = dyn_cast<PtrToIntInst>(IntOp)) {
    Type *PtrTy = P2I->getPointerOperand()->getType();
    unsigned AS = cast<PointerType>(PtrTy)->getAddressSpace();
    unsigned PtrBits = DL->getPointerSizeInBits(AS);
    unsigned IntBits = IntOp->getType()->getIntegerBitWidth();
    if (PtrBits == IntBits) {
      // Pure round-trip; safe to chase.
      return resolveVirtualRefImpl(P2I->getPointerOperand(), State, Visited);
    }
  }
  return std::nullopt;
}

// Select / PHI: NOT handled by resolveVirtualRef.  These are resolved
// during merge processing (Section 2.4) and PHI processing
// (Section 2.3.19), which adds the PHI/select result to the alias map
// if appropriate.  By the time resolveVirtualRef is called on a use of
// the PHI, the alias-map lookup at step (1) covers it.  Falling through
// here returns nullopt, which is correct for the "PHI not yet processed"
// case.
```

#### 2.7.6 Cross-block resolution

`resolveVirtualRef` is called with a specific `PEABlockState` — the state of the block currently being processed. There is no implicit cross-block resolution: a `Value*` is resolved according to *this* block's view of which `ObjectID`s are still virtual.

Cross-block flow happens through three mechanisms, none of which are `resolveVirtualRef`'s concern:

1. **The alias map is global.** A `Value*` that was added as a virtual alias in block A is still in the alias map when processing block B. `resolveVirtualRef` finds it via the alias-map lookup. The check `State.getObjectState(ID).isVirtual()` then disambiguates based on block B's view — block B's state may have materialized the object (e.g., via a merge from another predecessor), in which case `resolveVirtualRef` returns `std::nullopt`.

2. **PHIs at merge blocks.** When processing the merge block's PHIs (Section 2.3.19), the PHI's result is added to the alias map if compatible. Subsequent uses in the merge block then resolve via step 1.

3. **Loop iteration.** During loop re-iteration the alias map for nodes within the loop is reset (`aliases.set(node, null)` in Graal, see Section 1.5). After the reset, `resolveVirtualRef` on the reset node returns `std::nullopt` until that node is re-processed and re-added.

#### 2.7.7 Concrete pseudocode in C++ with LLVM API

Putting it all together:

```cpp
std::optional<ObjectID>
PartialEscapeAnalysis::resolveVirtualRefImpl(
    Value *V, const PEABlockState &State,
    DenseSet<Value *> &Visited) const {
  if (V == nullptr) return std::nullopt;
  if (!Visited.insert(V).second) return std::nullopt;

  // (1) Alias map.
  if (Value *Replacement = AliasMap.getAlias(V)) {
    if (auto *Marker = dyn_cast<VirtualObjectMarker>(Replacement)) {
      ObjectID ID = Marker->getID();
      if (State.getObjectState(ID).isVirtual())
        return ID;
      return std::nullopt;
    }
    return std::nullopt;
  }

  // (2) Constants and special values.
  if (auto *C = dyn_cast<Constant>(V)) {
    if (C->isNullValue() || isa<UndefValue>(C) || isa<PoisonValue>(C) ||
        isa<GlobalValue>(C) || isa<ConstantInt>(C) || isa<ConstantFP>(C))
      return std::nullopt;
    // ConstantExpr GEP/cast falls through.
  }

  // (3) GEP.
  if (auto *GEP = dyn_cast<GEPOperator>(V))
    return resolveVirtualRefImpl(GEP->getPointerOperand(), State, Visited);

  // (4) AddrSpaceCast.
  if (auto *ASC = dyn_cast<AddrSpaceCastOperator>(V)) {
    if (auto *DstPT = dyn_cast<PointerType>(ASC->getType()))
      if (DstPT->getAddressSpace() != 1) return std::nullopt;
    if (auto *SrcPT = dyn_cast<PointerType>(ASC->getOperand(0)->getType()))
      if (SrcPT->getAddressSpace() != 1) return std::nullopt;
    return resolveVirtualRefImpl(ASC->getOperand(0), State, Visited);
  }

  // (5) BitCast.
  if (auto *BC = dyn_cast<BitCastOperator>(V)) {
    if (BC->getType()->isPointerTy()) {
      auto *DstPT = cast<PointerType>(BC->getType());
      auto *SrcPT = cast<PointerType>(BC->getOperand(0)->getType());
      assert(DstPT->getAddressSpace() == SrcPT->getAddressSpace() &&
             "use addrspacecast to change address space");
      (void)DstPT; (void)SrcPT;
    }
    return resolveVirtualRefImpl(BC->getOperand(0), State, Visited);
  }

  // (6) Freeze.
  if (auto *FI = dyn_cast<FreezeInst>(V))
    return resolveVirtualRefImpl(FI->getOperand(0), State, Visited);

  // (7) IntToPtr(PtrToInt(x)) round-trip launder.
  if (auto *I2P = dyn_cast<IntToPtrInst>(V)) {
    if (auto *P2I = dyn_cast<PtrToIntInst>(I2P->getOperand(0))) {
      Type *PtrTy = P2I->getPointerOperand()->getType();
      unsigned AS = cast<PointerType>(PtrTy)->getAddressSpace();
      unsigned PtrBits = DL->getPointerSizeInBits(AS);
      unsigned IntBits = P2I->getType()->getIntegerBitWidth();
      if (PtrBits == IntBits)
        return resolveVirtualRefImpl(P2I->getPointerOperand(), State,
                                     Visited);
    }
    return std::nullopt;
  }

  // (8) Anything else (PHI, Select, Call, Argument, etc.) — no recursion.
  return std::nullopt;
}
```

**Design note — `GEPOperator` vs. `GetElementPtrInst`**: LLVM's `GEPOperator` is an `Operator` wrapper that covers both `GetElementPtrInst` and `ConstantExpr` of kind GEP. Using `dyn_cast<GEPOperator>` (instead of `dyn_cast<GetElementPtrInst>`) automatically handles both cases. Similarly `AddrSpaceCastOperator` and `BitCastOperator` cover the constant-expression and instruction forms uniformly.

#### 2.7.8 What resolveVirtualRef does NOT do

To keep this lookup small and predictable, the following are explicitly out of scope:

- **Materialization.** `resolveVirtualRef` never triggers materialization. Callers that need to materialize call `ensureMaterialized(ID, ...)` after `resolveVirtualRef` returns an ID.
- **PHI processing.** Resolving a PHI's operands is the merge logic's job. `resolveVirtualRef` reads PHI results via the alias map; it does not recurse into a PHI's incoming values.
- **Select processing.** Same reason. `Select` instructions with one or both arms virtual are handled by Section 2.3.17, which may add the select result to the alias map if both arms are virtual and merge-compatible.
- **Call return value tracking.** A call's return value is registered in the alias map only by the Tier-1 allocation handler (for `jeandle.new_instance` / `jeandle.newarray`). Other calls' return values are never virtual (any call that returns a `ptr addrspace(1)` is an opaque heap pointer from the analysis's perspective).
- **Memory disambiguation.** Two loads from the same field do not get resolved as "the same virtual object" by `resolveVirtualRef`. Each load is resolved through the alias map only if it was added there by Section 2.3.4.
- **Type narrowing.** `resolveVirtualRef` does not consult `!java-klass` metadata or any type information. It only chases pointers structurally and through the alias map.
- **Field offset extraction.** That is `resolveFieldOffset`'s job (Section 2.8). Two queries with different roles — `resolveVirtualRef` finds *which object*, `resolveFieldOffset` finds *which field of that object*.

---

### 2.8 Field Offset Resolution

`resolveFieldOffset(Value *Ptr)` returns the byte offset from the base of the virtual object that `Ptr` points into, or `std::nullopt` if the offset cannot be determined as a compile-time constant. It is paired with `resolveVirtualRef` at every memory operation site: first resolve the base, then resolve the offset.

The function must work on top of arbitrary legal LLVM IR while being optimised for Jeandle's interpreter-emitted patterns (see `jeandleAbstractInterpreter.cpp:2419` — `compute_instance_field_address` emits canonical `GEP i8, obj, i64 offset`).

#### 2.8.1 GEP pattern matching (five patterns)

```cpp
std::optional<int64_t>
PartialEscapeAnalysis::resolveFieldOffset(Value *Ptr) const {
  const unsigned PtrBits = DL->getPointerSizeInBits(/*AS=*/1);

  // ---------- Pattern 1: Jeandle-canonical i8-typed GEP. ----------
  // This is the dominant case (see jeandleAbstractInterpreter.cpp:2419).
  //   %fp = getelementptr i8, ptr addrspace(1) %obj, i64 <const>
  if (auto *GEP = dyn_cast<GEPOperator>(Ptr)) {
    if (GEP->getNumIndices() == 1 &&
        GEP->getSourceElementType()->isIntegerTy(8)) {
      if (auto *CI = dyn_cast<ConstantInt>(GEP->getOperand(1))) {
        APInt V = CI->getValue().sextOrTrunc(PtrBits);
        return V.getSExtValue();
      }
      // Single-index i8 GEP with non-constant offset: see 2.8.2.
      return std::nullopt;
    }

    // ---------- Pattern 2: Struct/array GEP with all-constant indices. ----------
    // E.g. `getelementptr %java.lang.Integer, ptr addrspace(1) %obj,
    //       i32 0, i32 1` — rare but legal IR.
    APInt Acc(PtrBits, 0, /*isSigned=*/true);
    if (GEP->accumulateConstantOffset(*DL, Acc))
      return Acc.getSExtValue();

    // ---------- Pattern 3: GEP with first index constant zero. ----------
    // `getelementptr <ty>, ptr addrspace(1) %obj, i64 0, i32 K, ...`
    // — accumulateConstantOffset already covers this if all subsequent
    // indices are constants.  If a later index is non-constant (array
    // element indexing into a virtual array), the call returns false and
    // we fall through.
    return std::nullopt;
  }

  // ---------- Pattern 4: Bitcast / freeze / addrspacecast chain to a GEP.
  // Strip via stripPointerCastsAndAliases and retry.
  Value *Stripped = Ptr->stripPointerCastsAndAliases();
  if (Stripped != Ptr)
    return resolveFieldOffset(Stripped);

  // ---------- Pattern 5: Ptr is the base allocation itself. ----------
  // No GEP at all — the load/store is targeting the object header
  // (mark word / klass pointer / array length).  Offset is zero by
  // construction of the IR.
  if (resolveVirtualRefImpl(Ptr, /*State=*/AnyState, ...)) {
    // The caller already knows the base resolves to a virtual object;
    // we report offset = 0.
    return 0;
  }

  return std::nullopt;
}
```

**Design note — why not always use `accumulateConstantOffset`?** It would work for Patterns 1, 2, and 3 uniformly. We special-case Pattern 1 because (a) it's by far the dominant case (>99% of memory operations in Jeandle IR) and the explicit fast-path avoids the per-index APInt arithmetic of `accumulateConstantOffset`, and (b) the special case makes the policy for non-constant offsets (2.8.2) easy to spot in the source.

**Design note — `getOperand(1)` vs. `idx_begin()`**: For a single-index GEP, `GEP->getOperand(1)` and `*GEP->idx_begin()` are the same value. `getOperand(1)` is slightly faster because `idx_begin()` returns a use iterator. Both are correct; we use whichever reads more clearly at the call site.

#### 2.8.2 Non-constant offsets — escape policy, future ScalarEvolution work

If `resolveFieldOffset` returns `std::nullopt` because the offset is non-constant, the calling Tier-2 processor (Sections 2.3.3 store, 2.3.4 load) must treat the access as **opaque** — it could read or write any field of the virtual object, and we have no way to track which. The safe response is to **materialize** the base virtual object before the access:

```cpp
// In processStore / processLoad:
auto BaseID = resolveVirtualRef(Ptr, State);
auto Offset = resolveFieldOffset(Ptr);
if (BaseID && !Offset) {
  ensureMaterialized(*BaseID, State, CurrentBlock, CurrentInst,
                     MaterializationReason::EscapingInstruction);
  // ... fall through to normal (non-virtual) load/store handling ...
}
```

**Future work — ScalarEvolution**: For loops with a virtual array indexed by an affine recurrence (e.g., `for (i=0; i<3; i++) a[i] = ...` where `a` is a length-3 virtual array), `ScalarEvolution::getSCEV(IndexValue)` could be used to enumerate the constant values the index takes across the loop, allowing the entire access to remain virtual. This is an opt-in pass we explicitly defer to a later milestone — Graal does not do it either (Graal requires `MaximumEscapeAnalysisArrayLength` constant indices, and arrays accessed with non-constant indices escape).

The hook for this future work is to extend `resolveFieldOffset` with an overload that returns `SmallVector<int64_t>` (a set of possible offsets) and a corresponding `ObjectState` extension to permit "weak update" semantics on uncertain offsets. None of this exists in v1 of PEA — non-constant offsets always force escape.

#### 2.8.3 Negative offsets

LLVM GEPs may legally produce negative offsets (the index is signed `i64`). For Java objects, the only legitimate negative offsets are into the object header: on some platforms the mark word and klass pointer live at offsets that are reachable from a "narrow oop" base via negative offsets. Jeandle's interpreter, however, emits all header accesses with non-negative offsets relative to the oop base.

Policy: `resolveFieldOffset` returns the signed offset verbatim (via `APInt::getSExtValue`). If the returned offset is negative, the caller (Sections 2.3.3/2.3.4) checks whether the offset corresponds to a known header slot (see 2.8.7–2.8.9). If not, the access is treated as opaque and the base is materialized — we do not silently accept negative offsets that we cannot account for, because doing so would risk under-approximating which field is being touched.

```cpp
int64_t Off = *resolveFieldOffset(Ptr);
if (Off < 0) {
  // Only acceptable if it's a recognised header slot below zero (rare).
  if (!isKnownHeaderOffset(Off, VObj)) {
    ensureMaterialized(*BaseID, State, CurrentBlock, CurrentInst,
                       MaterializationReason::EscapingInstruction);
    return;
  }
}
```

#### 2.8.4 Offset extending past object end

For instance objects, `VirtualObject::SizeInBytes` is known (extracted at allocation time from `jeandle.new_instance` arg1, see Section 2.3.1). An access whose `(Offset + AccessSize)` exceeds `SizeInBytes` is out-of-bounds. This is undefined behaviour in Java (the bytecode verifier prevents it), so it can only arise from broken IR or from sub-field type punning through `Unsafe`. Policy: materialize.

For arrays, the bound is `arrayOopDesc::header_size_in_bytes() + ArrayLengthVal * elementSize`. If the access offset exceeds this bound, materialize.

```cpp
auto FieldEnd = static_cast<uint64_t>(Off) +
                DL->getTypeStoreSize(AccessType);
uint64_t ObjEnd = VObj->Kind == VirtualObject::Instance
                      ? VObj->SizeInBytes
                      : computeArraySizeInBytes(VObj);
if (FieldEnd > ObjEnd) {
  ensureMaterialized(*BaseID, State, ...);
  return;
}
```

For variable-length virtual arrays (`ArrayLengthVal` is non-constant — which we already excluded in Section 2.3.2 by requiring constant length ≤ 32), this check uses the constant length and is therefore exact.

#### 2.8.5 Sub-field accesses / type punning

A field of type `long` may be partially read by an `i32` load at offsets `+0` and `+4`. LLVM IR is free to do this (it's how `VarHandle` partial-word atomics are lowered). Policy for v1: any access whose `(offset, access size)` pair does not exactly correspond to a previously-stored field (same offset, same access size matching the stored type's size) causes materialization. This is conservative but safe — type-punned accesses to virtual objects are rare in Jeandle-targeted Java code, and supporting them would require modelling subword reads as bit-selecting projections on the stored value.

The check is implemented in `getOrCreateFieldIndex`:

```cpp
int VirtualObject::getOrCreateFieldIndex(int64_t Offset, Type *AccessTy) {
  for (size_t i = 0; i < Fields.size(); ++i) {
    if (Fields[i].Offset == Offset) {
      // Existing field at this offset.  Type must match.
      if (Fields[i].LLVMType &&
          DL->getTypeStoreSize(Fields[i].LLVMType) !=
              DL->getTypeStoreSize(AccessTy))
        return -2;  // signal "type mismatch — materialize"
      return i;
    }
    // Detect partial overlap: an access at Offset that overlaps with an
    // existing field's [Off, Off+size) range — also materialize.
    if (Fields[i].LLVMType) {
      uint64_t FieldEnd = Fields[i].Offset +
                          DL->getTypeStoreSize(Fields[i].LLVMType);
      uint64_t AccessEnd = Offset + DL->getTypeStoreSize(AccessTy);
      if (Offset < (int64_t)FieldEnd && Fields[i].Offset < (int64_t)AccessEnd)
        return -2;
    }
  }
  // New field — append.
  FieldDesc F;
  F.Offset = Offset;
  F.LLVMType = AccessTy;
  F.IsReference = AccessTy->isPointerTy() &&
                  cast<PointerType>(AccessTy)->getAddressSpace() == 1;
  Fields.push_back(F);
  return Fields.size() - 1;
}
```

The caller checks for `-2` and materializes the base. Returning a sentinel rather than an `Optional<int>` avoids the cost of constructing an `Optional` on the hot path.

#### 2.8.6 Array element offset computation

For a virtual array with `ElementType T` and length `N`, element `k` lives at byte offset:

```text
arrayOopDesc::base_offset_in_bytes(T) + k * sizeof(T)
```

`arrayOopDesc::base_offset_in_bytes(T)` is a HotSpot constant that depends on `T` (because array header padding depends on alignment of the element type). Jeandle exposes this via the VMConstants struct (see 2.8.10). For a typical `byte[]` it is 16 (on 64-bit with compressed klass), for an `Object[]` it is 16 with compressed oops or 24 without.

A GEP-based array element access in Jeandle IR has the form:

```text
%elt = getelementptr i8, ptr addrspace(1) %arr,
       i64 (base_offset + k * element_size)
```

That is, the interpreter pre-computes the byte offset into a single i64 constant. `resolveFieldOffset` returns this constant via Pattern 1. The element index `k` is then recovered by the array-aware caller as:

```cpp
int64_t Off = *resolveFieldOffset(Ptr);
int64_t Base = VMConstants->arrayBaseOffset(VObj->ElementType);
uint64_t ElemSize = DL->getTypeStoreSize(VObj->ElementType);
if (Off < Base || (Off - Base) % ElemSize != 0) {
  // Either a header access or an unaligned access — handle separately.
  // For unaligned: materialize.
} else {
  int64_t Idx = (Off - Base) / ElemSize;
  if (Idx < 0 || Idx >= VObj->getConstantArrayLength()) {
    // Out of bounds — materialize.
  } else {
    // Use Idx as the index into VObj->Entries.
  }
}
```

For non-constant index (`k` is a loop variable, `Off` therefore non-constant) the array access goes through 2.8.2 and materializes — modulo the future ScalarEvolution work.

#### 2.8.7 arrayOopDesc::length_offset_in_bytes — special slot

Array length is stored at `arrayOopDesc::length_offset_in_bytes()` (a HotSpot constant, typically 12 on 64-bit with compressed klass, 16 without). A load at this offset returns the array length. For a virtual array, this load is virtualizable to the array's `ArrayLengthVal`.

Recognition:

```cpp
// In processLoad on a virtual array base:
if (Off == VMConstants->arrayLengthOffset()) {
  // Replace the load with the tracked length value.
  recordEffect(EffectKind::ReplaceLoad, /*Target=*/LoadInst,
               /*Replacement=*/VObj->ArrayLengthVal);
  return;
}
```

This bypasses the normal field-index path — the array length is a dedicated slot, not a regular field, because (a) it lives in the header (offset < `arrayOopDesc::base_offset_in_bytes`), and (b) it is always immutable after allocation, so it has no entry in `VObj->Fields`.

Stores at the array-length offset are illegal in normal Java code (the length is set by the allocator and never modified). If we see one, materialize defensively — this is likely Unsafe.put or broken IR.

#### 2.8.8 Klass pointer offset

The klass pointer is stored at `oopDesc::klass_offset_in_bytes()` (typically 8 on 64-bit with compressed klass pointer at offset 8, or 0 with non-compressed depending on layout — Jeandle reads this from the VM, see 2.8.10). A load at this offset returns the klass pointer.

For a virtual object, the klass is constant and known: `VObj->Klass` (instance) or `VObj->ArrayKlass` (array). The load is replaced with a constant:

```cpp
if (Off == VMConstants->klassOffset()) {
  Constant *KlassConst = ConstantExpr::getIntToPtr(
      ConstantInt::get(Type::getInt64Ty(Ctx), VObj->Klass),
      LoadInst->getType());
  recordEffect(EffectKind::ReplaceLoad, LoadInst, KlassConst);
  return;
}
```

This is the same logic as Section 2.3.8 (`jeandle.load_klass`). The two paths exist because the abstract interpreter emits *either* a `jeandle.load_klass` call (when the bytecode is a clearly-typed klass query) *or* a plain load at the klass offset (when the bytecode came from Unsafe / reflection). Both must be handled.

Stores to the klass offset: materialize (same rationale as array length).

#### 2.8.9 Mark word

The mark word lives at offset 0 of every Java object (the very first 8 bytes). It encodes lock state, hash code, and GC bits. Reads of the mark word are extremely rare in normal Java code — they appear in `System.identityHashCode` lowering and in lightweight-locking fast paths.

For a virtual object the mark word is conceptually "default-initialised" (no biased lock, no inflated lock, no precomputed hash). A load of the mark word from a virtual object can be replaced with the constant default mark word (a VM constant, see 2.8.10):

```cpp
if (Off == 0) {
  Constant *DefaultMark = ConstantInt::get(
      Type::getInt64Ty(Ctx), VMConstants->defaultMarkWord());
  recordEffect(EffectKind::ReplaceLoad, LoadInst, DefaultMark);
  return;
}
```

However — and this is a subtle point — if `ObjectState::LockCount > 0`, the virtual object is "logically locked" and its mark word should reflect that. In v1 of PEA we side-step this by materializing on mark-word load whenever `LockCount > 0`. The full handling would require modelling the mark-word's lock encoding inside `ObjectState`, which is significant complexity for negligible benefit (mark-word reads of locked virtual objects are vanishingly rare).

Stores to the mark word: also materialize. The `identityHashCode` slow path can compute and install a hash, but on a virtual object we have not chosen an identity yet. The materialization is the moment we commit to one.

#### 2.8.10 Header offset constants — VMConstants design recommendation

The header offsets used in 2.8.7–2.8.9 (`arrayOopDesc::length_offset_in_bytes`, `oopDesc::klass_offset_in_bytes`, default mark word value, plus `arrayOopDesc::base_offset_in_bytes(T)` for each primitive `T` and for reference arrays) are **not constants known to LLVM** — they depend on JVM build flags (`UseCompressedClassPointers`, `UseCompressedOops`) and on the target architecture. Hard-coding them in `jeandle-llvm` would break the moment the JVM is built with a different option set.

**Current state**: `VMCallbacks` (`jeandle-llvm/llvm/include/llvm/IR/Jeandle/VMCallback.h:117`) currently exposes per-callsite query functions (`IsSubtype`, `GetCommonSuperKlass`, `GetFieldType`, `IsInterface`, `IsObjectKlass`, `IsEffectivelyFinal`). It does NOT expose any of the header-offset constants.

**Recommendation**: Add a parallel `VMConstants` struct that is populated at LLVM registration time (i.e., when the HotSpot side initialises the `JeandleCompiler` and creates the LLVM target machine — see `jeandleCompiler.cpp`). The struct holds raw integers, not callbacks, because these values are immutable for the lifetime of the VM:

```cpp
// In jeandle-llvm/llvm/include/llvm/IR/Jeandle/VMConstants.h (new file).
namespace llvm::jeandle {

struct VMConstants {
  // oopDesc layout.
  int64_t MarkWordOffset = 0;
  int64_t KlassOffset = 8;             // 8 with compressed klass, 16 without
  int64_t DefaultMarkWord = 0;         // platform-dependent encoding

  // arrayOopDesc layout.
  int64_t ArrayLengthOffset = 12;      // 12 with compressed klass, 16 without

  // Per-element-type base offsets.  Indexed by element type kind
  // (T_BOOLEAN, T_BYTE, T_CHAR, T_SHORT, T_INT, T_LONG, T_FLOAT, T_DOUBLE,
  // T_OBJECT).  Mirrors HotSpot's arrayOopDesc::base_offset_in_bytes().
  int64_t ArrayBaseOffset[9] = {};

  // Element sizes for primitive arrays.
  uint64_t ElementSize[9] = {};

  // Whether klass / oop pointers are compressed.  Affects type and size
  // of the klass slot.
  bool UseCompressedClassPointers = false;
  bool UseCompressedOops = false;

  // Convenience accessors used by PEA.
  int64_t arrayLengthOffset() const { return ArrayLengthOffset; }
  int64_t klassOffset() const { return KlassOffset; }
  int64_t markWordOffset() const { return MarkWordOffset; }
  int64_t defaultMarkWord() const { return DefaultMarkWord; }
  int64_t arrayBaseOffset(Type *ElementTy) const;
  uint64_t arrayElementSize(Type *ElementTy) const;
};

// Global accessor.  The HotSpot side calls Jeandle::setVMConstants(...) once
// at startup; the analysis pass reads it via Jeandle::getVMConstants().
const VMConstants &getVMConstants();
void setVMConstants(VMConstants);

}  // namespace llvm::jeandle
```

**Registration**: in `jeandleCompiler.cpp` (HotSpot side), inside the initialisation routine that creates the LLVM TargetMachine, populate the struct from HotSpot's own headers:

```cpp
#include "oops/oop.hpp"
#include "oops/arrayOop.hpp"

llvm::jeandle::VMConstants C;
C.MarkWordOffset = oopDesc::mark_offset_in_bytes();
C.KlassOffset = oopDesc::klass_offset_in_bytes();
C.DefaultMarkWord = static_cast<int64_t>(markWord::prototype().value());
C.ArrayLengthOffset = arrayOopDesc::length_offset_in_bytes();
C.ArrayBaseOffset[T_BOOLEAN] = arrayOopDesc::base_offset_in_bytes(T_BOOLEAN);
C.ArrayBaseOffset[T_BYTE]    = arrayOopDesc::base_offset_in_bytes(T_BYTE);
// ... and so on for each BasicType ...
C.ElementSize[T_BOOLEAN] = type2aelembytes(T_BOOLEAN);
// ...
C.UseCompressedClassPointers = UseCompressedClassPointers;
C.UseCompressedOops = UseCompressedOops;

llvm::jeandle::setVMConstants(std::move(C));
```

**Why a struct, not callbacks?** The values never change after VM startup. A struct gives the optimiser an inlinable load instead of an indirect call through a function pointer, which matters on the hot path of `resolveFieldOffset`. The `VMCallbacks` plumbing remains for query-style hooks that genuinely need the VM at query time (`IsSubtype`, etc.).

**Why not embed in `DataLayout`?** LLVM's `DataLayout` is keyed off the target triple and is not extensible per-VM-build-flag. Adding `oopDesc` knowledge to `DataLayout` would be intrusive and would not survive an upstream LLVM rebase. The `VMConstants` struct is a Jeandle-local concept that lives entirely in `jeandle-llvm/llvm/include/llvm/IR/Jeandle/`.

**Compatibility with existing code**: The transform pass (Section 4) emits allocation calls via `jeandle.new_instance` / `jeandle.newarray` whose runtime knows the layout — it doesn't need `VMConstants`. PEA only needs `VMConstants` for the *analysis* path (deciding which loads are header accesses). This is a strict additive change to the LLVM-side public surface; no existing code paths change.

(End of Section 2.8.)

### 2.9 Exception Edge State Splitting

This section formalizes Section 2.3.20 as an algorithmic protocol that interacts with the merge logic of Section 2.4 and the materialization protocol of Section 2.3.18.

**Per-invoke state split.** Every `InvokeInst II` in a block `B` is processed as follows:

```cpp
void processInvoke(InvokeInst *II, PEABlockState &State, BasicBlock *B) {
  bool isAllocation = isJeandleAllocation(II);

  // Step 1: Argument materialization (skipped for allocations).
  if (!isAllocation) {
    for (unsigned i = 0; i < II->arg_size(); ++i) {
      Value *arg = II->getArgOperand(i);
      if (arg->getType() != ptr addrspace(1)) continue;
      auto ObjID = State.resolveVirtualRef(arg);
      if (!ObjID || !State.getObjectState(*ObjID).isVirtual()) continue;
      materialize(*ObjID, B, /*InsertBefore=*/II);
      Effects[B].append(Effect::ReplaceInput{II, i,
                          State.getObjectState(*ObjID).MaterializedPtr});
    }
  }

  // Step 2: Snapshot pre-result state for the unwind edge.
  PEABlockState PreResultState = State.clone();    // COW

  // Step 3: Handle the invoke's result (for allocations only).
  if (isAllocation) {
    // Section 2.3.1 / 2.3.2 has already created the VirtualObject and added
    // it to the alias map. State now reflects the post-allocation normal-edge
    // view. PreResultState is the pre-allocation state (also = unwind-edge view).
  }
  // Non-allocation invokes either return void or return a fresh non-virtual
  // value (not added to alias map). State is unchanged by the invoke result.

  // Step 4: Publish per-successor exit states.
  BasicBlock *N = II->getNormalDest();
  BasicBlock *U = II->getUnwindDest();
  publishPredExitState(N, /*slot=*/predIndex(N, B), State);
  publishPredExitState(U, /*slot=*/predIndex(U, B),
                       isAllocation ? PreResultState : State);
}
```

(In the non-allocation case, `State` and `PreResultState` are equivalent because no allocation happened; we could skip the clone, but clone-on-write makes it free.)

**`publishPredExitState`.** Records `BlockExitStates[(predBB = B, slot = i)] = state` so that when the successor block `N` or `U` is later visited by the RPO walk, its merge processor finds the per-predecessor state.

**Cost analysis.** Naïvely, every invoke triggers two `clone()` calls. With COW (Section 1), each clone is O(1) — only the `arrayRefCount` is bumped. Deep copies happen lazily, only when a successor block mutates the state. In functions with K invokes and M virtual objects, total memory cost is O(K + M) for the per-invoke split state lists, not O(K * M).

**Multi-predecessor unwind block.** A single unwind block may serve multiple invokes — e.g., a `try { a(); b(); c(); } catch (...) { ... }` Java block lowers to three invokes whose unwind destinations are the same landingpad block (Jeandle currently creates a unique unwind dest per invoke, so this case does not arise from the current IR generator, but PEA must handle it for future-proofing and for IR generated by external tools). The unwind block then has K predecessors and acts as a merge point. Section 2.4 merge logic runs there with all K per-invoke `UnwindState`s as inputs.

**Multi-predecessor normal dest.** The normal destination of an invoke can also have multiple predecessors — e.g., if Jeandle emits a branch into the same `normal_dest` block from elsewhere (atypical, but legal). Same handling: Section 2.4 merge logic, with the per-invoke `NormalState` as one of the predecessor states.

**Allocation invokes with the same unwind destination.** Two allocations `inv_A`, `inv_B` whose unwind destinations both flow to a common merge block: the merge sees two `PreResultState`s, one per allocation. The new ObjectID created by `inv_A` is not in `inv_A`'s `PreResultState`. The new ObjectID created by `inv_B` is not in `inv_B`'s `PreResultState`. The intersection has neither object; both ObjectIDs are dropped at the unwind merge (Phase 1b filter). This is correct: on the unwind path, neither allocation completed.

**Allocation invokes whose normal destination merges with a non-invoke predecessor.** Common case: a block does `obj = new X();` and then falls into a merge block also reachable from a path that did not allocate. The merge has two predecessors: post-allocation state (contains new ObjectID) and the other path (does not contain new ObjectID). Per Phase 1b, the new ObjectID is in the union but not the intersection — it is dropped from the merged state. Uses of the allocation result downstream of the merge must either be on a path dominated by the allocation (then PEA's virtual tracking still applies inside that path before the merge) or must be materialized at the merge.

In practice, this means: if the allocation result is live across the merge (its PHI consumer in the merge block is the canonical case), it must be materialized in the predecessor that has it virtual (Phase 1c.v), and the PHI consumer gets a materialized pointer from one input and the corresponding value (null, another materialized ptr) from the other.

**`landingpad` and TLS exception oop.** Each unwind block's first non-PHI instruction is `landingpad i64` followed by a `load` from the TLS exception slot. The landingpad result is i64 (rewritten to token type by RS4GC later), not a Java reference; PEA ignores it. The loaded exception oop is a fresh `ptr addrspace(1)` produced by the load — also not a virtual ref. Both are treated as opaque non-virtual values.

**`resume` terminator.** Treated like `ret`: any virtual ref reaching `resume` (via the resume's argument) must be materialized. Currently Jeandle does not emit `resume`, but PEA implements the rule for completeness.

**`catchpad` / `cleanuppad` / `catchswitch` (Windows EH).** Not emitted by Jeandle (Linux-only). PEA asserts on encountering them.

**`callbr` (asm goto).** Not emitted by Jeandle. If encountered, PEA materializes virtual args before the `callbr` (like a regular call) and treats all successors as inheriting the same post-call state.

**Invoke result attributes (post-split summary).**
- Normal successor: `II` evaluates to a `ptr addrspace(1)` (for allocations and Java methods returning Object). Attributes: `nonnull` (always), `java-klass`/`java-klass-exact` (only for `jeandle.new_instance`/`jeandle.newarray` and for some method returns annotated by upstream profile data). PEA does not add or modify these attributes; it only reads `java-klass-exact` when extracting the klass for `VirtualObject::Klass` (Section 2.3.1).
- Unwind successor: `II`'s result is **not** available (it's an SSA dominance violation to use it on the unwind path); LLVM IR enforces this via the invoke's def being scoped to the normal-dest block in terms of dominance. PEA simply does not add the result to the unwind state's alias map.

**Coalescing materializations.** If multiple invokes in the same block escape the same virtual object, the second escape is a no-op (the object is already Materialized after the first). `ensureMaterialized` returns `false` and records no new effects. The first materialization's allocation invoke + field stores are inserted exactly once, before the first escaping invoke.

**Interaction with merge stabilization.** When a merge of the normal-dest block triggers re-materialization that flows back into the invoke's argument set, the invoke's argument escape decision is independent — it has already been recorded. The merge's effects (per the do-while loop) are localized to the merge block and its predecessors, not the invoke's parent block. So invoke-level escape is monotonic and stable.

---

## 3. Transform Pass: `PartialEscapeTransform`

```cpp
class PartialEscapeTransform : public PassInfoMixin<PartialEscapeTransform> {
public:
  PreservedAnalyses run(Function &F, FunctionAnalysisManager &FAM);
};
```

The transform pass consumes the `PEAResult` produced by `PartialEscapeAnalysis` and rewrites the IR in-place. It is responsible for: (a) materializing partially-escaping objects at the recorded escape points, (b) eliminating fully-virtual allocations and their dependent stores/loads/locks/type-checks, (c) building the PHI nodes that join scalar-replaced field values across control-flow merges, and (d) restoring SSA / dominance / phi-input-order invariants after the rewrite.

The pass follows Graal's two-pass `EffectList.apply(graph, obsoleteNodes, cfgKills)` model verbatim (see `EffectList.java:217-236` and `EffectsClosure.applyEffects` in `EffectsClosure.java:154-232`). Non-CFG-kill effects are applied first; CFG-kill effects second.

### 3.1 Effect Application Order

#### 3.1.1 The two-pass model

Graal's `EffectsClosure.applyEffects()` iterates blocks in RPO twice:

```
for each block in RPO: effects.apply(graph, obsoleteNodes, /*cfgKills=*/false)
for each block in RPO: effects.apply(graph, obsoleteNodes, /*cfgKills=*/true)
```

The reason is documented inline in Graal:

> "Effects that modify the cfg (e.g., removing a branch for an if that got a constant condition) need to be performed after all other effects, because they change phi value indexes."

Translated to LLVM, the same constraint applies: `PHINode::removeIncomingValue`, `BranchInst` replacement, and block deletion all renumber PHI input slots. If we replace a load and then delete an incoming edge in the same walk, the load's replacement value may end up in the wrong PHI slot. Therefore we MUST drain all non-CFG-kill effects across all blocks before touching CFG.

#### 3.1.2 EffectKind enumeration and pass assignment

Every `Effect` recorded by the analysis carries an `EffectKind`. Jeandle's enumeration (pass 1 is the non-CFG-kill pass, pass 2 is the CFG-kill pass):

| EffectKind                       | Description                                                                  | Pass | Graal analog                              |
|----------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------|-------------------------------------------|
| `InsertMaterialization`          | Emit allocation invoke + field stores + lock restoration at an insert point  | 1    | `addFixedNodeBefore` (CommitAllocationNode)|
| `CreatePHI`                      | Insert a new `PHINode` at the start of a merge or loop-exit block            | 1    | `addFloatingNode` (ValuePhiNode)          |
| `SetPHIIncoming`                 | Wire up `PHINode::addIncoming(value, pred)` for one predecessor              | 1    | `initializePhiInput`                      |
| `ReplaceUsesWith`                | `oldVal->replaceAllUsesWith(newVal)` (load, JavaOp, virtual ref → scalar)    | 1    | `replaceAtUsages`                         |
| `ReplaceOperand`                 | `inst->setOperand(idx, newVal)` (single-use refinement)                      | 1    | `replaceFirstInput`                       |
| `AddMetadata`                    | Attach `!java-klass`, `!java-klass-exact`, `!nonnull`, `!range`, `!noundef`  | 1    | (n/a — stamps are inputs in Graal)        |
| `RecordAliasMapping`             | Append to the alias map for downstream passes (documentation only)           | 1    | (n/a — alias map is implicit)             |
| `EraseInstruction`               | `inst->eraseFromParent()` for non-terminator, non-PHI instructions           | 2    | `deleteNode` (non-CFG variant)            |
| `EraseAllocationInvoke`          | Replace `InvokeInst` allocation with unconditional branch to normal dest     | 2    | `deleteNode` (CFG-kill variant)           |
| `SimplifyConditionalBranch`      | `BranchInst` with constant condition → unconditional branch                  | 2    | `killIfBranch`                            |
| `RemoveUnreachableBlock`         | `DeleteDeadBlock(BB, &DTU)` — runs after structural changes settle           | 2    | implicit (DCE phase)                      |

Effects 1–7 add or rewrite uses without removing CFG edges. Effects 8–11 either delete instructions whose uses no longer exist (8 — requires pass-1 RAUW first) or actually rip predecessor edges out of PHIs (9–11 — would corrupt phi-index ordering if interleaved).

#### 3.1.3 Block iteration order

We use `ReversePostOrderTraversal<Function*> RPOT(&F)` over the analysis function. The pass walks `RPOT` once per phase:

```cpp
ReversePostOrderTraversal<Function*> RPOT(&F);
for (BasicBlock *BB : RPOT)
  applyEffects(PEAResult.BlockEffects[BB], /*cfgKills=*/false);
for (BasicBlock *BB : RPOT)
  applyEffects(PEAResult.BlockEffects[BB], /*cfgKills=*/true);
```

Within a block, effects are stored in a `SmallVector<Effect, 16>` keyed by insertion order (the `sequence number`). This ensures that, e.g., the materialization of object `B` (which is a field of `A`) is emitted before the materialization of `A`, so that `A`'s field-store can reference `B`'s materialized pointer. The analysis is responsible for adding effects in dependency-correct order (Section 2 details how).

#### 3.1.4 Why this matches Graal exactly

Graal stores effects per HIR block (`BlockMap<GraphEffectList> blockEffects` in `EffectsClosure.java:106`), iterates the CFG via `ReentrantBlockIterator`, and calls `effects.apply(graph, obsoleteNodes, false)` followed by `effects.apply(graph, obsoleteNodes, true)` across all collected blocks. Our `BlockMap` is a `DenseMap<BasicBlock*, SmallVector<Effect>>` and the iteration order is RPO via `ReversePostOrderTraversal`, which is the natural LLVM equivalent of Graal's structured-CFG block order.

### 3.2 Materialization Code Generation

Materialization synthesizes an `invoke @jeandle.new_instance` (or `@jeandle.newarray`), a chain of field-store instructions, and (if `LockCount > 0`) one or more `call @jeandle.monitorenter_with_*` calls. Because `invoke` has two CFG successors (normal + unwind), materialization is itself a *CFG-shaping* event that must be performed carefully relative to the block it lands in.

#### 3.2.1 Insertion-point computation

The analysis records an `InsertBefore` instruction with each `InsertMaterialization` effect (Section 2.4). Resolution at transform time:

```
if Effect.InsertBefore is the first instruction of its block:
    InsertPoint = block.begin()
else if Effect.InsertBefore is the block's terminator AND terminator is not an invoke:
    InsertPoint = terminator iterator (the new invoke replaces it as the new terminator
                                       — see 3.2.3 below for split logic)
else:
    InsertPoint = Effect.InsertBefore->getIterator()
```

#### 3.2.2 Field stores and lock calls (the "easy" parts)

After the materialization invoke has been inserted at `InsertPoint` (see 3.2.3), the analysis-tracked field values are stored using `GEP + store` mirroring the original abstract-interpreter pattern (`jeandleAbstractInterpreter::do_field_access`):

```cpp
IRBuilder<> B(invokeNormalDest, invokeNormalDest->begin());
B.SetCurrentDebugLocation(origAllocDebugLoc);
for (const FieldValue &FV : Effect.FieldValues) {
  if (FV.Kind == FieldValue::Unknown) continue;        // default zero/null suffices
  Value *Slot = B.CreateInBoundsGEP(Type::getInt8Ty(C), MaterializedPtr,
                                    B.getInt64(FV.Offset));
  Value *V = (FV.Kind == FieldValue::Scalar)         ? FV.Scalar
           : (FV.Kind == FieldValue::MaterializedRef) ? FV.MaterializedPtr
           /* VirtualRef must have been resolved already by prerequisite mat */;
  StoreInst *S = B.CreateAlignedStore(V, Slot, Align(FV.Alignment));
  if (FV.WasAtomic) S->setAtomic(AtomicOrdering::Unordered);
}
for (LockRecord &L : Effect.Locks) {     // innermost lock first → last enter
  CallInst *Enter = B.CreateCall(Module->getFunction(L.MonitorEnterFnName),
                                 {MaterializedPtr, L.LockSlot});
  Enter->setCallingConv(CallingConv::Hotspot_JIT);
  // The i1 result is irrelevant for virtual objects (always true). We discard it
  // by NOT branching on it — the original branch path was eliminated when the
  // lock was virtualized (Section 3.6).
}
```

`L.MonitorEnterFnName` matches the locking-mode-specific JavaOp emitted by the abstract interpreter (`jeandle.monitorenter_with_monitor_lock`, `..._thin_lock`, or `..._lightweight_lock` — see `jeandleAbstractInterpreter.cpp:3115-3120`). `L.LockSlot` is a stack-slot pointer that was reserved by the original abstract interpreter (`lock.lock()`).

#### 3.2.3 Block splitting when InsertBefore is mid-block

The allocation must be an `InvokeInst` because the runtime allocator may throw `OutOfMemoryError`. An invoke has two successors, so the block that contains the invoke must be terminated by that invoke. If `InsertPoint` is mid-block, we have to split:

```cpp
BasicBlock *Origin = InsertPoint->getParent();
// 1. Split AT the insertion point. SplitBlock leaves Origin terminated by an
//    unconditional br to the new block.
BasicBlock *NormalDest =
    SplitBlock(Origin, &*InsertPoint, /*DTU=*/&DTU, /*LI=*/nullptr,
               /*MSSAU=*/nullptr, /*BBName=*/"mat.cont");
// 2. Build (or reuse) an unwind landingpad block. We can reuse the unwind
//    destination of any *enclosing* InvokeInst whose unwind handler covers
//    this point. Otherwise we synthesize one identical to what the abstract
//    interpreter would have built (see jeandleAbstractInterpreter.cpp:2776).
BasicBlock *UnwindDest = findOrSynthesizeUnwindDest(Origin, &F);
// 3. Remove the unconditional br that SplitBlock placed at the end of Origin.
Origin->getTerminator()->eraseFromParent();
// 4. Emit the materialization invoke as the new terminator of Origin.
IRBuilder<> B(Origin);
B.SetCurrentDebugLocation(Effect.DebugLoc);
InvokeInst *MatInvoke = B.CreateInvoke(
    AllocFn,                                              // jeandle.new_instance or newarray
    /*NormalDest=*/NormalDest,
    /*UnwindDest=*/UnwindDest,
    /*Args=*/{KlassPtr, SizeOrLength},
    /*OperandBundles=*/{ Effect.DeoptBundle });           // see 3.2.5
MatInvoke->setCallingConv(CallingConv::Hotspot_JIT);
// 5. Copy ret attributes (java-klass / java-klass-exact) from the original
//    allocation invoke. The original is recorded in Effect.OriginalAlloc.
copyAllocationReturnAttributes(MatInvoke, Effect.OriginalAlloc);
// 6. Field-store + lock initialization happens in NormalDest, before the
//    original instruction that was at InsertPoint (now the first non-PHI
//    instruction of NormalDest).
```

Notes:

- `findOrSynthesizeUnwindDest` walks up enclosing `InvokeInst`s in the original IR. The abstract interpreter ensures every BCI has a known unwind path (see `dispatch_exception_for_invoke`, `jeandleAbstractInterpreter.cpp:2776`). If no enclosing handler exists (e.g., the function has no exception handlers covering this point), we synthesize a landingpad+resume block matching the abstract interpreter's pattern: `i64`-typed `landingpad` marked `cleanup`, followed by a resume of the read-from-TLS exception oop. Reuse is preferred to keep the CFG simple.
- Using `DomTreeUpdater` (`DTU`) in `Lazy` mode keeps the dominator tree consistent: every `splitBlock` + new edge + erased terminator must be reported.

If the original allocation that PEA *eliminated* had its own unwind destination, that block becomes unreferenced only if PEA also removed all other invokes pointing into it. The downstream `removeUnreachableBlocks` (Section 3.7) handles cleanup; PEA does not delete the old unwind dest manually.

#### 3.2.4 Grouped (recursive) materializations at one point

When multiple virtual objects materialize at the same `InsertBefore` (e.g., outer object `A` is forced to materialize and it has a virtual-ref field referencing `B`), the analysis orders the effects so `B` is materialized first (deepest first). Each materialization splits the block, so the chain looks like:

```
Origin → invoke@mat(B) → matB.cont (stores into B) → invoke@mat(A) → matA.cont (stores into A, using %matB) → original_InsertBefore
```

Each prior `matX.cont` becomes the parent block for the next split. This matches Graal's `CommitAllocationNode` lowering, where the lowering emits one allocation per virtual object in dependency order, sharing a single TLAB bump path. In Jeandle we do not share TLAB paths (each invoke is its own slow-path-capable call), but the topological emission order is identical.

#### 3.2.5 Operand bundles, calling convention, and attributes

Every JavaOp call in Jeandle is emitted with `CallingConv::Hotspot_JIT` and the original allocation carries an `OperandBundleDef("deopt", ...)` (see `jeandleAbstractInterpreter.cpp:2294-2297`, `create_current_deopt_bundle`). Because PEA does not synthesize deopt info, the materialization invoke MUST inherit the deopt bundle of *the instruction at the escape point* — that is, the bundle that would have been on the original allocation if it had occurred there. The analysis captures this bundle as `Effect.DeoptBundle`:

1. If the escape point is itself an `InvokeInst` or `CallInst` with a `"deopt"` bundle, copy its bundle verbatim.
2. Otherwise (e.g., escape via a `ret`), copy the bundle of the original allocation `InvokeInst` (`Effect.OriginalAlloc`).

Calling convention: `setCallingConv(CallingConv::Hotspot_JIT)`.

Function attributes on the result (e.g., `nonnull`, `noundef`, `java-klass=N`, `java-klass-exact`) are copied from the original allocation via `copyAllocationReturnAttributes`. These attributes are critical for downstream passes (`TypeCheckElimination` reads `java-klass-exact`).

#### 3.2.6 DebugLoc preservation

Every newly emitted instruction (`invoke`, GEPs, stores, lock calls) gets `setDebugLoc(Effect.DebugLoc)`. The `DebugLoc` source is:

1. If the original allocation had a non-null `DebugLoc`, use it (this preserves the line/BCI of the `new` bytecode).
2. Otherwise, fall back to the `InsertBefore`'s `DebugLoc`.

This preserves source-line attribution for sampling profilers and inline-info passes that run later.

#### 3.2.7 Metadata propagation

| Metadata kind          | Source                                          | Action on materialized invoke / stores             |
|------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|
| `!java-klass`          | original allocation's return-attr `java-klass`  | Set as return-attr on the materialization invoke   |
| `!java-klass-exact`    | original allocation's return-attr               | Set as return-attr                                  |
| `!nonnull`             | implicit (allocation can't return null on normal path) | Set on materialized invoke return            |
| `!noundef`             | implicit                                        | Set on materialized invoke return                  |
| `!range`               | none for objects; for arraylength replacement, copy from analysis-tracked length | Attach to the constant replacement |
| `!tbaa`/`!alias.scope` | the original load/store being eliminated        | Discard — value flows through SSA, not memory      |
| `!dbg`                 | see 3.2.6                                       | Copied                                              |
| operand bundle `"deopt"`| see 3.2.5                                      | Copied                                              |

### 3.3 PHI Node Creation

For each `Effect::CreatePHI` followed by N `Effect::SetPHIIncoming` effects:

```cpp
IRBuilder<> B(MergeBB, MergeBB->getFirstInsertionPt());
B.SetCurrentDebugLocation(Effect.DebugLoc);
PHINode *Phi = B.CreatePHI(Effect.Type, Effect.NumIncoming, Effect.Name);
// Apply !java-klass / !nonnull metadata if the PHI joins typed values.
if (Effect.JavaKlass) Phi->setMetadata("java-klass", Effect.JavaKlass);
PHIMap[Effect.PhiId] = Phi;
```

PHI incoming-value wiring (`SetPHIIncoming`):

```cpp
PHINode *Phi = PHIMap[Effect.PhiId];
// LLVM requires that the i-th block returned by Phi->blocks() corresponds to
// the i-th value returned by Phi->incoming_values(). Using addIncoming(value, block)
// preserves the invariant: each call appends a (value, block) pair.
Phi->addIncoming(Effect.Value, Effect.PredBlock);
```

#### 3.3.1 Predecessor order matching

LLVM's `PHINode` does not require that the order of `addIncoming` calls match the order of CFG predecessors as returned by `predecessors(BB)`. What it requires is that the *pair* (value, predBlock) is consistent: `Phi->getIncomingValueForBlock(Pred)` must return the value that should flow when control comes from `Pred`. We achieve this by always emitting `SetPHIIncoming` effects keyed on the predecessor block, never on an index. This is more robust than Graal's `initializePhiInput(node, index, value)` because index-based wiring can be invalidated by CFG-kill effects.

If, by the time pass 1 runs for the PHI's block, the predecessor in the effect no longer terminates into MergeBB (because that predecessor was rerouted by a prior block-split during materialization — see 3.2.3), we use the *new* terminating block of that split chain. The materialization code maintains a side-table `BlockReplaced(OldPred → NewPred)` consulted during pass-1 PHI wiring.

#### 3.3.2 Loop exit PHIs (proxy PHIs)

For a virtual object whose state needs to leave a loop scope (e.g., a tracked field value), the analysis records a `CreatePHI` effect in the unique loop-exit block (analogous to Graal's `ProxyNode`). The incoming value is:

- From the loop body's exit edge: the in-loop scalar value (or materialized pointer, if the object was materialized in the loop).
- From the loop preheader: never reachable for an exit block (the exit block has a single predecessor — the loop). Therefore `addIncoming` is called exactly once per exit block.

If LLVM has not yet canonicalized loops to LCSSA form when PEA runs, exit blocks may have multiple predecessors from different loop exits. In that case we add one incoming for each loop exit edge; the analysis tracks the value per-edge.

### 3.4 Load / Call Replacement

```cpp
for (auto [Load, Replacement] : Result.LoadReplacements) {
  // Copy useful metadata from the load that would not naturally transfer.
  if (Replacement->getType()->isPointerTy()) {
    if (MDNode *JK = Load->getMetadata("java-klass"))
      copyKlassToReplacementUser(Load, Replacement, JK);
  }
  Load->replaceAllUsesWith(Replacement);
  // Defer erase to pass 2 via EraseInstruction effect.
}
for (auto [Call, Replacement] : Result.CallReplacements) {
  if (!Call->getType()->isVoidTy())
    Call->replaceAllUsesWith(Replacement);
  // Defer erase to pass 2.
}
```

#### 3.4.1 Metadata-copy details

- `!java-klass` on a load is propagated to the constant replacement only when the replacement is itself an oop value (`ptr addrspace(1)`). For scalar fields (`i32`, `i64`, `float`, etc.) no klass metadata is meaningful.
- `!nonnull` on a load is propagated when the analysis can prove the field cannot be null at the read point (e.g., the corresponding store wrote a `nonnull` value or an allocated object).
- `!range` on `jeandle.arraylength` replacement: `!range !{i32 N, i32 N+1}` where `N` is the tracked length. Since we cannot attach `!range` to a `Constant`, we attach a `freeze` + `add 0` wrapper only if downstream passes require it; in practice constant folding makes this unnecessary.
- `!noundef` is preserved as-is on the original load's *users* since RAUW transfers the constant directly.

#### 3.4.2 Deterministic iteration

`Result.LoadReplacements` is a `MapVector<Instruction*, Value*>` so iteration order matches insertion order (the analysis insertion order is RPO). This avoids non-determinism in the resulting IR.

#### 3.4.3 Special-case: checkcast / instanceof folded to constant

`jeandle.checkcast`, `jeandle.check_instanceof` return `i1`; `jeandle.instanceof` returns `i32`. When replaced with `i1 true`/`i1 false`/`i32 0`/`i32 1`, the immediate user is typically a conditional branch. Pass 1 only RAUWs the value; pass 2's `SimplifyConditionalBranch` effect — emitted by the analysis when it folded the check — rewrites the branch.

### 3.5 Allocation Elimination

For a fully-virtual object whose allocation is an `InvokeInst` (the normal case for `jeandle.new_instance` / `jeandle.newarray` since they can throw `OutOfMemoryError`):

```cpp
// Pass 2 effect: EraseAllocationInvoke
InvokeInst *Alloc = Effect.OriginalAlloc;
BasicBlock *Normal = Alloc->getNormalDest();
BasicBlock *Unwind = Alloc->getUnwindDest();
// Step 1: replace remaining uses (should be none after pass 1, but defensively
// poison out any leftover uses such as those in unreachable blocks).
if (!Alloc->use_empty())
  Alloc->replaceAllUsesWith(PoisonValue::get(Alloc->getType()));
// Step 2: replace the invoke with an unconditional branch to the normal dest.
BranchInst::Create(Normal, Alloc->getParent());
// Step 3: remove the unwind edge — this may render the landingpad block
// dead. We do NOT delete it here; pass 2's RemoveUnreachableBlock effect
// (after CFG settles) handles it.
Alloc->eraseFromParent();
// Step 4: notify DTU.
DTU.applyUpdates({{DominatorTree::Delete, Alloc->getParent(), Unwind}});
```

For a non-invoke allocation (`CallInst`, possible only for the slow-path runtime call from `JeandleRuntimeRoutine::new_instance_callee`; uncommon for virtualizable objects since slow-path allocations are flagged escape in analysis):

```cpp
Alloc->replaceAllUsesWith(PoisonValue::get(Alloc->getType()));
Alloc->eraseFromParent();
```

#### 3.5.1 Use-list and RAUW ordering

CRITICAL: by the time pass 2 runs, the analysis-recorded replacements (loads, JavaOp calls, identity comparisons, lock-result branches) MUST have already been RAUW'd in pass 1. Any remaining uses of the virtual allocation are either bugs or pre-existing poison uses in unreachable code. Step 1's defensive RAUW-to-poison protects against the latter.

We never erase the allocation while uses remain — `eraseFromParent` asserts an empty use-list and would crash. The two-pass model guarantees this ordering.

### 3.6 Lock Elimination

For an eliminated `jeandle.monitorenter_with_*` or `jeandle.monitorexit_with_*`:

```cpp
// Pass 1: RAUW the i1 result with `true`. Virtual objects never contend.
CallInst *Lock = Effect.Lock;
Lock->replaceAllUsesWith(ConstantInt::getTrue(Type::getInt1Ty(C)));
// Pass 2 (EraseInstruction): erase the call.
Lock->eraseFromParent();
// Pass 2 (SimplifyConditionalBranch): the original `br i1 %lock, %entered, %slow`
// becomes `br label %entered` because i1 simplifies to constant true. The slow
// path becomes unreachable.
```

The CFG produced by the abstract interpreter is precisely (see `shared_lock` at `jeandleAbstractInterpreter.cpp:3100-3134` and `shared_unlock` at 3136-3168):

```
%lock.ok = call i1 @jeandle.monitorenter_with_thin_lock(%obj, %slot)
br i1 %lock.ok, label %monitor_entered, label %monitorenter_slow_path
```

After PEA elimination, the i1 is `true`, the branch simplifies to `br label %monitor_entered`, and the slow path becomes unreachable. `RemoveUnreachableBlock` cleans it up.

For balanced enter/exit on the same virtual object (LockCount returns to 0 by function exit), both calls are eliminated. If the enter is virtualized but a later use forces materialization, the analysis emits a *materialization* effect that includes the lock restoration (see 3.2.2), so the lock is reinstated at the materialization point. The original lock site is still erased.

### 3.7 Dead Code Cleanup

After both passes complete:

```cpp
// 1. Delete blocks made unreachable by EraseAllocationInvoke /
//    SimplifyConditionalBranch.
EliminateUnreachableBlocks(F);                          // llvm/Transforms/Utils/Local.h
// 2. Merge straight-line single-predecessor / single-successor blocks and
//    fold trivially constant branches.
for (BasicBlock &BB : llvm::make_early_inc_range(F))
  simplifyCFG(&BB, TTI, &DTU);                          // llvm/Transforms/Utils/SimplifyCFG.h
// 3. Fold introduced constants. Cheap and avoids leaving %x = add i32 0, %y
//    style cruft.
for (Instruction &I : llvm::make_early_inc_range(instructions(F))) {
  if (Value *V = simplifyInstruction(&I, F.getDataLayout())) {
    I.replaceAllUsesWith(V);
    I.eraseFromParent();
  }
}
// 4. Drop PHIs that became single-incoming or dead.
for (BasicBlock &BB : F)
  for (PHINode &PHI : llvm::make_early_inc_range(BB.phis()))
    if (PHI.getNumIncomingValues() == 1) {
      PHI.replaceAllUsesWith(PHI.getIncomingValue(0));
      PHI.eraseFromParent();
    } else if (PHI.use_empty() && isInstructionTriviallyDead(&PHI)) {
      PHI.eraseFromParent();
    }
// 5. Debug-build sanity check.
#ifndef NDEBUG
  assert(!verifyFunction(F, &errs()) && "PEA produced malformed IR");
#endif
```

We deliberately do NOT run `instcombine` here — it is much heavier than needed, and Jeandle's pipeline runs the full default pipeline downstream.

### 3.8 Invariants and Verification

After the transform pass completes, the following invariants MUST hold (debug-build verifier walks the function and asserts each):

1. **All `NeverEscapes`-classified allocations are erased.** Walk all `InvokeInst`s in the function; for each that calls `jeandle.new_instance` or `jeandle.newarray`, look up its `AllocationID` in `PEAResult.Classifications`. Any allocation classified `NeverEscapes` must be absent from the post-transform IR.
2. **All `PartiallyEscapes` allocations have materialization at their escape points.** For each escape point recorded in `Result.EscapePoints`, the dominator-tree path from function entry to that escape point must contain a materialization invoke (`InvokeInst` calling `jeandle.new_instance`/`newarray`) whose result reaches the escape point through SSA or via the post-transform alias map.
3. **No virtual-ref sentinel survives in the IR.** During analysis we may use a sentinel `Value *` (e.g., a tagged `UndefValue`) to denote "virtual object identifier #K." After transform, none of these sentinel values may remain. The verifier enumerates all instruction operands and asserts none match.
4. **SSA dominance holds.** `verifyFunction(F)` includes the standard SSA verifier (every use is dominated by its def). The PEA verifier additionally checks that every materialized pointer dominates every use that was rewired to it by RAUW.
5. **PHI input correctness.** For every `PHINode P` in the function, `P->getNumIncomingValues() == pred_size(P->getParent())`, and for each predecessor `Q`, `P->getIncomingValueForBlock(Q)` is dominated by `Q`'s terminator.
6. **Metadata well-formedness.** `!java-klass` nodes are `{i64 N}` with `N > 0`. `!nonnull` is empty. `!range` is `{T A, T B}` with `A < B`.
7. **Operand bundles preserved.** Every materialization invoke has a `"deopt"` operand bundle.
8. **Calling convention preserved.** Every materialization invoke has `getCallingConv() == CallingConv::Hotspot_JIT`.

A `dumpPEAStats(PEAResult &)` helper prints the per-allocation classification and the count of eliminated stores/loads/locks for debugging via `-debug-only=partial-escape-transform`.

### 3.9 Interaction with Downstream Passes

PEA runs immediately before `JavaOperationLower(0)` (Section 5.2). The output of PEA is therefore handed to:

1. **`JavaOperationLower(0)`** — inlines phase-0 JavaOps (`monitorenter_with_*`, `monitorexit_with_*`, `checkcast`, `check_instanceof`, `instanceof`, `arraylength`, `load_klass`, `array_store_check`, `check_if_value_based`, `current_thread`, `safepoint_poll`, etc.). Effects of PEA on this pass:
   - Eliminated lock JavaOps are simply gone — nothing to inline.
   - Eliminated checkcast/instanceof JavaOps are gone — type checks already folded.
   - Materialized allocation invokes (`jeandle.new_instance`/`newarray`) are *not* phase-0 JavaOps; they are phase-1 (lower-phase=1), so they pass through.
2. **`InstSimplifyPass`** — folds the constants we introduced (`true`/`false` from lock/check elimination; null from default-zero fields).
3. **`TypeCheckElimination`** — sees a cleaner IR: the materialized allocations carry `java-klass`/`java-klass-exact` attributes, enabling further folding of any remaining type checks on related values.
4. **`buildPerModuleDefaultPipeline(level)`** — standard LLVM O0/O1/O2/O3 optimization. PEA-introduced PHIs and constants are subject to GVN, SCCP, etc.
5. **`InsertGCBarriers`** — inserts pre/post barriers for surviving stores. PEA eliminated the stores to virtual fields, so no barriers are emitted for them. This is correct *because* PEA runs before `InsertGCBarriers`; were PEA moved later, it would need to also remove the barrier `call`s that wrap any eliminated store. Document this as a hard placement constraint in `Pipeline.cpp`.
6. **`JavaOperationLower(1)`** — inlines phase-1 JavaOps including `jeandle.new_instance`/`newarray`. Materialized allocations are inlined here.
7. **`TLSPointerRewrite`** / **`RewriteStatepointsForGC`** — unaffected by PEA.

PEA preserves the analyses listed in `PreservedAnalyses` by returning `PreservedAnalyses::none()` only when it actually changed the IR (use a `Changed` flag). Otherwise it returns `PreservedAnalyses::all()`.

---

## 4. JavaOp and IR Pattern Handling Summary

The table below covers every IR pattern PEA recognizes. The "Effect Kinds Used" column lists the `EffectKind`s emitted by the analysis when the pattern triggers an optimization. The "Escape Outcome" column states the EscapeKind classification implication for the involved virtual object.

### 4.1 Allocation and field access

| Pattern | Detection Rule | Analysis Action | Transform Action | Effect Kinds Used | Escape Outcome |
|---------|---------------|-----------------|------------------|-------------------|----------------|
| `invoke @jeandle.new_instance(klass, size) ["deopt"()]` | called fn name == `jeandle.new_instance` | Create VirtualObject, ObjectState (all fields Unknown), alias `Alloc → V_k` | If NeverEscapes: erase; if PartiallyEscapes: emit at escape point | `RecordAliasMapping`; later `InsertMaterialization` or `EraseAllocationInvoke` | NeverEscapes / PartiallyEscapes |
